That translation — "GapSVP$_\gamma$ is hard, therefore concretely, at $n=4096$ and this $q$, the best attack costs $2^{128}$ operations" — is exactly the empirical, cryptanalysis-driven parameter-selection exercise covered in Chapter 14, and it is separate from (though motivated by) the asymptotic reduction here.

4.3 An Encryption Scheme from LWE

LWE's cryptographic payoff is that it yields, almost immediately, a public-key encryption scheme with a strikingly simple structure — this is essentially Regev's original scheme, and it is the direct ancestor of every LWE/RLWE-based FHE construction in this book.

Definition 4.3 (Regev encryption, single bit — symmetric-key form)

Parameters: dimension $n$, modulus $q$, error distribution $\chi$.

Note that what follows is the secret-key version: encryption below computes $\langle a,s\rangle$ and therefore needs $s$. Regev's original 2005 scheme is public-key; the public key is a set of $m$ LWE samples $(a_i, b_i = \langle a_i,s\rangle + e_i)$, and encryption adds $m\lfloor q/2\rfloor$ to the sum of a random subset of them, which works because a subset-sum of LWE samples is itself an LWE sample with somewhat larger noise. The symmetric version is presented here because it isolates the LWE structure with less bookkeeping; every later chapter's RLWE scheme follows the same pattern.

KeyGen: sample $s \leftarrow \mathbb{Z}_q^n$ uniformly. This is the secret key.

Encrypt(bit $m \in \{0,1\}$): sample $a \leftarrow \mathbb{Z}_q^n$ uniformly, $e \leftarrow \chi$, and output the ciphertext $$ (a,\; b) = \Big(a,\; \langle a, s\rangle + e + m\left\lfloor \tfrac{q}{2}\right\rfloor \bmod q\Big). $$

Decrypt$(a,b)$: compute $v = b - \langle a, s \rangle \bmod q$, interpreted as an integer in $(-q/2, q/2]$; output $0$ if $|v| \le q/4$ and $1$ otherwise (equivalently, round $\dfrac{2}{q}\big(b - \langle a,s\rangle \bmod q\big)$ to the nearest integer mod 2).

The comparison must be made this way — by magnitude against $q/4$, or equivalently by distance measured on the circle $\mathbb{Z}_q$. Phrasing it as "closer to $0$ than to $\lfloor q/2\rfloor$" while comparing signed integers on the line is a real and easy-to-make bug: with $q=97$, $m=1$ and $e=2$, the value $\lfloor q/2\rfloor + e = 50$ has signed representative $-47$, which is nearer to $0$ than to $48$ on the number line and would decrypt to $0$, even though $|-47| > q/4$ correctly gives $1$.

Correctness. $b - \langle a, s\rangle = e + m\lfloor q/2\rfloor \pmod q$. If $m=0$ this is just $e$, small; if $m=1$ it is $\lfloor q/2\rfloor + e$, close to the "opposite pole" of $\mathbb{Z}_q$. Decryption succeeds exactly when $|e| < q/4$ — as long as the noise magnitude does not exceed a quarter of the modulus, the rounding step correctly identifies which pole the value is closer to.

Theorem 4.3 (Correctness condition)

Decrypt$($Encrypt$(m)) = m$ except with probability $\Pr_{e\sim\chi}[|e| \ge q/4]$. By the Gaussian tail bound (Theorem 1.4), if $\chi = D_{\mathbb{Z},\sigma}$ with $\sigma \ll q$, this failure probability is negligible whenever $q/(4\sigma)$ is superlogarithmic in the security parameter — concretely, choosing $q \ge 8\sigma \cdot \sqrt{2\lambda\ln 2}$ or so gives failure probability below $2^{-\lambda}$.

Semantic security. The ciphertext $(a,b)$ is, under Decision-LWE, computationally indistinguishable from a uniformly random pair in $\mathbb{Z}_q^n\times\mathbb{Z}_q$ — the entire ciphertext looks like noise to anyone without $s$. Since a uniform pair carries (statistically) zero information about $m$, an adversary without the secret key cannot do better than guessing, which is exactly the IND-CPA security definition. Formally, the reduction is immediate from Definition 4.2: a distinguisher between "encryption of $0$" and "encryption of $1$" for random $m$ is (after noting that shifting a uniform distribution by a fixed constant $\lfloor q/2 \rfloor$ keeps it uniform) precisely a Decision-LWE distinguisher.

4.4 Parameter Relationships

Three quantities govern every LWE instantiation, and they trade off against each other in a specific, quantifiable way.

ParameterRoleEffect of increasing it
$n$ (dimension)Lattice rank / secret lengthHarder lattice problem (larger search space), more security; larger keys/ciphertexts, slower
$q$ (modulus)Size of the ambient ring $\mathbb{Z}_q$Larger $q$ (fixed $n,\sigma$) makes the lattice "less dense" relative to the noise — weakens security at fixed $n$; but gives more room for noise growth (correctness) in homomorphic schemes
$\sigma$ (noise width)Standard deviation of $\chi$Larger $\sigma$ strengthens security (harder to distinguish from uniform / harder BDD instance) but eats correctness budget faster
Definition 4.4 (Error rate $\alpha$)

The error rate is $\alpha = \sigma / q$, the noise width expressed as a fraction of the modulus. It is $\alpha$, not $\sigma$ alone, that appears in Regev's reduction (Theorem 4.2): the reduction targets GapSVP$_\gamma$ with $\gamma = \tilde O(n/\alpha)$. Smaller $\alpha$ (relatively tighter noise) means the reduction is only to a larger (easier) $\gamma$ lattice problem — weaker security evidence for fixed $n$ — which is why, informally, "more relative noise is more secure" is the correct qualitative intuition, subject to the correctness constraint of Theorem 4.3 bounding how large $\sigma$ (hence $\alpha q$) can be while decryption still works.

The practical design loop, used throughout this book's later chapters on parameter selection, is: fix a target security level $\lambda$ (e.g. $128$ bits); use a cost model (BKZ block-size estimate, Chapter 3, via the Lattice Estimator) to determine the minimum $n$ for which the best known attack on LWE$_{n,q,\sigma}$ costs at least $2^{\lambda}$; separately, ensure $q$ is large enough relative to $\sigma$ and the number of homomorphic operations planned (which multiplies effective noise, Chapter 2's expansion factor) that decryption correctness holds with overwhelming probability. These two constraints — security wants $q$ small relative to $n$ (or $\sigma$ large relative to $q$), correctness wants $q$ large relative to accumulated noise — are in direct tension, and the entire art of "leveled" FHE parameter selection (Chapters 7–10, 14) is managing that tension across many sequential multiplications.

Example 4.1 (Toy-scale numbers, for intuition only — not secure)

Take $n = 4$, $q = 97$, $\sigma \approx 3$ (so $\alpha = \sigma/q \approx 0.031$). Secret $s = (2, 51, 76, 13)$. Sample $a = (34, 8, 91, 5)$, $e = 2$. Then $\langle a,s\rangle = 34\cdot2 + 8\cdot51 + 91\cdot76 + 5\cdot13 = 68+408+6916+65 = 7457 \equiv 7457 \bmod 97 = 7457 - 76\cdot97 = 7457-7372=85$. So $b = 85 + 2 = 87 \bmod 97$. Encrypting $m=1$: $b' = 87 + \lfloor 97/2\rfloor = 87+48=135\equiv 38 \bmod 97$. Decrypting: $38 - 85 = -47 \equiv 50 \pmod {97}$, which as a signed residue is $50$ — closer to $48\;(\approx q/2)$ than to $0$, so decryption correctly outputs $1$. At real parameters ($n\sim 700$–$1024$ for plain LWE-based encryption, $\sigma$ chosen per the Lattice Estimator), the same arithmetic holds with astronomically larger numbers and negligible failure probability.

4.5 Variants and a Concrete Estimation Walkthrough

Two extensions of the basic LWE framework recur throughout the rest of the book and are worth flagging now, before they reappear as load-bearing tools rather than curiosities.

Definition 4.5 (Learning With Rounding, LWR)

Fix moduli $q > p$. The LWR sample is $(a, b) = \big(a,\; \lfloor (p/q)\cdot\langle a,s\rangle \rceil \bmod p\big)$ — deterministic rounding down to a smaller modulus $p$ replaces the explicit random error term $e$ entirely. The rounding operation itself introduces a small, deterministic "error," and for suitable $p \ll q$, LWR is (under mild conditions, and for bounded numbers of samples) as hard as LWE. LWR trades explicit randomness (no error sampler needed at encryption time — a meaningful implementation simplification and a way to avoid certain randomness-source side channels) for a harder-to-analyze, non-Gaussian implicit error; it appears prominently in Saber and other NIST PQC round candidates, and analogous deterministic-rounding ideas appear in FHE modulus-switching (Chapter 7).

Second, it is worth being explicit about what "128-bit security" concretely means and how it is checked, since every scheme in this book will eventually cite a target like this. The exercise is: given candidate parameters $(n, q, \sigma)$, estimate the cost of the best known attack, and confirm it exceeds $2^{128}$ classical operations (or, per the modest quantum-sieving margin of Chapter 3, the correspondingly adjusted quantum figure — which is only slightly more demanding, not double).

Example 4.2 (A concrete security-estimation sketch)

Consider a primal lattice attack against LWE$_{n,q,\sigma}$: an adversary builds a lattice $\mathcal{L}$ from the rows of $A$ together with $q$ times the identity matrix (the exact embedding construction varies by attack variant) of dimension roughly $2n$ (for $m\approx n$ samples used), containing a vector related to $(e, s)$ of Euclidean norm roughly $\sigma\sqrt{n}$; the attacker runs BKZ with block size $\beta$ and succeeds once BKZ's expected shortest-vector output length (via the root-Hermite-factor heuristic of Chapter 3, Theorem 3.3) drops below that target norm. Solving for the minimal $\beta$ that achieves this, then plugging into a sieving cost model ($\approx 2^{0.292\beta}$ classical operations is a commonly cited estimate from the lattice-sieving literature, though the exact constant is model-dependent and has been revised downward by newer sieving analyses Verify), gives the attack's concrete bit-cost. For illustration only — not a substitute for running an actual estimator — typical "128-bit secure" LWE-based schemes at $q \approx 2^{50}$ require $n \approx 2048$ — per the HomomorphicEncryption.org Security Standard, $n=1024$ with a ternary secret supports only $\log_2 q \approx 27$ at 128-bit classical security, while $n=2048$ supports $\log_2 q \approx 54$, so a fifty-bit modulus at $n=1024$ would be badly insecure — and FHE schemes, which need much larger $q$ (hundreds to thousands of bits, to absorb multiplicative noise growth across many levels, Chapters 7-10) correspondingly need larger $n$ — commonly $n = N \in \{2^{12}, \dots, 2^{16}\}$ for the ring dimension once RLWE (Chapter 5) is in play — to keep $\log_2 q / n$ within the regime BKZ cannot penetrate at reasonable block sizes. This is exactly the computation the community-maintained Lattice Estimator automates, tracking multiple attack variants (primal, dual, hybrid meet-in-the-middle for sparse/ternary secrets) and updating its cost models as cryptanalysis improves.

Do Not Hand-Roll Security Estimates

Example 4.2 is a sketch meant to build intuition for the shape of the estimation problem, not a formula to plug numbers into for a real deployment. Concrete lattice-attack cost estimation has many subtleties — the sieving constant, the accuracy of the geometric series assumption for the specific $\beta$ regime, whether the secret is ternary/sparse (enabling faster hybrid attacks), whether multiple LWE samples with correlated structure are available to the attacker — that are actively revised as cryptanalysis research progresses. Every scheme discussed in this book should be parameterized using the current version of a maintained estimator, not a static table copied from an old paper; Chapter 14 returns to this in full.

Artifact 4.1 — LWE Encryption/Decryption From Scratch, With a Deliberate Failure Demo

A minimal, from-scratch LWE bit-encryption implementation, plus a deliberate demonstration of decryption failure when noise is pushed past the $q/4$ correctness threshold — making the correctness/security tension of Section 4.4 concrete rather than abstract.

"""
lwe_toy.py — textbook LWE bit encryption, built from scratch for teaching.
NOT constant-time, NOT secure at these toy parameters. Illustrates:
  1. Correct encrypt/decrypt round-tripping.
  2. Deliberate failure when noise exceeds the q/4 correctness bound.
"""

import random
from typing import List, Tuple

Vector = List[int]


def sample_uniform_vector(n: int, q: int) -> Vector:
    return [random.randrange(q) for _ in range(n)]


def sample_discrete_gaussian(sigma: float) -> int:
    """Very simple approximate discrete Gaussian sampler: round a Box-Muller
    normal sample. Good enough for demonstration; production code should use
    a proper CDT/rejection sampler with side-channel resistance."""
    return round(random.gauss(0, sigma))


def dot_mod(a: Vector, b: Vector, q: int) -> int:
    return sum(x * y for x, y in zip(a, b)) % q


def keygen(n: int, q: int) -> Vector:
    return sample_uniform_vector(n, q)


def encrypt(bit: int, s: Vector, q: int, sigma: float) -> Tuple[Vector, int]:
    n = len(s)
    a = sample_uniform_vector(n, q)
    e = sample_discrete_gaussian(sigma)
    b = (dot_mod(a, s, q) + e + bit * (q // 2)) % q
    return a, b


def decrypt(ct: Tuple[Vector, int], s: Vector, q: int) -> int:
    a, b = ct
    noisy = (b - dot_mod(a, s, q)) % q
    # map to signed representative in (-q/2, q/2]
    if noisy > q // 2:
        noisy -= q
    # round to nearest multiple of q/2, then take that multiple mod 2
    return 1 if abs(noisy) > q // 4 else 0


def run_trials(n: int, q: int, sigma: float, trials: int = 200) -> float:
    s = keygen(n, q)
    failures = 0
    for _ in range(trials):
        bit = random.randrange(2)
        ct = encrypt(bit, s, q, sigma)
        recovered = decrypt(ct, s, q)
        if recovered != bit:
            failures += 1
    return failures / trials


if __name__ == "__main__":
    random.seed(0)

    n, q = 64, 3329          # LWE dimension and modulus (toy scale, Kyber's q)

    print("=== Well-chosen noise: sigma small relative to q ===")
    sigma_safe = 3.2
    fail_rate = run_trials(n, q, sigma_safe, trials=500)
    print(f"n={n}, q={q}, sigma={sigma_safe}  ->  failure rate = {fail_rate:.4%}")

    print("\n=== Deliberately broken: sigma pushed past the q/4 threshold ===")
    # Correctness requires |e| < q/4 with high probability. Push sigma so
    # that q/4 is only about 1.5 standard deviations out. Decryption fails
    # when |e| exceeds the threshold in EITHER direction, so the relevant
    # figure is the two-tailed one: P(|Z| > 1.5) = 13.4%, not the 6.7%
    # single-tail value. Roughly one ciphertext in seven fails, causing
    # frequent decryption failures despite everything else being unchanged.
    sigma_unsafe = (q / 4) / 1.5
    fail_rate_bad = run_trials(n, q, sigma_unsafe, trials=500)
    print(f"n={n}, q={q}, sigma={sigma_unsafe:.1f}  ->  failure rate = {fail_rate_bad:.4%}")
    print("\nThis is Theorem 4.3 made concrete: the SAME scheme, the SAME key")
    print("generation and encryption code, fails routinely once noise growth")
    print("(here, injected directly; in real schemes, accumulated by homomorphic")
    print("operations -- Chapters 5-10) is not kept below the q/4 correctness bound.")

Run with safe parameters, failure rate is $0\%$ over hundreds of trials; with $\sigma$ pushed to roughly $q/6$, failures appear routinely — the exact same mechanism (noise creeping past $q/4$) that motivates modulus chains, rescaling, and bootstrapping in every scheme covered in Part II.

Key Takeaways
  • LWE asks to solve (or distinguish from random) a linear system $b = As+e \bmod q$ perturbed by small error $e$; the error is what makes the problem hard despite the underlying linear algebra being trivial. Certain
  • Search-LWE and Decision-LWE are polynomial-time equivalent (for polynomially bounded $q$), via a coordinate-by-coordinate hybrid reduction. Certain
  • Regev's reduction shows Decision-LWE is at least as hard as worst-case GapSVP/SIVP at approximation factor $\tilde O(n/\alpha)$ via a quantum algorithm — the foundational hardness evidence for essentially all schemes in this book. Certain
  • Regev encryption — encrypt by adding $e + m\lfloor q/2\rfloor$, decrypt by rounding — is the direct ancestor of every FHE scheme's encryption structure; decryption is correct exactly while $|e| < q/4$. Certain
  • Security wants noise large relative to $q$ (larger $\alpha$); correctness wants noise small relative to $q$. Managing this tension across many homomorphic operations is the central engineering problem of Part II. Certain
Chapter 5

Ring-LWE and the Path to Practicality

Learning Objectives
  • State the Ring-LWE (RLWE) problem and explain precisely how it compresses an $n$-dimensional LWE instance into a single ring element while preserving (and amplifying) computational capacity.
  • Present the LPR (Lyubashevsky-Peikert-Regev) encryption scheme built on RLWE.
  • Weigh the ring structure's two-sided consequence: NTT-driven efficiency versus a larger attack surface for structure-exploiting attacks (subfield/subring attacks).
  • Understand, at the mechanical level, how homomorphic addition and multiplication fall directly out of ring arithmetic, and why multiplication — unlike addition — causes noise growth that compounds with each successive operation.
  • Implement RLWE encryption with homomorphic add and multiply, and use it to evaluate $f(x) = x^2 + x$ entirely under encryption.

Chapter 4 built LWE encryption from an $n$-dimensional vector secret. It works, but it is expensive: a single ciphertext bit costs $O(n)$ words of storage ($a \in \mathbb{Z}_q^n$ plus one scalar $b$), and encrypting $\ell$ bits independently costs $O(n\ell)$ storage and, more importantly, gives no way to combine those $\ell$ ciphertexts' worth of computation into shared, amortized work. Ring-LWE — introduced by Lyubashevsky, Peikert, and Regev in 2010 — replaces the vector secret with a single ring element, using exactly the ring machinery of Chapter 2. The payoff is not merely smaller keys; it is the entire practical viability of FHE.

5.1 The Ring-LWE Problem

Definition 5.1 (Ring-LWE distribution)

Let $R = \mathbb{Z}[X]/(X^N+1)$ (Chapter 2) and $R_q = R/qR$. Fix a secret $s \in R_q$ (in practice sampled from a narrow/ternary/error-like distribution rather than uniformly, in the "Hermite normal form" variant used by essentially every FHE scheme — see the remark below). The RLWE distribution $A_{s,\chi}$ over $R_q \times R_q$ is sampled by drawing $a \leftarrow R_q$ uniformly, $e \leftarrow \chi$ (an error distribution over $R$, typically each coefficient an independent discrete Gaussian — this is the coefficient embedding view; the canonical-embedding view of Chapter 2 gives an equivalent, geometrically cleaner formulation), and outputting $$ (a,\; b) = (a,\; a\cdot s + e \bmod q). $$

Compare directly against Definition 4.1: the inner product $\langle a,s\rangle$ over $\mathbb{Z}_q^n$ has become a ring product $a\cdot s$ over $R_q$. This single substitution is where everything changes.

Theorem 5.1 (RLWE compresses $N$ LWE-like equations into one sample)

A single RLWE sample $(a,b) \in R_q\times R_q$ encodes $N$ scalar equations. Writing $a, s, e, b$ as coefficient vectors in $\mathbb{Z}_q^N$, the ring product $a\cdot s \bmod (X^N+1)$ is, by the negacyclic convolution structure (Chapter 2, Definition 2.2), exactly $A_a s$ for a specific structured (negacyclic, hence not arbitrary) matrix $A_a \in \mathbb{Z}_q^{N\times N}$ built from the coefficients of $a$. So one RLWE sample is algebraically equivalent to $N$ LWE samples that all share the same error-free structured coefficient matrix — vastly more information per sample than $N$ independent LWE samples ($a$ alone, one ring element, generates the entire structured matrix), at the cost of those $N$ "rows" no longer being independent, uniformly random vectors.

The practical consequence: storing one RLWE public-key sample costs $O(N)$ ring-element coefficients (two length-$N$ vectors, $a$ and $b$) to protect a length-$N$ secret, versus $O(N^2)$ for the LWE analogue (an $N\times n$ matrix $A$ needed to reach comparable dimension/capacity) — an asymptotic factor of $N$ reduction in key and ciphertext size, and, because ring multiplication is $O(N\log N)$ via the NTT (Chapter 1) rather than $O(N^2)$ matrix-vector multiplication, a corresponding speedup in every operation performed on that ciphertext. This is the "$O(N)$ storage for $O(N)$ parallelism" headline: a single ring-element ciphertext simultaneously represents (via the CRT/canonical-embedding slot structure of Chapter 2) up to $N$ independent plaintext slots that can be operated on in parallel by a single ring operation — the SIMD batching that makes CKKS-based encrypted machine learning (Part IV) computationally feasible at all.

Hermite Normal Form and Secret Distribution

Nearly every deployed FHE scheme samples the secret $s$ from the same narrow error-like distribution as $e$ (ternary $\{-1,0,1\}^N$ or a discrete Gaussian), rather than uniformly from $R_q$ as in the textbook definition above — this is the "Hermite Normal Form" (HNF) variant. It is a deliberate efficiency/security trade: a small secret makes key-switching and relinearization (Chapter 7) cheaper and keeps noise growth more predictable, and it is supported by a hardness reduction (small-secret RLWE remains as hard as standard RLWE, under mild conditions, via a secret-re-randomization argument) — but it is a real, if well-studied, deviation from the "clean" theorem statement, and parameter estimators (Chapter 14) explicitly model the secret distribution rather than assuming uniformity.

5.2 The LPR Encryption Scheme

Definition 5.2 (LPR / RLWE encryption)

Parameters: ring dimension $N$, ciphertext modulus $q$, plaintext modulus $t \ll q$ (typically $t\mid q$ or handled via explicit scaling), error distribution $\chi$.

KeyGen: sample small secret $s \leftarrow \chi_{\text{key}}$ (e.g. ternary), sample $a \leftarrow R_q$ uniformly, $e\leftarrow \chi$; set public key $pk = (a,\, b= -a\cdot s + e \bmod q)$ (or $b = a\cdot s+e$, sign convention varies by presentation); secret key $sk = s$.

Encrypt(plaintext $m \in R_t$): sample small $u,e_1,e_2 \leftarrow \chi$; output ciphertext $$ (c_0,\, c_1) = \Big(b\cdot u + e_1 + \left\lfloor\tfrac{q}{t}\right\rfloor m,\;\; a\cdot u + e_2\Big) \in R_q\times R_q. $$

Decrypt$(c_0,c_1)$: compute $c_0 + c_1\cdot s \bmod q$; scale down by $t/q$ and round to recover $m \in R_t$.

Why decryption works. Substituting, $c_0 + c_1 s = bu+e_1+\lfloor q/t\rfloor m + asu+e_2 s = (b+as)u + e_1 + e_2 s + \lfloor q/t\rfloor m$. Since $b = -as+e$ (from KeyGen), $b+as = e$, so this collapses to $eu + e_1 + e_2 s + \lfloor q/t\rfloor m$ — a message term $\lfloor q/t\rfloor m$ plus an aggregate noise term $eu+e_1+e_2 s$ built entirely from small (secret-key-scale or fresh-encryption-scale) ring elements. Scaling by $t/q$ and rounding recovers $m$ exactly, provided the aggregate noise's coefficient (or canonical-embedding) norm stays below $q/(2t)$ — the ring-arithmetic analogue of Theorem 4.3's $q/4$ correctness bound, now governed by the expansion-factor bound of Chapter 2 (Theorem 2.1) rather than a scalar inequality.

This is, structurally, the RLWE-encryption skeleton that BFV/BGV (Chapters 8–9) formalize with careful scale management, and that CKKS (Chapter 10) reuses with $m$ interpreted as an encoded approximate real/complex vector rather than an exact modular value.

5.3 Ring Structure: A Double-Edged Sword

Section 5.1 sold the ring structure as a pure efficiency win. It is not free. Replacing $N$ unstructured LWE samples with one structured RLWE sample means the hardness of RLWE rests not just on generic lattice hardness (Chapter 3) but specifically on the hardness of lattice problems over ideal lattices — lattices with the extra algebraic structure inherited from the ring $R$.

Theorem 5.2 (RLWE worst-case hardness, informal — Lyubashevsky–Peikert–Regev 2010, with the classical-reduction variant due to Peikert–Regev–Stephens-Davidowitz 2017)

Solving Decision-RLWE (for appropriate error distributions and moduli, over the ring of integers of a cyclotomic number field) is at least as hard as solving worst-case approximate SVP on ideal lattices in that ring — lattices that are additionally closed under multiplication by ring elements — for polynomial approximation factors.

The qualifier "on ideal lattices" is the crux of the entire trade-off. Ideal lattices are a strict, highly structured subclass of all lattices (Chapter 3): they carry a full ring-automorphism group action (the Galois group of Section 2.1.1), and this extra structure is exactly what the NTT and the $O(N\log N)$/$O(N)$ efficiency gains of Section 5.1 exploit. But structure that a defender can exploit for speed is, in principle, structure an attacker might exploit for cryptanalysis too — and this is not a purely theoretical worry.

Subfield and Subring Attacks — What Is (and Isn't) Actually Known

A line of cryptanalysis beginning around 2015–2017 identified attacks specific to RLWE and NTRU instances with poorly chosen structure. The main threads are Cramer, Ducas, Peikert and Regev on recovering short generators of principal ideals in cyclotomic rings (Eurocrypt 2016); Albrecht, Bai and Ducas on the subfield lattice attack against overstretched NTRU assumptions (Crypto 2016); and Cheon, Jeong and Lee's algorithm for NTRU problems (eprint 2016/139). Together they showed that: RLWE over cyclotomic rings that have large subfields, combined with certain small-modulus or "spherical error over the wrong embedding" choices, admit an attack exploiting the subfield/subring structure that beats generic lattice reduction. Crucially, these attacks do not apply to the standard power-of-two cyclotomic rings, prime moduli, and Gaussian-in-the-canonical-embedding error distributions used by production FHE schemes (BFV, BGV, CKKS as deployed) — they target specific, narrower parameter regimes (e.g., rings with many intermediate subfields, or specific non-standard error distributions) that were never adopted in practice specifically because this line of research flagged them as risky. The broader, still-open research question is whether any exploitable gap exists between "hardness of RLWE" and "hardness of generic (non-ideal) LWE/lattice problems" at the parameter regimes actually deployed — as of this writing, no such gap is known for standard parameters, and the consensus position (reflected in NIST PQC's acceptance of module-LWE/RLWE-based schemes) is that the extra ring structure has not been shown to weaken security at deployed parameter scales. This remains an area of active research and should be treated as such rather than as settled.

The pragmatic response, adopted by essentially every deployed scheme, is module-LWE (MLWE): work over $R_q^k$ (vectors of $k$ ring elements) rather than a single ring element, for small $k$ (e.g. $k=2$ or $3$, as in Kyber/ML-KEM). This interpolates between plain LWE ($k=n$, ring dimension $1$) and plain RLWE ($k=1$), retaining most of the efficiency benefit of ring structure (each of the $k$ components still gets $O(N\log N)$ ring multiplication) while diluting the pure algebraic structure an attacker could target, and it has become the default choice for standardized key-encapsulation mechanisms. Most FHE schemes covered in this book still use plain RLWE (module-LWE FHE variants exist and are discussed briefly in Chapter 15), a choice justified by the absence of known attacks at the deployed parameter scale and the greater relative importance, in the FHE setting, of squeezing out every available efficiency gain.

5.4 From RLWE to Homomorphic Encryption

Everything built so far encrypts and decrypts; nothing yet computes. The final step — and the entire reason this book exists — is noticing that the LPR ciphertext structure (Definition 5.2) supports algebraic operations on ciphertexts that correspond to algebraic operations on the underlying plaintexts, without ever decrypting.

5.4.1 Homomorphic Addition

Given ciphertexts $ct_1 = (c_{0,1}, c_{1,1})$ encrypting $m_1$ and $ct_2=(c_{0,2},c_{1,2})$ encrypting $m_2$ (under the same key), define $$ ct_{\text{add}} = (c_{0,1}+c_{0,2},\; c_{1,1}+c_{1,2}). $$ Decrypting: $(c_{0,1}+c_{0,2}) + (c_{1,1}+c_{1,2})s = \big(c_{0,1}+c_{1,1}s\big) + \big(c_{0,2}+c_{1,2}s\big) = \big(\lfloor q/t\rfloor m_1 + \text{noise}_1\big)+\big(\lfloor q/t\rfloor m_2+\text{noise}_2\big) = \lfloor q/t\rfloor(m_1+m_2) + (\text{noise}_1+\text{noise}_2)$, which decrypts to $m_1+m_2 \bmod t$ as long as the summed noise still fits under the correctness bound. Homomorphic addition is just componentwise ciphertext addition — free of any structural complication — and noise grows only additively (noise$_1$ + noise$_2$, roughly doubling in magnitude, or growing as $\sqrt{2}\times$ in RMS terms for independent noise), which is cheap relative to the correctness budget.

5.4.2 Homomorphic Multiplication

Multiplication is where the real engineering begins. Naively multiplying $ct_1\cdot ct_2$ coefficientwise does not work — ciphertexts are pairs, and $(c_{0,1}+c_{1,1}s)(c_{0,2}+c_{1,2}s)$ expands to a quadratic polynomial in $s$: $$ (c_{0,1}+c_{1,1}s)(c_{0,2}+c_{1,2}s) = c_{0,1}c_{0,2} + (c_{0,1}c_{1,2}+c_{1,1}c_{0,2})s + c_{1,1}c_{1,2}s^2. $$ Defining $d_0 = c_{0,1}c_{0,2}$, $d_1 = c_{0,1}c_{1,2}+c_{1,1}c_{0,2}$, $d_2 = c_{1,1}c_{1,2}$ (all computable directly from the ciphertexts, no secret key needed — this is the crucial homomorphism property), the triple $(d_0,d_1,d_2)$ decrypts, under the extended decryption rule $d_0+d_1 s+d_2 s^2$, to (up to the scaling factor and noise) $m_1\cdot m_2$. The ciphertext has grown from a degree-1 to a degree-2 polynomial in $s$; a further multiplication would produce degree $3$, and so on — an exponential blowup in ciphertext size that would make more than a couple of multiplications entirely impractical. Relinearization (Chapter 7) is the technique — using a specially structured evaluation/relinearization key, itself built from RLWE samples of $s^2$ — that compresses the degree-2 ciphertext $(d_0,d_1,d_2)$ back down to a degree-1 ciphertext $(c_0',c_1')$ still encrypting $m_1m_2$ (with some additional noise from the relinearization process), restoring the invariant that every ciphertext, regardless of how many multiplications preceded it, is always a pair.

Multiplicative noise growth is the crux of Chapters 7–12. Unlike addition, where noise terms simply add, multiplication produces cross terms in which the two noise components multiply each other and, more importantly, in which each noise term is multiplied by a message-scale quantity; by the ring expansion-factor bound (Chapter 2, Theorem 2.1), each such product's coefficient norm can be as large as $N$ times the product of the input norms. It is tempting to look at the raw tensor product — whose components are near-uniform elements of $R_q$, magnitude $\sim q$ — and conclude that the noise itself picks up a factor of $q$. It does not. In BFV the tensor product is immediately rescaled by $t/q$ with rounding (Chapter 8), and that division is exactly what cancels the ciphertext-scale factors: the surviving noise expansion is governed by the plaintext modulus $t$ and the ring dimension $N$, not by $q$. Noise after multiplication therefore grows multiplicatively rather than merely doubling, by a factor polynomial in $t$ and $N$ and independent of $q$ — which is exactly why homomorphic multiplication, not addition, determines how many sequential operations a leveled scheme can support before either running out of modulus budget or requiring bootstrapping (Chapter 12).

5.4.3 Counting the Multiplicative Depth a Fixed Modulus Can Absorb

It is worth making the noise-budget arithmetic concrete before it reappears formally in Chapter 7. Suppose fresh ciphertext noise has canonical-embedding norm roughly $B$, and write $C$ for the noise expansion factor of one multiplication: the constant such that multiplying two ciphertexts with noise $\le B$ yields noise $\lesssim C\cdot B$. The BFV analysis of Fan and Vercauteren (2012) gives $C$ on the order of $t\cdot N$ (up to small constants and a term from relinearization) — crucially, $C$ depends on the plaintext modulus $t$ and the ring dimension $N$, but not on $q$, because the $t/q$ rescaling in the multiplication routine divides the ciphertext-scale factors back out.

Why $C$ cannot contain a factor of $q$. Suppose it did — say $C \approx Nq$, as a naive reading of the raw tensor product suggests. Then a single multiplication would push noise from $B$ to $\approx NqB$, already far beyond the entire correctness budget $q/(2t)$, and no scheme could evaluate even one product. The fact that BFV demonstrably evaluates many sequential multiplications (Artifact 5.1 below evaluates $x^2+x$; real deployments reach depth 20 and beyond) is itself proof that the $q$-scale factors must cancel. Whenever a noise-growth estimate predicts a depth below 1, the estimate is wrong, not the scheme.

After $d$ sequential multiplications (a circuit of multiplicative depth $d$, in the sense used throughout Part II), noise has grown to roughly $B\cdot C^{d}$ — exponential in circuit depth against a fixed budget, since each extra level multiplies the noise bound by a constant factor rather than adding a constant amount. Correctness requires this to stay below $q/(2t)$ (the ring-arithmetic analogue of Theorem 4.3's bound). Solving $B\cdot C^{d} < q/(2t)$ for $d$: the maximum supportable depth is roughly $$d \;\approx\; \frac{\log\!\big(q/(2t)\big) - \log B}{\log C}, \qquad C \sim tN,$$ which is logarithmic in $q$, not polynomial. This is the single most consequential asymptotic fact governing scheme design in Part II: because raising $q$ only buys logarithmically more multiplicative depth, while raising $q$ (at fixed $n$) weakens security in direct proportion (Section 4.4's $\alpha=\sigma/q$ relationship), there is a hard ceiling on how deep a circuit a "pure leveled" scheme can evaluate before either $q$ must grow large enough to threaten security at any fixed $n$, or $n$ must grow to compensate — driving up cost — or, as Chapter 12 develops, the ciphertext must be periodically refreshed via bootstrapping rather than allowed to keep consuming budget indefinitely.

Artifact 5.1 — RLWE Encryption With Homomorphic Add/Multiply, Evaluating $f(x)=x^2+x$ Under Encryption

A from-scratch RLWE-based scheme following the LPR structure of Definition 5.2, with homomorphic addition and an unrelinearized (degree-tracking) multiplication, used to evaluate $f(x) = x^2+x$ entirely on encrypted data and verify the result against plaintext computation. The multiplication routine also has to confront, in miniature, the scale-tracking problem that Chapter 8 solves properly for BFV: naively multiplying two "already-scaled-by-$\delta$" ciphertexts mod $q$ produces a result scaled by $\delta^2$, which generally exceeds $q$ and gets scrambled by modular reduction before it can be divided back down — so the tensor product must be computed as an exact (unreduced) integer polynomial, rescaled by $t/q$ with rounding, and only then reduced mod $q$.

"""
rlwe_toy.py — a minimal RLWE-based (LPR-style) homomorphic scheme, built on
ring_poly.py (Chapter 2) and mod_arith.py (Chapter 1). Demonstrates
homomorphic addition and multiplication by evaluating f(x) = x^2 + x
entirely on ciphertexts. Not relinearized (degree grows with each
multiplication) and not constant-time -- teaching code only.
"""

import random
from typing import List, Tuple
from ring_poly import RingElement, make_ring_element


def sample_small(N: int, q: int, bound: int = 1) -> RingElement:
    """Sample a 'small' ring element with coefficients in {-bound,...,bound},
    stored in [0, q). Used for secrets and errors -- the ring analogue of a
    narrow discrete Gaussian / ternary distribution."""
    coeffs = [random.randint(-bound, bound) % q for _ in range(N)]
    return RingElement(coeffs, q)


def sample_uniform_ring(N: int, q: int) -> RingElement:
    return RingElement([random.randrange(q) for _ in range(N)], q)


def scale_and_round(poly: RingElement, from_mod: int, to_mod: int) -> RingElement:
    """Round each coefficient of poly (mod from_mod) down to mod to_mod,
    i.e. multiply by to_mod/from_mod and round -- used for plaintext scaling."""
    coeffs = []
    for c in poly.coeffs:
        c_signed = c if c <= from_mod // 2 else c - from_mod
        scaled = round(c_signed * to_mod / from_mod) % to_mod
        coeffs.append(scaled)
    return RingElement(coeffs, to_mod)


class RLWEParams:
    def __init__(self, N: int, q: int, t: int, err_bound: int = 1):
        self.N = N
        self.q = q
        self.t = t
        self.err_bound = err_bound
        self.delta = q // t   # scaling factor floor(q/t)


def keygen(p: RLWEParams):
    s = sample_small(p.N, p.q, p.err_bound)
    a = sample_uniform_ring(p.N, p.q)
    e = sample_small(p.N, p.q, p.err_bound)
    b = -(a.schoolbook_mul(s)) + e                # b = -a*s + e  (public key)
    return s, (a, b)


def encrypt(m_coeffs: List[int], pk, p: RLWEParams) -> Tuple[RingElement, RingElement]:
    a, b = pk
    m = RingElement([c % p.t for c in m_coeffs] + [0]*(p.N-len(m_coeffs)), p.t)
    m_scaled = RingElement([(c * p.delta) % p.q for c in m.coeffs], p.q)

    u = sample_small(p.N, p.q, p.err_bound)
    e1 = sample_small(p.N, p.q, p.err_bound)
    e2 = sample_small(p.N, p.q, p.err_bound)

    c0 = b.schoolbook_mul(u) + e1 + m_scaled
    c1 = a.schoolbook_mul(u) + e2
    return (c0, c1)


def decrypt_deg1(ct, s: RingElement, p: RLWEParams) -> List[int]:
    c0, c1 = ct
    noisy = c0 + c1.schoolbook_mul(s)
    return scale_and_round(noisy, p.q, p.t).coeffs


def decrypt_deg2(ct3, s: RingElement, p: RLWEParams) -> List[int]:
    """Decrypt an un-relinearized product ciphertext (d0, d1, d2), which
    encrypts under d0 + d1*s + d2*s^2 (Section 5.4.2)."""
    d0, d1, d2 = ct3
    s2 = s.schoolbook_mul(s)
    noisy = d0 + d1.schoolbook_mul(s) + d2.schoolbook_mul(s2)
    return scale_and_round(noisy, p.q, p.t).coeffs


def he_add(ct1, ct2) -> Tuple[RingElement, RingElement]:
    return (ct1[0] + ct2[0], ct1[1] + ct2[1])


def _balanced(c: int, q: int) -> int:
    """Map a residue in [0, q) to the balanced signed representative in
    (-q/2, q/2] -- needed so 'raw' products approximate real-number products
    rather than wrapping mod q."""
    return c if c <= q // 2 else c - q


def _raw_negacyclic_mul(a: List[int], b: List[int], N: int) -> List[int]:
    """Exact (unbounded-integer, NOT reduced mod q) negacyclic convolution.
    Small-scale schoolbook is fine here: N is tiny in this demo, and Python
    ints are arbitrary precision, so no overflow/wraparound corrupts the
    scale-tracking below."""
    result = [0] * N
    for i in range(N):
        if a[i] == 0:
            continue
        for j in range(N):
            k = i + j
            val = a[i] * b[j]
            if k >= N:
                result[k - N] -= val   # negacyclic wraparound: sign flips
            else:
                result[k] += val
    return result


def _round_div(x: int, denom: int) -> int:
    """Round x / denom to the nearest integer (denom > 0), correct for
    negative x -- Python's // floors rather than rounds."""
    if x >= 0:
        return (x + denom // 2) // denom
    return -((-x + denom // 2) // denom)


def he_mul(ct1, ct2, p: RLWEParams):
    """Homomorphic multiplication: degree-1 x degree-1 -> degree-2 ciphertext
    (d0, d1, d2), as derived algebraically in Section 5.4.2.

    A point worth dwelling on: since each input ciphertext already encodes
    its message scaled by delta = floor(q/t) (Definition 5.2), the raw tensor
    product (c0_1+c1_1 s)(c0_2+c1_2 s) decrypts to approximately delta^2 *
    m1*m2, not delta * m1*m2 -- and delta^2 generally exceeds q, so reducing
    naively mod q at every step (as plain RingElement.schoolbook_mul does)
    scrambles the scale beyond recovery. The fix -- and this IS what BFV
    actually does (Chapter 8) -- is to compute each raw product as an exact
    integer polynomial (no mod-q reduction), rescale every coefficient by
    t/q with rounding, and only THEN reduce mod q. That single rescale
    collapses the doubled scale factor delta^2 back down to one factor of
    delta, exactly canceling with decrypt_deg2's single descale step."""
    c0_1 = [_balanced(c, p.q) for c in ct1[0].coeffs]
    c1_1 = [_balanced(c, p.q) for c in ct1[1].coeffs]
    c0_2 = [_balanced(c, p.q) for c in ct2[0].coeffs]
    c1_2 = [_balanced(c, p.q) for c in ct2[1].coeffs]

    raw_d0 = _raw_negacyclic_mul(c0_1, c0_2, p.N)
    raw_d1 = [x + y for x, y in zip(_raw_negacyclic_mul(c0_1, c1_2, p.N),
                                     _raw_negacyclic_mul(c1_1, c0_2, p.N))]
    raw_d2 = _raw_negacyclic_mul(c1_1, c1_2, p.N)

    d0 = RingElement([_round_div(c * p.t, p.q) % p.q for c in raw_d0], p.q)
    d1 = RingElement([_round_div(c * p.t, p.q) % p.q for c in raw_d1], p.q)
    d2 = RingElement([_round_div(c * p.t, p.q) % p.q for c in raw_d2], p.q)
    return (d0, d1, d2)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    random.seed(2)

    # Small demo parameters: real schemes use N >= 2**12 and q with 100s of bits.
    N, q, t = 16, 12289, 8   # q = 12289 is NTT-friendly (Kyber-era prime), t=8 plaintext modulus
    params = RLWEParams(N=N, q=q, t=t, err_bound=1)

    s, pk = keygen(params)

    x_val = 5   # plaintext integer, encoded as constant-term polynomial, x_val < t
    x_poly = [x_val] + [0] * (N - 1)
    ct_x = encrypt(x_poly, pk, params)

    # --- homomorphic evaluation of f(x) = x^2 + x -------------------------
    ct_x_squared = he_mul(ct_x, ct_x, params)            # degree-2 ciphertext
    x_sq_recovered = decrypt_deg2(ct_x_squared, s, params)
    print("decrypt(x^2) constant term:", x_sq_recovered[0], " expected:", (x_val*x_val) % t)

    # add x (degree-1) to x^2 (degree-2): pad the degree-1 ciphertext with a
    # zero d2 component so shapes match, then add componentwise.
    zero = RingElement([0]*N, q)
    ct_x_padded = (ct_x[0], ct_x[1], zero)
    ct_result = tuple(a + b for a, b in zip(ct_x_squared, ct_x_padded))

    result = decrypt_deg2(ct_result, s, params)
    expected = (x_val * x_val + x_val) % t
    print("decrypt(x^2 + x) constant term:", result[0], " expected:", expected)
    print("f(x) = x^2 + x, evaluated entirely under encryption:",
          result[0] == expected)

For $x=5, t=8$: $f(5) = 25+5=30 \equiv 30 \bmod 8 = 6$. The program decrypts the homomorphically computed ciphertext and confirms the constant-term slot recovers $6$ — the entire computation $x\mapsto x^2+x$ performed without ever decrypting an intermediate value, using only the ciphertext-space operations derived algebraically in Section 5.4. Note the ciphertext returned by he_mul has grown to three ring-element components (degree 2 in $s$); real schemes immediately relinearize (Chapter 7) to bring it back to two components before any further multiplication, which this toy implementation deliberately omits to keep the degree-growth mechanism visible.

Key Takeaways
  • RLWE replaces the LWE secret vector $s\in\mathbb{Z}_q^n$ with a single ring element $s\in R_q$; one RLWE sample is algebraically equivalent to $N$ structured LWE-like equations, giving $O(N)$ storage where a comparable LWE instance needs $O(N^2)$, plus $O(N\log N)$ ring multiplication via the NTT. Certain
  • RLWE's worst-case hardness reduction targets ideal lattices specifically, not general lattices — extra structure that buys efficiency but is also the surface that subfield/subring attacks target; at standard deployed parameters (power-of-two cyclotomics, prime moduli, canonical-embedding Gaussian error) no such attack is currently known to beat generic lattice reduction, but this remains actively studied. Likely
  • The LPR scheme's decryption identity $c_0+c_1 s = e u + e_1 + e_2 s + \lfloor q/t\rfloor m$ is the direct algebraic ancestor of BFV, BGV, and CKKS decryption. Certain
  • Homomorphic addition is componentwise ciphertext addition with additive noise growth; homomorphic multiplication produces a degree-2 (in $s$) ciphertext with multiplicative noise growth, requiring relinearization (Chapter 7) to restore the degree-1 invariant — the central engineering challenge of every scheme in Part II. Certain
Part II

Core FHE Theory

Part I built the hardness foundation — modular arithmetic, polynomial rings, lattices, and (Ring-)LWE. Part II is where that foundation becomes a cryptosystem. Chapters 6–9 trace the field from partially homomorphic encryption through Gentry's blueprint to the two "exact arithmetic" workhorses of modern FHE, BFV and BGV; later chapters in this part (10–13) continue on to CKKS, TFHE, bootstrapping, and ciphertext packing.
Chapter 6

The Arc of Homomorphic Encryption — From Partial to Full

Learning Objectives
  • State precisely what "homomorphic" means for an encryption scheme, and distinguish partial, somewhat, leveled, and fully homomorphic encryption
  • Explain the algebraic mechanism behind RSA, ElGamal, and Paillier's single-operation homomorphisms
  • Understand the significance of the Boneh–Goh–Nissim scheme as the first "one multiplication, unlimited additions" construction
  • State Gentry's bootstrapping theorem and the role of squashing and circular security in his 2009 blueprint
  • Trace the second generation of LWE/RLWE-based schemes (BV11, BGV12, BFV12) and explain why modulus switching made FHE practical rather than merely possible

Every scheme in this book is an answer to a question posed, in essentially modern form, in 1978: can an untrusted party compute on data it cannot read? The forty-year path from that question to a deployable answer is not a smooth line of incremental improvement — it is a sequence of qualitative jumps, each of which removed a specific obstruction that had been considered fundamental. This chapter is a guided tour of that arc: partially homomorphic encryption (PHE), the Boneh–Goh–Nissim bridge, Gentry's 2009 blueprint for full homomorphism, and the second-generation lattice schemes that turned Gentry's existence proof into engineering practice.

6.1 What "Homomorphic" Means for a Cryptosystem

Definition 6.1 — Homomorphic Encryption

Let $\mathcal{E} = (\mathsf{KeyGen}, \mathsf{Enc}, \mathsf{Dec})$ be a public-key encryption scheme with plaintext space $(\mathcal{M}, \star)$ and ciphertext space $\mathcal{C}$. $\mathcal{E}$ is homomorphic with respect to $\star$ if there exists an efficient algorithm $\mathsf{Eval}$ such that for all valid key pairs, all $m_1, m_2 \in \mathcal{M}$, and $c_i \leftarrow \mathsf{Enc}(m_i)$, $$\mathsf{Dec}\bigl(\mathsf{Eval}(\star, c_1, c_2)\bigr) = m_1 \star m_2.$$ $\mathcal{E}$ is fully homomorphic (FHE) if this holds for an arbitrary Boolean or arithmetic circuit built from a functionally complete gate set (e.g. $\{+, \times\}$ over a ring, or $\{\mathrm{NAND}\}$), for circuits of unbounded depth, with $\mathsf{Eval}$'s output size independent of circuit depth (compactness) and correctness holding with overwhelming probability for every depth.

Two clauses in this definition do almost all of the work, and both will recur throughout Part II. Unbounded depth rules out any scheme whose ciphertexts accumulate an operation-dependent quantity — noise, error, ciphertext size — that eventually causes decryption to fail. Compactness rules out a cheat: without it, "computing" $f$ homomorphically could simply mean shipping the ciphertexts and a description of $f$ to the decrypting party, who decrypts everything and evaluates $f$ in the clear. Compactness forces $\mathsf{Eval}$ to actually collapse the computation into a fixed-size answer before decryption ever happens.

Schemes are classified by how far short of Definition 6.1 they fall:

  • Partially homomorphic encryption (PHE): homomorphic for exactly one operation ($+$ or $\times$), unboundedly many times.
  • Somewhat homomorphic encryption (SHE): homomorphic for both $+$ and $\times$, but only up to some fixed, scheme-dependent multiplicative depth before correctness breaks down.
  • Leveled FHE: SHE where the depth bound $L$ is a design-time parameter — you choose $L$ before generating keys, pay a parameter cost proportional to $L$, and the scheme is guaranteed correct for any circuit of depth $\le L$.
  • Fully homomorphic encryption (FHE): Definition 6.1 in full — no depth bound, via bootstrapping (Chapter 12).

Every scheme this book treats in depth — BGV, BFV, CKKS, TFHE — is, in its bare form, a leveled SHE scheme. "Fully" homomorphic is a property earned by bolting on bootstrapping, which is precisely why Microsoft SEAL, which implements BFV, BGV and CKKS but never calls Gentry's bootstrapping procedure, is correctly described as leveled rather than fully homomorphic Certain, verified — a point we will return to at the end of Chapter 7.

6.2 Partially Homomorphic Encryption: One Operation, No Limit

The question of whether encryption could support computation was posed explicitly by Rivest, Adleman, and Dertouzos in their 1978 paper "On Data Banks and Privacy Homomorphisms," written the same year RSA itself was published. They observed that RSA already has a useful algebraic structure and asked whether richer "privacy homomorphisms" — schemes supporting both addition and multiplication simultaneously — might exist. Certain Answering that question fully took thirty-one years; answering it partially took almost no time at all, because several already-known or soon-to-be-invented schemes turned out to be homomorphic for exactly one operation.

6.2.1 RSA: multiplicative

Textbook RSA encrypts $m \in \mathbb{Z}_n^*$ (where $n = pq$) as $c = m^e \bmod n$. Given $c_1 = m_1^e$ and $c_2 = m_2^e$, $$c_1 \cdot c_2 \equiv (m_1 m_2)^e \pmod n,$$ so $\mathsf{Dec}(c_1 c_2) = m_1 m_2 \bmod n$. Ciphertext multiplication is plaintext multiplication, unboundedly — RSA is multiplicatively homomorphic. It is not additively homomorphic: there is no efficient combination of $m_1^e$ and $m_2^e$ that yields $(m_1+m_2)^e$ without knowing $m_1, m_2$.

6.2.2 ElGamal: multiplicative

ElGamal encrypts $m$ (embedded in a cyclic group $G = \langle g \rangle$ of order $q$) under public key $h = g^x$ as $(c_1, c_2) = (g^r, m \cdot h^r)$ for random $r$. Component-wise multiplication of two ciphertexts, $$(g^{r_1} g^{r_2},\ m_1 h^{r_1} \cdot m_2 h^{r_2}) = (g^{r_1+r_2},\ (m_1 m_2) h^{r_1+r_2}),$$ is a valid encryption of $m_1 m_2$ under randomness $r_1+r_2$. ElGamal is multiplicatively homomorphic, exploited directly in mixnets and re-randomizable voting schemes.

6.2.3 Paillier: additive

Paillier (1999) encrypts $m \in \mathbb{Z}_n$ under modulus $n=pq$ as $c = g^m r^n \bmod n^2$ for random $r \in \mathbb{Z}_n^*$ and fixed generator $g$. Multiplying ciphertexts: $$c_1 c_2 \equiv g^{m_1+m_2}(r_1r_2)^n \pmod{n^2},$$ which decrypts to $m_1+m_2 \bmod n$. Paillier is the canonical example of the complementary case: multiplication in the ciphertext group realizes addition in the plaintext group, and it additionally supports "plaintext-times-ciphertext" scalar multiplication ($c^k$ decrypts to $km$), which is enough for weighted sums — the operation behind private-sum aggregation, e-voting tallies, and additively homomorphic secure aggregation in federated learning (Chapter 23).

Example 6.1 — Why "one operation" is a hard ceiling

Given only Paillier ciphertexts $c_1 = \mathsf{Enc}(m_1)$, $c_2 = \mathsf{Enc}(m_2)$, there is no known efficient way to produce $\mathsf{Enc}(m_1 m_2)$. The obstruction is not a missing trick; it is that Paillier's ciphertext group $(\mathbb{Z}_{n^2}^*, \times)$ is only linearly related to the plaintext group $(\mathbb{Z}_n, +)$ via the discrete-exponential map $m \mapsto g^m$. Multiplying plaintexts would require exponentiating an exponent — an operation the group structure simply does not expose. This is the wall RAD asked about in 1978, and it is a wall for every scheme built from a single abelian group.

PHE schemes remain useful today precisely because many real workloads need only one operation: Paillier-based aggregation, ElGamal-based re-encryption mixnets, and RSA-based blind signatures are all production technology. But no PHE scheme can evaluate even a two-gate circuit that mixes $+$ and $\times$ — e.g. it cannot compute $(m_1+m_2)\cdot m_3$ — which excludes essentially every ML inference pipeline.

6.3 The Bridge: Boneh–Goh–Nissim (2005)

The next structural advance came from an unexpected direction: pairing-based cryptography. Boneh, Goh, and Nissim (2005) constructed a scheme using bilinear groups $(G_1, G_2, G_T)$ with a pairing $e: G_1 \times G_1 \to G_T$ that supports unboundedly many additions and exactly one multiplication. Certain

The idea: encrypt $m$ additively (à la ElGamal-in-the-exponent) in $G_1$. Ciphertexts in $G_1$ can be combined additively without limit using the group operation, exactly as in Paillier/ElGamal-style schemes. But the pairing $e(\cdot,\cdot)$ lets you take two $G_1$-ciphertexts and multiply their encoded exponents into $G_T$ — the pairing computes a "multiplication in the exponent" as a side effect of bilinearity, $e(g^{a}, g^{b}) = e(g,g)^{ab}$. This yields exactly one level of multiplication, landing the result in $G_T$; because $G_T$ has no further pairing available, you cannot multiply again. But you can still add unboundedly within $G_T$ using its group operation.

Result 6.1 (Boneh–Goh–Nissim, informal)

BGN evaluates any polynomial that is a sum of monomials of degree $\le 2$ in the plaintexts — equivalently, any 2-DNF formula, or any quadratic form. Certain

This is a genuine qualitative leap past PHE: it is the first scheme to mix operation types at all. It is enough for private information retrieval, computing quadratic statistics (variances, dot products, hence linear classifiers), and small-scale private set operations. It is not enough for anything with real multiplicative depth — no cubic terms, no iterated multiplication, no neural network. But historically BGN mattered less for what it computed than for what it demonstrated: mixing $+$ and $\times$ homomorphically was possible at all, using entirely different mathematics (bilinear pairings) from what would ultimately win. It also sharpened the target: the open problem was no longer "can we mix operations," but "can we mix them to unbounded depth."

6.4 Gentry 2009: The Bootstrapping Blueprint

Craig Gentry's 2009 STOC paper, "Fully Homomorphic Encryption Using Ideal Lattices," answered the thirty-one-year-old RAD question in the affirmative, and did so via a technique — bootstrapping — general enough to be the template every subsequent FHE scheme still uses. Certain

6.4.1 The core obstruction: noise growth

Gentry's scheme, like every scheme in this book, is a lattice-based SHE construction in which ciphertexts carry a hidden "noise" term that is essential for security (Chapter 4's LWE hardness assumption is precisely that noisy linear equations are hard to solve) but that grows with every homomorphic operation, especially multiplication. Push a ciphertext through enough gates and the noise overtakes the signal: decryption returns garbage. An SHE scheme is therefore correct only up to some fixed multiplicative depth $L$ determined by its parameters — the exact obstruction Definition 6.1's "unbounded depth" clause is designed to detect.

6.4.2 The bootstrapping theorem
Theorem 6.2 — Gentry's Bootstrapping Theorem (informal)

Let $\mathcal{E}$ be a somewhat homomorphic encryption scheme that is bootstrappable: $\mathcal{E}$ can homomorphically evaluate its own decryption circuit $\mathsf{Dec}_{sk}(\cdot)$, augmented with at least one additional gate (e.g. one more addition or one more NAND), without exceeding its noise budget. Then $\mathcal{E}$ can be converted into a fully homomorphic encryption scheme of unbounded depth, at the cost of one bootstrapping operation per "level" of circuit evaluated. Certain

The mechanism: take a ciphertext $c$ whose noise is near the correctness ceiling. Encrypt the secret key itself under a (possibly different) public key of the same scheme, producing $\widehat{sk} = \mathsf{Enc}_{pk'}(sk)$ — this is published as part of an augmented public key. Then homomorphically evaluate the decryption circuit $\mathsf{Dec}(\cdot)$, feeding it the encrypted ciphertext $\mathsf{Enc}_{pk'}(c)$ (encrypted under the fresh key) and the encrypted secret key $\widehat{sk}$, both as ciphertexts. The output is a new encryption, under $pk'$, of the same plaintext $\mathsf{Dec}_{sk}(c)$ — but this new ciphertext's noise level is fixed by the depth of the decryption circuit, not by whatever noise $c$ had accumulated. If the scheme is bootstrappable — if it has enough remaining noise budget to evaluate its own (possibly complex) decryption circuit plus one more gate — then bootstrapping acts as a noise "reset": refresh, and continue computing.

The circular security assumption

Bootstrapping under a single key pair requires publishing an encryption of the secret key under (functionally) its own public key. Security then no longer follows from semantic security alone: it requires assuming the scheme stays secure when the adversary is handed an encryption of its own key, a strictly stronger assumption called circular security. The assumption is unproven in general and is a standing caveat on every unbounded-depth FHE deployment. Certain

Two precisions, both easy to get wrong and both worth carrying into Chapter 12. First, bootstrappability alone does not require circular security — it gives leveled FHE outright. Generate $L{+}1$ independent key pairs and publish the chain $\mathrm{Enc}_{pk_{i+1}}(sk_i)$; each bootstrap moves the ciphertext from key $i$ to key $i{+}1$, no key ever encrypts itself, and any circuit of depth $\le L$ is evaluable under plain semantic security. The cost is an evaluation key that grows linearly in $L$, and a depth ceiling fixed in advance. Circular security is what buys you a single, constant-size key pair and genuinely unbounded depth — it is the price of compactness and unboundedness, not of bootstrapping as such (Gentry 2009, §4; Barak, CS 127 notes).

Second, circular security is not the same as KDM security. Circular security is the special case in which the adversary sees encryptions of the key itself (or a fixed simple function of it); KDM security quantifies over arbitrary efficiently-computable functions of the key. FHE needs the former, which is weaker; conflating them overstates the assumption.

6.4.3 Squashing the decryption circuit

Bootstrappability is not automatic — for Gentry's original ideal-lattice scheme, the natural decryption circuit was too deep for the scheme to evaluate homomorphically (decryption itself involves a large inner product and modular reduction, which is a higher-multiplicative-depth computation than the scheme could tolerate at its noise ceiling — a chicken-and-egg problem sometimes called the "vicious cycle"). Gentry's fix was squashing: modify the scheme so that decryption is expressible as a much shallower circuit, specifically a sparse subset-sum: publish, as part of the public key, a large set of rational numbers (a "hint") of which a sparse subset (indexed secretly by bits of the real secret key) sums to something close to the noise term. Decryption is then reduced to (a) a sparse subset-sum computation, plus (b) rounding — both low-depth relative to the original decryption circuit. This brings the decryption circuit's depth below the scheme's homomorphic capacity, achieving bootstrappability at the cost of introducing a new hardness assumption (sparse subset-sum) and a large public key. Certain

Gentry's 2009 construction was a landmark and an existence proof, not a deployable system: parameter sizes and bootstrapping latency were wildly impractical (early implementations measured bootstrapping times in minutes, with public keys in the gigabytes). What it established was that the RAD question had a "yes" answer, and it handed the field a template — build a bootstrappable SHE, then bootstrap — that every practical scheme since has either used directly (TFHE, Chapter 11) or deliberately architected around (BGV/BFV/CKKS's leveled design, which tries to avoid needing bootstrapping at all for a target circuit).

6.5 Second Generation: LWE, RLWE, and the Rise of Modulus Switching

The years 2010–2012 replaced Gentry's ideal-lattice construction with schemes built directly on (Ring-)LWE, and introduced a technique — modulus switching — that changed FHE's complexity from "theoretically possible" to "computationally tractable." This is the work Part I's Chapters 4–5 were building toward.

6.5.1 Brakerski–Vaikuntanathan 2011 (BV11)

Brakerski and Vaikuntanathan, "Efficient Fully Homomorphic Encryption from (Standard) LWE" (FOCS 2011), gave the first FHE-track scheme whose security rested on plain LWE — no ideal-lattice or ring assumption required — closing a gap left by Gentry's construction (which relied on ideal lattices over specific rings, a less battle-tested assumption at the time). Certain Their scheme introduced two technical devices that became permanent fixtures of the field:

  • Relinearization: homomorphic multiplication naturally produces a ciphertext that decrypts under a higher-degree function of the secret key (Chapter 8 makes this precise for BFV). Relinearization uses an auxiliary evaluation key to fold the ciphertext back down to a linear function of the secret key, so it can be fed into further homomorphic operations exactly like a fresh ciphertext.
  • Dimension-modulus reduction: the direct ancestor of modulus switching (§6.5.2) — reduce the LWE dimension and modulus together after each multiplication to control noise growth without bootstrapping.
6.5.2 BGV12: modulus switching and leveled FHE

Brakerski, Gentry, and Vaikuntanathan, "(Leveled) Fully Homomorphic Encryption without Bootstrapping" (ITCS 2012), crystallized the idea into modulus switching: maintain a decreasing chain of ciphertext moduli $q_L > q_{L-1} > \cdots > q_0$, and after each multiplication, homomorphically "switch" the ciphertext from modulus $q_i$ to the smaller $q_{i-1}$. Chapter 9 works through the mechanics in full; the headline result is in the paper's title — a scheme whose multiplicative depth budget $L$ is fixed entirely at parameter-generation time, with no bootstrapping required to reach that depth. This is the first scheme that is "leveled FHE" in the sense of §6.1 as a design goal rather than an incidental property, and it is also the paper that popularized batching plaintext slots via the Chinese Remainder Theorem over the plaintext ring (Chapter 13), turning FHE into a SIMD computation model. Credit for the technique itself belongs earlier, to Smart and Vercauteren ("Fully Homomorphic SIMD Operations", eprint 2011/133); BGV made it standard practice rather than inventing it. Certain

6.5.3 BFV12: scale-invariance

Also in 2012, Brakerski published "Fully Homomorphic Encryption without Modulus Switching from Classical GapSVP" (CRYPTO 2012), giving a scheme that controls noise growth without an explicit modulus chain, via a design in which the plaintext-to-ciphertext scaling factor is baked in at encryption time and multiplication naturally rescales itself — "scale-invariant" in the sense Chapter 8 formalizes. Fan and Vercauteren then adapted this scale-invariant scheme to the ring-LWE setting in "Somewhat Practical Fully Homomorphic Encryption" (ePrint 2012/144), producing what the field now calls BFV. Certain BGV and BFV thus emerged the same year, from the same underlying (R)LWE hardness assumption, targeting the identical goal — exact leveled arithmetic over $\mathbb{Z}_t$ — via two philosophically distinct noise-management strategies. Chapters 8 and 9 are, in a real sense, a matched pair for exactly this reason, and Chapter 7 exists to give you the shared vocabulary (noise budget, multiplicative depth) needed to compare them honestly.

MilestoneYearClassKey contribution
RSA1978PHEMultiplicative homomorphism; RAD pose the general question the same year

Boneh–Goh–Nissim2005BridgeUnlimited $+$, exactly one $\times$, via bilinear pairings

Artifact 6.1 — Toy Paillier vs. Chapter 5 RLWE SHE, side by side

The cleanest way to internalize the leap from PHE to SHE is to instrument both a PHE scheme and an SHE scheme and watch what each can and cannot do. Below is a minimal, insecure-parameter toy comparison (Python, arbitrary precision via pow/Python integers) — not for production use, purely pedagogical.

import random

# ---- Toy Paillier (additive PHE) ----
def paillier_keygen(bits=256):
    p, q = gen_prime(bits), gen_prime(bits)   # toy prime generator, omitted
    n = p * q
    g = n + 1                          # standard simplification: g = n+1
    lam = (p-1) * (q-1)
    return (n, g), lam

def paillier_encrypt(pk, m):
    n, g = pk
    r = random.randrange(1, n)
    return (pow(g, m, n*n) * pow(r, n, n*n)) % (n*n)

def paillier_add(pk, c1, c2):
    n, _ = pk
    return (c1 * c2) % (n*n)          # ciphertext MULT = plaintext ADD

# paillier_add composes without limit: try 10,000 additions, still decrypts exactly.
# There is no paillier_mult: no algebraic operation on (c1, c2) yields Enc(m1*m2).

# ---- Chapter 5 toy RLWE SHE (both + and *, but noise-limited) ----
# ct = (c0, c1) in R_q; using the she_add / she_mult from Chapter 5's reference implementation:
ct1, ct2, ct3 = she_encrypt(sk, m1), she_encrypt(sk, m2), she_encrypt(sk, m3)

ct_sum  = she_add(ct1, ct2)                 # noise: additive, cheap
ct_prod = she_mult(ct1, ct2, relin_key)     # noise: multiplicative, expensive

# (m1 + m2) * m3 -- a circuit PHE cannot express at all:
result = she_mult(ct_sum, ct3, relin_key)
assert she_decrypt(sk, result) == (m1 + m2) * m3   # holds ONLY while noise budget remains

# Push she_mult in a loop and log noise_budget(ct) after each call (Chapter 7's instrument) --
# you will watch it fall to zero and decryption fail, which is precisely the wall PHE never hits
# (because PHE has no multiplicative noise growth to hit it with) and which SHE hits by design.

The contrast to internalize: Paillier's paillier_add loop runs forever because there is no noise term at all in the scheme's algebraic structure — correctness is exact modular arithmetic, full stop. The RLWE SHE's she_add/she_mult loop is bounded not by an algebraic limitation but by a noise budget that both operations consume at very different rates. That asymmetry — cheap additive growth, expensive multiplicative growth — is the entire subject of Chapter 7.

Key Takeaways
  • Definition 6.1's two teeth are unbounded depth and compactness; everything short of both is PHE, SHE, or leveled FHE, not FHE proper.
  • RSA and ElGamal are multiplicatively homomorphic; Paillier is additively homomorphic — each unlimited in exactly one operation, with no known way to add the other.
  • Boneh–Goh–Nissim (2005) was the first to mix operation types: unlimited additions, exactly one multiplication, via bilinear pairings — evaluating any 2-DNF/quadratic form.
  • Gentry (2009) proved bootstrapping converts any bootstrappable SHE scheme into FHE: with a chain of independent keys this yields leveled FHE under plain semantic security, and a circular security assumption upgrades it to unbounded depth with a single compact key pair. Squashing made his ideal-lattice scheme's decryption circuit shallow enough to be bootstrappable.
  • BV11 moved FHE onto plain LWE and introduced relinearization; BGV12 introduced modulus switching and made leveled FHE (no bootstrapping needed up to a chosen depth) practical; BFV (Brakerski 2012 / Fan–Vercauteren 2012) achieved the same leveled goal via scale-invariance instead of a modulus chain.
  • BGV and BFV target the same problem — exact leveled integer arithmetic — with two different noise-management philosophies, which Chapters 8 and 9 develop in parallel.
Chapter 7

The Noise Barrier

Learning Objectives
  • Articulate the fundamental tension between noise-as-security and noise-as-obstruction in every LWE-based scheme
  • Derive, at least qualitatively, why additive noise growth is linear/cheap and multiplicative noise growth is quadratic/exponential-if-unmanaged
  • Define the noise budget formally and explain why, left unmanaged, the ciphertext modulus $q$ must grow exponentially in circuit depth
  • Compare the three canonical noise-management strategies — modulus switching (BGV), scale-invariance (BFV), rescaling (CKKS) — at the level of what each one is doing to the noise-to-modulus ratio
  • Explain why "what is this circuit's multiplicative depth?" is the first question to ask of any FHE workload, and why SEAL's leveled-only design makes that question a hard ceiling rather than a soft cost

If Chapter 6 was the history of FHE, this chapter is its physics. Every scheme surveyed there — and every scheme in Chapters 8 through 11 — is built on the same resource, and every design decision in those schemes is a decision about how to spend it. That resource is the noise budget, and this chapter formalizes it well enough that "how deep can my circuit be?" becomes a question with a number attached, not a guess.

7.1 The Fundamental Tension

Recall from Chapter 4 that the LWE assumption is: given many samples $(\mathbf{a}_i, b_i = \langle \mathbf{a}_i, \mathbf{s}\rangle + e_i \bmod q)$ with $e_i$ small and $\mathbf{s}$ fixed, recovering $\mathbf{s}$ is computationally hard. Without the error term $e_i$, this is ordinary linear algebra — Gaussian elimination recovers $\mathbf{s}$ from $n$ samples in polynomial time. The error is not an unfortunate side effect of an otherwise-clean construction; it is the entire source of hardness. Every RLWE-based ciphertext in this book is, structurally, an LWE/RLWE sample: a linear (or ring-linear) relation in the secret, perturbed by a small error that hides the plaintext and that makes the relation computationally irreversible without the secret key.

This produces an unavoidable tension:

Observation 7.1 — The noise dilemma

Security requires the noise to be large enough, relative to the "gap" an attacker would need to close, that no efficient algorithm can distinguish an LWE sample from uniform randomness (or otherwise recover $\mathbf{s}$).
Correctness requires the noise to remain small enough, relative to the plaintext's scaling in the ciphertext, that decryption's rounding step recovers the exact message rather than a neighboring, wrong value.
Every homomorphic operation performed on a ciphertext increases its noise. FHE, at the mechanical level, is the discipline of computing as much as possible before these two requirements collide. Certain

This single observation explains an enormous amount of what otherwise looks like arbitrary complexity in FHE engineering: why parameters must be chosen jointly for security and for a target circuit depth; why "just increase $q$" is not a free lunch (Chapter 14 will show it costs security bits, forcing $n$ up too, which costs performance); and why every scheme in this book invests real design effort in slowing noise growth rather than simply tolerating it.

7.2 Noise Growth Under Addition: Linear and Cheap

Consider two RLWE ciphertexts (using the additive-noise convention of Chapter 5) $\mathsf{ct}_1 = (c_0, c_1)$, $\mathsf{ct}_2 = (c_0', c_1')$ encrypting $m_1, m_2$ respectively, each satisfying a decryption relation of the schematic form $$c_0 + c_1 s \equiv \Delta m + v \pmod q,$$ where $\Delta$ is a scaling factor (Chapter 8 pins this down exactly for BFV) and $v$ is the noise term, $\|v\| \le B$ for some bound $B$. Homomorphic addition is component-wise: $\mathsf{ct}_{\mathrm{add}} = (c_0+c_0',\, c_1+c_1')$. Its decryption relation is the sum of the two: $$(c_0+c_0') + (c_1+c_1')s \equiv \Delta(m_1+m_2) + (v+v') \pmod q.$$ The new noise is $v_{\mathrm{add}} = v + v'$, and by the triangle inequality $\|v_{\mathrm{add}}\| \le \|v\| + \|v'\| \le 2B$. Noise under addition grows additively — at most doubling per addition of comparably-noisy operands, and in a circuit with $d$ sequential additions, the noise bound is $O(dB)$: linear in the number of operations. Certain This is why addition is, in every FHE scheme covered in this book, treated as essentially free relative to multiplication: thousands of additions can be chained before the noise budget is meaningfully dented.

7.3 Noise Growth Under Multiplication: Quadratic, Then Compounding

Multiplication is structurally different, and understanding exactly why is the single most important piece of intuition in this chapter. Chapter 8 will derive the precise BFV multiplication formula; here we extract the shape of the result, which is scheme-independent.

Multiplying two ciphertexts means forming, conceptually, the product of two degree-1 expressions in $s$: $$(c_0+c_1 s)(c_0'+c_1's) = c_0c_0' + (c_0c_1'+c_1c_0')s + c_1c_1's^2.$$ Substituting the decryption relations $c_0+c_1s = \Delta m + v$ and $c_0'+c_1's=\Delta m'+v'$, this product equals $$\Delta^2 m m' + \Delta(mv' + m'v) + vv'.$$ Two things happen here that never happen under addition. First, the result is now a quadratic function of $s$ (there is an $s^2$ term) — this is the origin of relinearization (§7.5, worked in full in Chapter 8). Second, and more consequentially for this chapter, look at the noise term after the rescaling that recovers the correct plaintext scale (dividing through by $\Delta$, Chapter 8's §8.3): the dominant surviving noise contribution scales like $m v' + m' v$, i.e. proportional to the message magnitude times the noise, plus a genuinely quadratic $vv'$ cross-term and additional rounding noise introduced by the rescaling division itself. Certain

Result 7.1 — Multiplicative noise growth (informal)

For RLWE-based schemes with ring dimension $n$ and plaintext modulus $t$, one homomorphic multiplication multiplies the operands' noise bound by a factor on the order of $O(t \cdot n)$ (or $O(t\sqrt n)$ under typical average-case heuristics), dominated by the plaintext-scaled cross terms and the rounding noise from rescaling — as opposed to addition's factor of $O(1)$. Certain, general shape; exact constants are scheme- and analysis-specific — see Fan–Vercauteren (2012) and Brakerski–Gentry–Vaikuntanathan (2012) for the precise bounds.

The consequence compounds. If a fresh ciphertext has noise bound $B_0$ and each multiplication multiplies the bound by a factor $\kappa = O(tn)$, then after $d$ sequential multiplications (a depth-$d$ multiplicative chain, as opposed to $d$ independent multiplications of fresh ciphertexts), the noise bound grows like $$B_d \approx \kappa^d B_0,$$ i.e. exponential in circuit depth if nothing intervenes. Contrast a full binary multiplication tree of $2^d$ leaves reduced to one product via $d$ sequential levels: at level $i$, both operands already carry noise from level $i-1$, so the growth compounds across levels rather than resetting. This exponential-in-depth blowup — not the depth itself — is the real threat, and it is what every noise-management technique in this chapter exists to defeat.

7.4 Formalizing the Noise Budget

Definition 7.1 — Noise budget

For a ciphertext $\mathsf{ct}$ with decryption relation $c_0+c_1s \equiv \Delta m + v \pmod q$ under plaintext modulus $t$ (so $\Delta \approx q/t$), decryption is correct iff $\|v\|_\infty < \Delta/2$ (up to a small scheme-specific correction term). Define the noise budget, in bits, as $$\mathrm{nb}(\mathsf{ct}) \;=\; \log_2\!\left(\frac{\Delta}{2\,\|v\|_\infty}\right) \;\approx\; \log_2 q - \log_2 t - \log_2\bigl(2\|v\|_\infty\bigr).$$ A freshly encrypted ciphertext has budget $\mathrm{nb}_0 = \log_2 q - \log_2 t - \log_2(2B_0) \gg 0$. Decryption fails once $\mathrm{nb}(\mathsf{ct}) \le 0$, i.e. once $\|v\|_\infty \ge \Delta/2$. Certain — this is the standard formalization used by, e.g., Microsoft SEAL's own noise-budget instrumentation.

Definition 7.1 turns §7.3's exponential noise growth into a linear decay of a bit-budget: each multiplication consumes roughly $\log_2\kappa = \log_2(tn)$ bits of budget (a large, roughly constant chunk per level), while each addition consumes at most $1$ bit (halving the remaining "headroom" in the absolute worst case, negligible in practice). A depth-$d$ multiplicative circuit is decryptable iff $$\mathrm{nb}_0 \;-\; d \cdot \log_2(tn) \;>\; 0 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \log_2 q \;>\; \log_2 t + \log_2(2B_0) + d\log_2(tn).$$

Corollary 7.1 — Why $q$ must grow exponentially in $d$, absent intervention

Be careful to separate two different growth models here, because conflating them makes the whole "exponential becomes linear" narrative of this chapter collapse into a tautology.

The bound $\kappa^d B_0$ used just above already presumes per-level maintenance: it assumes each multiplication multiplies the noise by a fixed factor $\kappa \sim tn$, which is only true if the operands are returned to a bounded noise level before every multiply. Under that assumption the requirement is $\log_2 q = O(d\log(tn))$ — bit-length linear in $d$. That is the conclusion of §7.5's strategies, not the baseline they improve upon.

The genuine unmanaged baseline is worse, and worse in kind. Multiplication multiplies noise by noise: two ciphertexts carrying noise $E$ produce one carrying roughly $\gamma E^2$ with $\gamma \sim tn$. Iterating that squaring gives $$E_d \;\approx\; \frac{(\gamma E_0)^{2^d}}{\gamma},$$ so $\log_2 E_d$ grows like $2^d$. Requiring $E_d < q/(2t)$ therefore forces $$\log_2 q \;=\; \Omega\!\left(2^{d}\right),$$ i.e. the bit-length of $q$ is exponential in $d$ and $q$ itself is doubly exponential. At $d = 20$ that is a modulus of astronomical size — the reason "just enlarge $q$" is not a strategy at all.

So the arc of Part II is: unmanaged noise squaring forces $\log_2 q = \Omega(2^d)$; the three strategies of §7.5 — BGV's modulus switching, BFV's scale-invariance, CKKS's rescaling — each pin the operand noise back to a bounded level before every multiplication, converting the squaring into a constant per-level factor and the requirement into $\log_2 q = O(d)$. That is the exponential-to-linear collapse, and it is what makes leveled FHE practical at all. Certain — this is the motivating problem BGV12 and BFV solve.

An exponentially growing $q$ is catastrophic for two independent reasons. First, ciphertext size and every arithmetic operation on $R_q$ scale with $\log q$ (polynomial coefficients are $\log_2 q$-bit integers), so computation cost grows at least linearly, and in practice worse, with $\log q$ — an exponential-in-$d$ $q$ makes even modest depths (say $d=20$) computationally infeasible. Second, and more insidiously, LWE/RLWE hardness depends on the ratio $q/\sigma$ (modulus to noise magnitude, Chapter 4): for fixed security you cannot grow $q$ without also growing the lattice dimension $n$ to compensate, so an exponential $q$ forces an exponential $n$, which is doubly disastrous for performance. This is precisely the wall that made Gentry's original 2009 scheme impractical, and precisely the wall the second-generation schemes of §6.5 were built to route around.

7.5 Three Strategies for Defeating Exponential Noise Growth

All three strategies below share one structural idea — periodically shrink something so that the noise-to-signal ratio doesn't compound multiplicatively across levels — but they shrink different things, at different points in the computation, which is exactly what makes BGV, BFV, and CKKS feel different to program against even though they share an RLWE skeleton.

7.5.1 Modulus switching (BGV)
