BGV's approach (worked in full in Chapter 9): after a multiplication, homomorphically transform the ciphertext from modulus $q_i$ to a smaller modulus $q_{i-1}$, choosing the transformation so that the plaintext is preserved exactly while the noise term scales down by approximately the same factor $q_{i-1}/q_i$ as the modulus itself. The key insight is that this operation deliberately trades away some of the modulus (which you were going to need less of anyway, since the ciphertext's remaining useful lifetime is shorter) in exchange for proportionally reducing the noise — pinning the absolute noise back near its fresh level so that the next multiplication squares a small quantity rather than an inflated one. Note carefully what is not claimed: the noise-to-modulus ratio is not restored by this operation. It degrades by a fixed number of bits at each level (§9.3), which is precisely why the chain is finite and why bootstrapping eventually becomes necessary; what modulus switching removes is the doubly-exponential compounding, not the per-level cost. Each level of the chain $q_L > q_{L-1} > \cdots > q_0$ absorbs the noise growth from one multiplication; a depth-$L$ circuit needs a chain of $L{+}1$ moduli, and $\log_2 q_L = O(L)$ rather than $O(2^L)$ — modulus switching converts Corollary 7.1's exponential requirement into a linear one. Certain

7.5.2 Scale invariance (BFV)

BFV's approach (Chapter 8) sidesteps the modulus chain altogether: fix a single modulus $q$ for the ciphertext's entire lifetime, and instead build the scaling factor $\Delta = \lfloor q/t\rfloor$ into every operation so that multiplication's natural rescaling step (dividing the tensor product by $\Delta$ and rounding, §8.3) does the same noise-control job that modulus switching does in BGV — but as an intrinsic part of the multiplication routine rather than a separate maintenance call on a shrinking chain of moduli. "Scale-invariant" refers to the fact that the relationship between plaintext and ciphertext scale ($\Delta$) is preserved by design across multiplications, rather than being renegotiated at each level via a change of modulus. Both strategies achieve the same asymptotic goal — noise growth linear (not exponential) in depth — via different mechanics, and Chapter 8 §8.3 makes BFV's version precise.

7.5.3 Rescaling (CKKS)

CKKS (Chapter 10) faces a related but distinct problem: it encodes real/complex numbers as $\lfloor \Delta x \rceil$ for a scale $\Delta$, and multiplying two CKKS ciphertexts multiplies their scales, $\Delta \cdot \Delta = \Delta^2$. Left unmanaged, the scale itself explodes exponentially in depth — a problem that looks like BGV/BFV's noise problem but is really about tracking the plaintext's implicit scale, not (only) its noise. CKKS's rescale operation divides the ciphertext by one factor of $\Delta$ and simultaneously drops to the next modulus in a BGV-style chain, restoring the scale to $\approx\Delta$ while consuming one level — CKKS's rescaling is, mechanically, modulus switching borrowed from BGV, but applied for a second purpose (scale management) on top of noise management. This dual role is why CKKS ciphertexts carry both a level and a scale, and why mismatched scales/levels (not just exhausted noise budget) are the dominant source of CKKS bugs — a theme Chapter 10 and Chapter 16 return to at length. Certain

StrategySchemeWhat shrinks, and whenExplicit user action?
Modulus switchingBGVCiphertext modulus $q_i \to q_{i-1}$, after each multiplyYes (or automatic, depending on library scaling technique — Chapter 9)
Scale invarianceBFVBuilt into the multiplication routine itself; single $q$ throughoutNo separate call required for correctness
RescalingCKKSCiphertext modulus and scale, after each multiplyYes — explicit rescale call in SEAL/OpenFHE

7.6 Multiplicative Depth Is the Question

Everything in this chapter compresses to one operational rule. Additions are close to free; multiplications are the resource being budgeted; and it is specifically sequential multiplications — multiplicative depth, not the total count of multiplication gates — that determines how much noise budget (equivalently, how large a modulus chain, equivalently how expensive the parameters) a circuit requires. A circuit that multiplies a million pairs of ciphertexts in parallel and sums the results (depth 1) is cheap. A circuit that multiplies 20 ciphertexts sequentially — $((((c_1 \cdot c_2)\cdot c_3)\cdots)\cdot c_{20})$, depth 19 — can require dramatically larger parameters, even though it has far fewer multiplication gates than the first example.

Practical consequence: depth drives every parameter decision

Before writing a line of FHE code, determine your circuit's multiplicative depth. A degree-2 polynomial (one multiplication) needs a tiny modulus chain. A depth-10 neural network layer stack, or a naive polynomial approximation of an activation function evaluated to high degree, needs a much larger one — bigger $q$, bigger $n$ for equivalent security, slower everything. This single number should be the first thing you compute about any new FHE workload, well before choosing a scheme or a library. Certain

This is also precisely why bootstrapping (Chapter 12) exists as a separate, expensive operation rather than something you reach for by default: bootstrapping refreshes the noise budget, converting a leveled scheme's hard depth ceiling into a soft, repeatable cost — but at a price (often orders of magnitude more expensive than a single multiplication) that makes "just raise the depth parameter" the right first move for any circuit whose depth is known and bounded in advance.

SEAL is leveled-only

Microsoft SEAL implements BFV, BGV and CKKS as leveled schemes and does not implement Gentry-style bootstrapping for any of them. Certain, verified (BGV has been present since SEAL v4.0.0, which added it via PR 283; v4.1.0 further sped it up by keeping BGV ciphertexts in NTT form — so older statements that "SEAL has no BGV" are out of date.) This means the coefficient modulus chain you choose at context-creation time is a hard ceiling on multiplicative depth for that program — there is no "refresh and keep going" escape hatch inside SEAL itself. If a workload's depth exceeds what a reasonable parameter set can support, the options are: restructure the circuit to reduce depth (Chapters 18–19's polynomial-approximation techniques), decrypt-and-re-encrypt outside the library at a trust boundary (defeats the purpose in most threat models), or switch to a library that implements bootstrapping for the scheme in question — OpenFHE and HEAAN both implement CKKS bootstrapping Certain, verified, which is one of the most common reasons production CKKS pipelines choose OpenFHE over SEAL despite SEAL's simpler API.

Artifact 7.1 — Instrumenting noise growth in the Chapter 5 RLWE SHE

The cleanest way to make §7.3's exponential-growth claim concrete is to instrument the toy RLWE SHE scheme from Chapter 5 and watch the noise budget fall in real time. The following extends Chapter 5's reference implementation with a noise-measurement hook and drives it to the decryption cliff.

import numpy as np

def noise_budget(ct, sk, q, t):
    """Decrypt without rounding to recover the raw noise term v, then
    report the budget in bits per Definition 7.1."""
    c0, c1 = ct
    raw = poly_add(c0, poly_mul_mod(c1, sk, q), q)   # c0 + c1*s mod q, centered
    raw_centered = center_mod(raw, q)                      # represent in (-q/2, q/2]
    delta = q // t
    # v = raw - delta * round(raw / delta); this is the residual noise polynomial
    m_est = np.round(raw_centered / delta)
    v = raw_centered - delta * m_est
    noise_inf = np.max(np.abs(v))
    return np.log2(delta / (2.0 * max(noise_inf, 1e-9)))

# Drive a multiplicative chain and log the budget after every level:
sk, pk, relin_key = she_keygen(n=4096, q=2**60, t=1024)
ct = she_encrypt(pk, m=np.array([3]))

budgets = [noise_budget(ct, sk, q=2**60, t=1024)]
for level in range(1, 15):
    ct = she_mult(ct, she_encrypt(pk, m=np.array([2])), relin_key)
    budgets.append(noise_budget(ct, sk, q=2**60, t=1024))
    print(f"after {level} multiplications: {budgets[-1]:.1f} bits of noise budget remaining")
    if budgets[-1] <= 0:
        print("  -- decryption will now fail; this is the cliff")
        break

Running this against Chapter 5's toy parameters ($n{=}4096$, $\log_2 q = 60$, $t{=}1024$) produces a budget that starts around 40–45 bits and drops by roughly $\log_2(tn) \approx \log_2(1024 \cdot 4096) = 22$ bits per multiplication — matching Result 7.1's predicted per-level cost — so correctness survives only one or two multiplications before the budget goes negative and decrypted output stops matching $3\cdot2^{\text{level}}$. Plotting budgets against level number produces the canonical "noise cliff" curve: a roughly linear decline in bits followed by an abrupt wrong-answer regime once the budget crosses zero, which is the direct experimental confirmation of Corollary 7.1 and the reason every practical scheme in Chapters 8–10 exists.

Key Takeaways
  • LWE/RLWE noise is not a flaw — it is the hardness source. Every homomorphic operation grows it; every scheme design is a strategy for managing that growth.
  • Addition grows noise additively/linearly (cheap); multiplication grows it by a factor of roughly $O(tn)$ per level, compounding to $O((tn)^d)$ — exponential — over a depth-$d$ chain if unmanaged.
  • The noise budget $\mathrm{nb}(\mathsf{ct}) = \log_2(\Delta/2\|v\|_\infty)$ falls by a roughly constant number of bits per multiplication; decryption fails once it reaches zero.
  • Left unmanaged, supporting depth $d$ requires $q = 2^{\Omega(d)}$ — the reason "just pick a bigger modulus" does not scale.
  • Modulus switching (BGV), scale-invariance (BFV), and rescaling (CKKS) are three distinct mechanisms that all convert this exponential requirement into a linear one; they differ in what they shrink and when.
  • Multiplicative depth, not total gate count, is the parameter that governs cost — determine it before choosing parameters or a library.
  • SEAL implements no bootstrapping for BFV or CKKS: its coefficient modulus chain is a hard depth ceiling. OpenFHE and HEAAN implement CKKS bootstrapping when depth exceeds what leveled parameters can support.
Chapter 8

BFV — Exact Arithmetic, Scale-Invariant

Learning Objectives
  • Construct the BFV plaintext-to-ciphertext scaling $\Delta = \lfloor q/t \rfloor$ and explain why it makes the scheme "scale-invariant"
  • Write out BFV key generation, encryption, and decryption precisely, over $R_q = \mathbb{Z}_q[X]/(X^n+1)$
  • Derive homomorphic addition and show its noise growth is additive
  • Derive homomorphic multiplication via tensoring, rescale-and-round, and relinearization, and show why its noise growth is the dominant cost
  • Set up a leveled BFV coefficient modulus chain in Microsoft SEAL and reason about parameter selection for a target circuit

BFV is the scheme most people mean when they say "I need exact homomorphic integer arithmetic and I don't want to think about a modulus chain." This chapter derives it from the ring-LWE assumption up, in enough detail that the "scale-invariant" claim from Chapter 7 stops being a slogan and becomes an equation you can check.

8.1 Setup and Notation

Fix $n$ a power of two and the cyclotomic ring $R = \mathbb{Z}[X]/(X^n+1)$. For an integer modulus $m$, write $R_m = R/mR$, i.e. polynomials in $R$ with coefficients reduced mod $m$ (represented, by convention, in the balanced interval $(-m/2, m/2]$). Fix two moduli: the ciphertext modulus $q$ (large) and the plaintext modulus $t$ (small), with $t \ll q$. The plaintext space is $R_t$; the ciphertext space is $R_q \times R_q$.

Definition 8.1 — The scaling factor $\Delta$

$$\Delta \;=\; \left\lfloor \frac{q}{t} \right\rfloor.$$ BFV embeds a plaintext $m \in R_t$ into $R_q$ via $\Delta \cdot m$ before encryption. Because $\Delta$ is a single fixed constant determined only by $(q,t)$ — not recomputed or renegotiated at each homomorphic operation — the encoding is called scale-invariant: the ratio between "one unit of plaintext" and "one unit of ciphertext" never changes across the ciphertext's lifetime, in contrast to CKKS, where the analogous scale $\Delta$ actively changes (via rescaling) as a bookkeeping mechanism (Chapter 7 §7.5.3, Chapter 10). Certain

Error distributions: let $\chi$ denote the error distribution used throughout (typically a discrete Gaussian or a bounded/ternary distribution, per Chapter 4's RLWE hardness assumption), sampling small polynomials in $R$ — coefficients concentrated near $0$, with a known bound $B$ on $\|\cdot\|_\infty$ except with negligible probability.

8.2 Key Generation, Encryption, Decryption

Definition 8.2 — BFV key generation

Sample secret key $s \leftarrow \chi$ (a "small" ring element — typically ternary, coefficients in $\{-1,0,1\}$). Sample $a \leftarrow R_q$ uniformly and $e \leftarrow \chi$. Set $$pk = (b, a), \qquad b = -(a s + e) \bmod q.$$ $sk = s$ is kept private; $pk = (b,a)$ is published. (This is exactly an RLWE sample under $s$, per Chapter 5 — public-key security reduces directly to RLWE hardness.)

Definition 8.3 — BFV encryption

To encrypt $m \in R_t$: sample $u \leftarrow \chi$ and $e_1, e_2 \leftarrow \chi$ independently. Output $$\mathsf{ct} = (c_0, c_1), \qquad c_0 = \Delta m + b u + e_1 \bmod q, \qquad c_1 = a u + e_2 \bmod q.$$

Proposition 8.1 — BFV decryption relation

For $\mathsf{ct}=(c_0,c_1)$ as in Definition 8.3, $$c_0 + c_1 s \;\equiv\; \Delta m + v \pmod q, \qquad v = e_1 + e_2 s - u e.$$

Proof. $$c_0+c_1s = \Delta m + bu + e_1 + (au+e_2)s = \Delta m + u(b+as) + e_1 + e_2s.$$ Since $b = -(as+e) \bmod q$, we have $b+as \equiv -e \pmod q$, so $$c_0+c_1s \equiv \Delta m + u(-e) + e_1+e_2s = \Delta m + (e_1+e_2s-ue) \pmod q. \qquad\blacksquare$$

The noise term $v = e_1+e_2s-ue$ is a sum/product of small polynomials, hence itself small: $\|v\|_\infty \le B_0$ for a bound $B_0$ that depends on $n$, the error bound $B$, and the number of terms — computable exactly but not illuminating to belabor here (see Fan–Vercauteren 2012, Lemma 1, for the precise bound). Certain

Definition 8.4 — BFV decryption

$$\mathsf{Dec}(sk, \mathsf{ct}) \;=\; \left\lfloor \frac{t}{q}\bigl[(c_0+c_1s) \bmod q\bigr] \right\rceil \bmod t.$$

Proposition 8.2 — Correctness

If $\|v\|_\infty < \Delta/2 - O(1)$ (a small additive correction from $q$ not dividing $t$ exactly, since $\Delta = \lfloor q/t\rfloor$), then $\mathsf{Dec}(sk,\mathsf{ct}) = m$.

Proof sketch. By Proposition 8.1, $(c_0+c_1s)\bmod q = \Delta m + v$ (working in the centered representative, assuming no wraparound — guaranteed by $\Delta m$ and $v$ both being $\ll q$). Then $\frac{t}{q}(\Delta m+v) = \frac{t\Delta}{q}m + \frac{t}{q}v$. Since $\Delta = \lfloor q/t\rfloor$, $\frac{t\Delta}{q} \approx 1$ (exactly $1$ up to an $O(t/q)$ correction), so this is $\approx m + \frac{t}{q}v$. Rounding recovers $m$ exactly provided the perturbation $\frac{t}{q}v$ has magnitude less than $1/2$, i.e. $\|v\|_\infty < \frac{q}{2t} \approx \Delta/2$. $\blacksquare$

Proposition 8.2 is the formal version of Chapter 7's Definition 7.1: $\mathrm{nb}(\mathsf{ct}) = \log_2(\Delta/2\|v\|_\infty) > 0$ is exactly the condition above.

8.3 Homomorphic Addition

Given $\mathsf{ct}=(c_0,c_1)$ encrypting $m$ with noise $v$ and $\mathsf{ct}'=(c_0',c_1')$ encrypting $m'$ with noise $v'$ (both under the same $\Delta, q, t$), define $$\mathsf{ct}_{\mathrm{add}} = (c_0+c_0' \bmod q,\ c_1+c_1' \bmod q).$$ By linearity of Proposition 8.1's relation, $$(c_0+c_0')+(c_1+c_1')s \equiv \Delta(m+m') + (v+v') \pmod q,$$ so $\mathsf{ct}_{\mathrm{add}}$ decrypts to $m+m' \bmod t$ (provided $\|v+v'\|_\infty$ stays under the correctness threshold), with noise $v_{\mathrm{add}} = v+v'$, $\|v_{\mathrm{add}}\|_\infty \le \|v\|_\infty+\|v'\|_\infty$ — precisely §7.2's additive growth, now derived rather than asserted. Plaintext addition (needed to combine an encrypted value with a known constant) is the degenerate case $\mathsf{ct}+ \Delta \cdot m_{\text{const}}$ added into $c_0$ alone, with no noise growth at all.

8.4 Homomorphic Multiplication

This is BFV's central, and most delicate, construction — the concrete instance of §7.3's general derivation.

8.4.1 Tensoring

Given $\mathsf{ct}=(c_0,c_1)$, $\mathsf{ct}'=(c_0',c_1')$, form the three coefficient polynomials of the symbolic product $(c_0+c_1X)(c_0'+c_1'X)$, computed over $\mathbb{Z}$ (not reduced mod $q$ — or reduced mod a larger auxiliary modulus in practice, to bound coefficient growth): $$d_0 = c_0c_0', \qquad d_1 = c_0c_1'+c_1c_0', \qquad d_2 = c_1c_1'.$$ By construction, $$d_0 + d_1 s + d_2 s^2 = (c_0+c_1s)(c_0'+c_1's) \equiv (\Delta m+v)(\Delta m'+v') \pmod{q} \;\text{(mod appropriate lift)}$$ $$= \Delta^2 mm' + \Delta(mv'+m'v) + vv'.$$ This relation lives at scale $\Delta^2$, not $\Delta$ — it is over-scaled by exactly one factor of $\Delta = q/t$, i.e. it is effectively a relation modulo $q^2/t$ rather than $q$.

8.4.2 Rescale-and-round

To bring the result back to the correct $\Delta$-scale (and back into $R_q$ rather than a doubled modulus), multiply each $d_i$ by $t/q$ and round to the nearest integer, coefficient-wise: $$\hat d_i = \left\lfloor \frac{t}{q}\, d_i \right\rceil \bmod q, \qquad i=0,1,2.$$ This is the direct BFV analogue of Chapter 7's "divide out the extra scale factor" step. One can show (Fan–Vercauteren 2012, §3.2) that $$\hat d_0 + \hat d_1 s + \hat d_2 s^2 \;\equiv\; \Delta\, (m m' \bmod t) \;+\; v_{\mathrm{mult}} \pmod q,$$ where $v_{\mathrm{mult}}$ collects: the rescaled cross-noise terms $\frac{t}{q}\Delta(mv'+m'v) \approx (mv'+m'v)$, the rescaled quadratic noise term $\frac{t}{q}vv'$, and new rounding noise from the three $\lfloor\cdot\rceil$ operations in $\hat d_i$, each contributing an $O(1)$-bounded-per-coefficient error that becomes an $O(n)$ or $O(\sqrt n)$ term after multiplication by $s, s^2$ (coefficient accumulation across a degree-$n$ polynomial product). The dominant term is the message-scaled cross-noise $(mv'+m'v)$, which is exactly Result 7.1's $O(t\cdot n)$-per-multiplication growth once $m,m'$ are taken up to their full range in $R_t$ (magnitude up to $\sim t/2$ per coefficient) and the $O(n)$ coefficient-accumulation factor from the ring multiplication is included. Certain, general shape and standard reference; exact constants per Fan–Vercauteren (2012).

8.4.3 Relinearization

$(\hat d_0, \hat d_1, \hat d_2)$ is a valid ciphertext in the sense that it decrypts correctly under the relation $\hat d_0+\hat d_1 s+\hat d_2 s^2$ — but it is a three-component object, quadratic in $s$, not the two-component linear form every other operation in this chapter expects. Left alone, a second multiplication would tensor this three-term object against another, producing a five-term (degree-4-in-$s$) result, and so on — ciphertext size growing linearly with multiplicative depth, which is untenable. Relinearization folds $\hat d_2 s^2$ back into a linear function of $s$ using a public relinearization key.

Definition 8.5 — Relinearization key (gadget-decomposition form)

Fix a decomposition base $w$ and let $\ell = \lceil \log_w q\rceil$. For $i=0,\ldots,\ell-1$, sample $a_i \leftarrow R_q$, $e_i \leftarrow \chi$, and set $$\mathsf{rlk}_i = \bigl(-(a_i s+e_i) + w^i s^2 \bmod q,\ a_i\bigr).$$ Each $\mathsf{rlk}_i$ is (up to the added $w^i s^2$ term) an RLWE encryption of $0$ under $s$ — i.e. $\mathsf{rlk}$ is an encryption of $s^2$, "digit-decomposed" across base $w$ so that later combination introduces only bounded, controllable extra noise rather than one large-noise encryption of $s^2$.

To relinearize: write $\hat d_2 = \sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1} w^i \hat d_2^{(i)}$ in base-$w$ digits (each digit small, $\|\hat d_2^{(i)}\|_\infty < w$), then compute $$(c_0'', c_1'') = (\hat d_0, \hat d_1) + \sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1} \hat d_2^{(i)} \cdot \mathsf{rlk}_i,$$ where $\hat d_2^{(i)} \cdot \mathsf{rlk}_i$ is understood component-wise. One checks this new two-component ciphertext $(c_0'', c_1'')$ satisfies $c_0''+c_1''s \equiv \hat d_0+\hat d_1 s+\hat d_2s^2 + v_{\mathrm{relin}} \pmod q$ for a small additional noise term $v_{\mathrm{relin}}$ controlled by the base $w$: smaller $w$ (more digits $\ell$) means smaller per-digit noise contribution but more terms in the sum and a larger public key; larger $w$ is the opposite trade. This base-$w$ (or, in modern RNS implementations, base-prime) decomposition is the same gadget-decomposition idea used for key switching generally, including CKKS relinearization and rotation key-switching in later chapters. Certain

Result 8.1 — BFV multiplication, end to end

Tensor $\to$ rescale-and-round $\to$ relinearize takes two two-component ciphertexts encrypting $m, m'$ at noise level $\|v\|,\|v'\| \le B$ and produces one two-component ciphertext encrypting $mm' \bmod t$ at noise level $\|v_{\mathrm{mult}}\| = O(t n B) + O(w n \ell)$ — dominated in typical parameter regimes by the first term, matching Chapter 7's Result 7.1. Certain, general shape

8.5 Leveled BFV in Microsoft SEAL (v4.4)

SEAL's BFV implementation follows Definitions 8.1–8.5 essentially verbatim, using RNS (Residue Number System) representation internally for performance — the single large $q$ is represented as a product of many smaller word-sized primes $q = q_1 q_2 \cdots q_k$, and all arithmetic is done in parallel modulo each $q_i$ via CRT, which is what CoeffModulus::Create is choosing when you hand it a list of bit-sizes. This does not change the mathematics of §8.1–8.4; it changes only how $R_q$ arithmetic is implemented.

A note on "leveled BFV"

BFV's scale-invariant design (§8.1) means it does not structurally require a shrinking modulus chain the way BGV does (Chapter 9) — a single $q$, held fixed, already gives linear-in-depth noise growth per §8.4. SEAL nonetheless exposes an optional mod_switch_to_next operation for BFV ciphertexts, primarily to shrink ciphertext size and per-operation cost partway through a computation once some of the original modulus "headroom" is no longer needed for the remaining circuit — an optimization, not a correctness requirement, in contrast to BGV where modulus switching is the noise-control mechanism itself. Likely — reflects BFV/SEAL's documented design intent; verify exact current behavior of mod_switch_to_next for BFV against the SEAL manual/examples for the version in use.

Artifact 8.1 — BFV integer arithmetic in SEAL (C++ skeleton)

The following mirrors the standard structure of SEAL's own bundled examples (native/examples/1_bfv_basics.cpp, 2_encoders.cpp, 3_levels.cpp). Signatures below are written as a skeleton — verify exact method names and overloads against the SEAL v4.4 header files / bundled examples before compiling, per this book's no-hallucination policy on volatile library APIs.

#include "seal/seal.h"
using namespace seal;

int main() {
    // -- 1. Parameters: ring dimension, coefficient modulus chain, plaintext modulus --
    EncryptionParameters parms(scheme_type::bfv);
    size_t poly_modulus_degree = 8192;
    parms.set_poly_modulus_degree(poly_modulus_degree);

    // A chain of primes; total bit-length sets both security level (via poly_modulus_degree)
    // and, per Ch.7, the multiplicative depth budget.
    parms.set_coeff_modulus(CoeffModulus::BFVDefault(poly_modulus_degree));

    // Plaintext modulus chosen to support batching (Ch.13): a prime t ≡ 1 (mod 2n).
    parms.set_plain_modulus(PlainModulus::Batching(poly_modulus_degree, 20));

    SEALContext context(parms);

    // -- 2. Key generation --
    KeyGenerator keygen(context);
    SecretKey secret_key = keygen.secret_key();
    PublicKey public_key;
    keygen.create_public_key(public_key);
    RelinKeys relin_keys;
    keygen.create_relin_keys(relin_keys);   // realizes Definition 8.5

    Encryptor encryptor(context, public_key);
    Evaluator evaluator(context);
    Decryptor decryptor(context, secret_key);
    BatchEncoder batch_encoder(context);    // packs a vector of integers mod t into one plaintext

    // -- 3. Encode + encrypt two integer vectors --
    std::vector v1(batch_encoder.slot_count(), 0), v2(batch_encoder.slot_count(), 0);
    v1[0] = 7;  v2[0] = 6;    // toy: compute 7*6 + 7 homomorphically in slot 0

    Plaintext p1, p2;
    batch_encoder.encode(v1, p1);
    batch_encoder.encode(v2, p2);

    Ciphertext c1, c2;
    encryptor.encrypt(p1, c1);
    encryptor.encrypt(p2, c2);

    // -- 4. Homomorphic multiply, then relinearize (Definition 8.5 / Result 8.1) --
    Ciphertext c_prod;
    evaluator.multiply(c1, c2, c_prod);
    evaluator.relinearize_inplace(c_prod, relin_keys);

    // -- 5. Homomorphic add of the original c1 (7*6 + 7), noise grows only additively (§8.3) --
    Ciphertext c_result;
    evaluator.add(c_prod, c1, c_result);

    // -- 6. Inspect remaining noise budget (Definition 7.1) before decrypting --
    std::cout << "noise budget: "
              << decryptor.invariant_noise_budget(c_result) << " bits\n";

    Plaintext p_result;
    decryptor.decrypt(c_result, p_result);
    std::vector v_result;
    batch_encoder.decode(p_result, v_result);
    std::cout << "result[0] = " << v_result[0] << " (expected 49)\n";
    return 0;
}

Two things to run and observe, connecting straight back to Chapter 7: call decryptor.invariant_noise_budget(...) after each step (encryption, after multiply, after relinearize, after add) and watch it fall by a large, roughly-constant chunk at multiply, essentially not at all at add — a live confirmation of §8.3 vs. §8.4. Then deliberately choose a smaller coeff_modulus (fewer/smaller primes in the chain) and re-run: past some point the multiplication step will drive the budget negative and v_result[0] will no longer equal 49 — the noise cliff from Chapter 7's Artifact 7.1, now inside a real, standards-track library rather than a toy implementation.

Key Takeaways
  • BFV scales plaintexts into the ciphertext ring by a single fixed constant $\Delta=\lfloor q/t\rfloor$, computed once and never renegotiated — this is what "scale-invariant" means precisely.
  • Decryption is $\lfloor (t/q)(c_0+c_1s \bmod q)\rceil \bmod t$; correctness requires the noise $\|v\|_\infty < \Delta/2$ (roughly), matching Chapter 7's noise-budget formalism exactly.
  • Homomorphic addition is component-wise with additive noise growth: $v_{\mathrm{add}}=v+v'$.
  • Homomorphic multiplication is tensor $\to$ rescale-and-round $\to$ relinearize: tensoring produces a 3-component, quadratic-in-$s$ ciphertext at scale $\Delta^2$; rescaling divides out one $\Delta$; relinearization uses a gadget-decomposed evaluation key to fold the $s^2$ term back into a 2-component ciphertext.
  • Multiplicative noise growth is dominated by message-scaled cross-noise terms, on the order of $O(tn)$ per multiplication — the concrete instance of Chapter 7's Result 7.1.
  • SEAL implements BFV with an RNS-represented coefficient modulus chain and an optional (not correctness-critical) mod_switch_to_next; the chain's total size is chosen to cover the circuit's multiplicative depth, per Chapter 7's central rule.
Chapter 9

BGV — Exact Arithmetic, Modulus Switching

Learning Objectives
  • Contrast BGV's "message in the bottom bits" plaintext embedding with BFV's scaled embedding
  • Derive the modulus-switching operation and show it scales noise down proportionally to the modulus
  • Construct the BGV modulus chain $q_L>\cdots>q_0$ and explain how each level absorbs one multiplication
  • Compare BGV and BFV on noise-management philosophy, API ergonomics, and typical parameter footprint
  • Set up leveled BGV parameters and basic operations in OpenFHE v1.5.1

BGV was, historically, first: Brakerski, Gentry, and Vaikuntanathan's 2012 paper is the one that coined "leveled fully homomorphic encryption" and made it a practical design target (§6.5.2). Where BFV bakes noise control into the multiplication routine itself, BGV makes it an explicit, separately-invoked operation — modulus switching — applied along a pre-planned chain of moduli. This chapter derives that mechanism and then puts it side-by-side with Chapter 8's BFV construction, since the two schemes are, in a precise sense, dual approaches to the identical problem.

9.1 Plaintext Embedding: Bottom Bits, Not Scaled Bits

BGV and BFV share the same plaintext space, $R_t$, and the same ring $R_q = \mathbb{Z}_q[X]/(X^n+1)$. The difference is where the message lives inside a ciphertext's decryption relation.

Definition 9.1 — BGV decryption relation

A BGV ciphertext $\mathsf{ct}=(c_0,c_1)$ under modulus $q$ satisfies $$c_0 + c_1 s \;\equiv\; m + t\cdot e \pmod q, \qquad m \in R_t,\ \|e\|_\infty \text{ small.}$$ Decryption is $\mathsf{Dec}(sk,\mathsf{ct}) = \bigl[(c_0+c_1s)\bmod q\bigr] \bmod t$. Certain

Compare Definition 8.1/Proposition 8.1: BFV writes the relation as $\Delta m + v$, with the message occupying the high-order bits (scaled up by $\Delta \approx q/t$) and noise occupying the low-order bits below $\Delta/2$. BGV instead writes $m + te$: the message occupies the low-order bits directly (no scaling — $m$ is literally $m$, mod $t$), and the noise is confined to being a multiple of $t$, living in the bits above $m$'s residue but reduced away by the final $\bmod\ t$ in decryption. This is why BGV's message is often described as living "in the bottom bits mod $t$": reducing $c_0+c_1s \bmod q$ and then $\bmod t$ simply throws away everything that is a multiple of $t$ (the noise $te$), leaving $m$ exactly, provided the true integer value of $(c_0+c_1s \bmod q)$, taken in its centered representative, has magnitude small enough that the $\bmod q$ reduction did not wrap around the true (unreduced) $m+te$. Concretely, correctness requires $\|m+te\|_\infty < q/2$, i.e. $t\|e\|_\infty \lesssim q/2$ — the direct BGV analogue of BFV's $\|v\|_\infty < \Delta/2$, differing only in where the scaling factor $t$ appears. Certain

Both encodings are valid and both give exact integer arithmetic mod $t$; the choice is purely about how noise is subsequently managed, which is the entire remaining content of this chapter.

9.2 Homomorphic Addition and Multiplication (Before Switching)

Addition is, as in BFV, component-wise and noise-additive: for $\mathsf{ct},\mathsf{ct}'$ with noise $e,e'$, $$\mathsf{ct}_{\mathrm{add}} = (c_0+c_0',\,c_1+c_1') \;\Rightarrow\; c_0''+c_1''s \equiv (m+m') + t(e+e') \pmod q,$$ noise $e_{\mathrm{add}}=e+e'$, additive growth exactly as §8.3 and Chapter 7 §7.2.

Multiplication follows the same tensor-then-relinearize shape as §8.4 — form $d_0=c_0c_0'$, $d_1=c_0c_1'+c_1c_0'$, $d_2=c_1c_1'$ and check $$d_0+d_1s+d_2s^2 \equiv (m+te)(m'+te') = mm' + t(me'+m'e) + t^2ee' \pmod q,$$ which is already at the correct scale for BGV — note there is no $\Delta^2$ over-scaling here and hence no rescale-and-round division step analogous to §8.4.2, because BGV never scaled up by $\Delta$ in the first place. Relinearization proceeds exactly as in Definition 8.5, folding $d_2s^2$ back to a linear ciphertext $(c_0'',c_1'')$ via a gadget-decomposed evaluation key. The resulting noise is $t(me'+m'e)+t^2ee' + v_{\mathrm{relin}}$ — and this is the point at which BGV and BFV genuinely diverge, so the common claim that per-multiplication growth is "the same in both schemes" is worth resisting.

Look at the $t^2ee'$ term. In BFV the multiplication routine ends with a rescale by $t/q$, which drags that quadratic cross term down to $O((t/q)\,ee')$ — negligible — leaving a growth factor of roughly $tn$ that is independent of how much noise the operands already carried. BGV has no such division: it never scaled up by $\Delta$, so there is nothing to divide back out, and $t^2ee'$ survives intact. The pre-switch BGV growth factor is therefore proportional to the operands' current noise, on the order of $t n E$ rather than a constant $tn$.

That distinction is not a technicality — it is the entire reason BGV needs modulus switching as a mandatory per-level step rather than an optional optimisation. A factor proportional to accumulated noise is exactly the noise-squaring recurrence of Corollary 7.1, which drives $\log_2 q$ toward $\Omega(2^d)$. Modulus switching after every multiplication is what pins $E$ back down and converts that squaring into the constant-factor behaviour BFV gets for free from its rescale. BFV can skip modulus switching and remain merely leveled; BGV cannot. Certain

What is scheme-dependent is what happens next: BFV's noise, once produced, simply sits there until the next multiplication compounds it further (§7.3's exponential-if-unmanaged growth). BGV instead intervenes immediately.

9.3 Modulus Switching

Definition 9.2 — Modulus switching

Given $\mathsf{ct}=(c_0,c_1) \in R_q^2$ and a smaller modulus $q' < q$ with $q \equiv q' \pmod{t}$ (or more precisely, chosen so the following rounding preserves the residue mod $t$), define the switched ciphertext $\mathsf{ct}' = (c_0',c_1')$ by rounding each coefficient of $\frac{q'}{q}c_i$ to the nearest integer congruent to $c_i \bmod t$: $$c_i' \;=\; \left\lfloor \frac{q'}{q} c_i \right\rceil_{t}, \qquad i=0,1,$$ where $\lfloor\cdot\rceil_t$ denotes rounding to the nearest integer in the correct residue class mod $t$ (not simply the nearest integer), which is the detail that makes the message survive the transformation exactly rather than merely approximately. Certain

Proposition 9.1 — Effect of modulus switching on noise

If $\mathsf{ct}$ decrypts correctly under $s$ at modulus $q$ with noise $e$ (i.e. $c_0+c_1s \equiv m+te \pmod q$), then $\mathsf{ct}'$ decrypts correctly under the same $s$ at modulus $q'$, to the same message $m$, with new noise $$e' \;\approx\; \frac{q'}{q}\,e \;+\; e_{\mathrm{round}},$$ where $e_{\mathrm{round}}$ is a small rounding-induced term (bounded independent of $e$, coming from the $\lfloor\cdot\rceil_t$ step, roughly on the order of $O(n)$ or $O(\sqrt n)\cdot\|s\|$ per the specific analysis in Brakerski–Gentry–Vaikuntanathan (2012)). Certain, general shape

The content of Proposition 9.1 is exactly what §7.5.1 promised: the noise scales down by the same factor $q'/q$ that the modulus itself shrinks by, up to a small additive rounding term. This is the entire trick — but it is worth being precise about what the trick does and does not buy, because it is easy to overclaim.

What modulus switching restores is the absolute noise magnitude, not the noise-to-modulus ratio. A multiplication grows noise by a factor $\kappa = O(tn)$ (§9.2); immediately switching from $q_i$ down to $q_{i-1} \approx q_i/\kappa$ divides that noise back down by approximately the same $\kappa$, returning the ciphertext to an absolute noise level comparable to a fresh one. The ratio, however, does not improve: both numerator and denominator were divided by $\kappa$, so the noise-to-modulus ratio after switching is the same as it was immediately after the multiplication — that is, a factor $\kappa$ worse than before the multiplication. Equivalently, the noise budget of Definition 7.1 falls by about $\log_2\kappa$ bits at every level, and that expenditure is permanent. It could not be otherwise: if switching genuinely restored the budget, a leveled scheme could evaluate unbounded depth without a modulus chain and bootstrapping would never have been necessary.

The payoff is subtler and more important. Because multiplication multiplies noise by noise, letting noise accumulate would square it at every level: $B \to B^2 \to B^4 \to \cdots \to B^{2^d}$, doubly exponential in depth, which is precisely Corollary 7.1's requirement that $\log_2 q$ grow like $O(2^d)$. Rescaling after every multiplication keeps the operands' absolute noise pinned near the fresh level $B$, so each multiplication squares a small quantity instead of an already-inflated one, and the per-level cost collapses to a fixed additive $\log_2\kappa$ bits. Do this once per multiplication along a pre-chosen chain and a depth-$d$ circuit needs a chain of $d{+}1$ moduli each of roughly fixed bit-length, i.e. $\log_2 q_L = O(d)$ — Corollary 7.1's exponential requirement converted to linear, exactly as claimed in Chapter 7.

9.4 The Modulus Chain

Definition 9.3 — BGV modulus chain

Fix a chain of moduli $q_L > q_{L-1} > \cdots > q_1 > q_0$ (typically each a product of the previous one by one additional small prime, in RNS form, so that switching from $q_i$ to $q_{i-1}$ amounts to dropping one prime factor). A freshly encrypted ciphertext lives at the top, $q_L$. Each homomorphic multiplication is immediately followed by a modulus switch to the next level down: after $j$ multiplications (along the longest chain of dependencies to that ciphertext), the ciphertext lives at $q_{L-j}$. The circuit's multiplicative depth budget is exactly $L$: once a ciphertext reaches $q_0$, no further multiplication (which would require switching to $q_{-1}$, undefined) is possible without bootstrapping. Certain

This is the precise mechanical referent of "leveled" in "leveled FHE": $L$ is chosen once, at parameter-generation time, to match (or exceed) the target circuit's multiplicative depth, and the chain's total bit-length $\log_2 q_L = \sum_{i=0}^L \log_2(q_i/q_{i-1})$ is — by Proposition 9.1's design — linear in $L$, each increment covering one level's worth of multiplicative noise growth plus a fixed security margin.

9.5 BGV vs. BFV: The Comparison Chapter 7 Promised

PropertyBFV (Ch. 8)BGV (Ch. 9)
Plaintext embeddingScaled: $\Delta m$, message in high bitsUnscaled: $m$, message in low bits mod $t$, noise as a multiple of $t$ above it
Noise-control mechanismBuilt into the multiply routine (rescale-and-round by $t/q$)Separate, explicit modulus-switch operation invoked (by the user or automatically) after each multiply
Ciphertext modulus over timeStructurally can stay fixed at a single $q$ (though SEAL offers optional switching for efficiency)Must decrease along a pre-planned chain — the chain length is the depth budget
Per-multiplication noise growth (dominant term)$O(tn)$, same shape$O(tn)$, same shape
Parameter planningChoose one large $q$ covering total depthChoose a chain whose total size covers total depth; each level's prime size tunable independently
API ergonomics (typical library)Fewer explicit maintenance calls — multiply, relinearize, doneRequires an explicit or library-automated mod-switch/mod-reduce call after each multiply
Common library choiceSEAL, OpenFHEHElib (native home of BGV), OpenFHE, SEAL (since v4.0)

The two schemes are best understood not as competitors with a clear winner but as two implementations of the same theorem: Chapter 7's Corollary 7.1 says exponential $q$-growth is avoidable, and both BFV's scale-invariance and BGV's modulus-switching are valid proofs of that avoidability, differing in engineering ergonomics rather than asymptotic power. In practice, the deciding factors are usually: which library you're already using (OpenFHE supports both natively; SEAL supports only BFV among the two); whether your workload benefits from BGV's per-level tunable prime sizes (useful when different parts of a circuit have very different noise-growth profiles); and team familiarity. Neither is uniformly faster or smaller — parameter tuning matters more than the scheme choice between these two specifically. Likely — reflects general community consensus and current library support patterns; concrete performance comparisons are workload- and version-dependent and should be benchmarked, not assumed.

9.6 BGV in OpenFHE (v1.5.1)

OpenFHE is, as of this writing, the primary actively-maintained production library implementing BGV alongside BFV and CKKS in one codebase (Chapter 15 surveys the library landscape in full). The BGV-specific entry point is CryptoContextBGVRNS, configured through the same CCParams<...> pattern OpenFHE uses for its other schemes.

Artifact 9.1 — The Chapter 8 computation, reproduced in OpenFHE BGV

The following reproduces Artifact 8.1's toy computation ($7 \times 6 + 7$) using BGV instead of BFV, to make the API-level and parameter-level comparison concrete. As with Artifact 8.1, this is a structural skeleton — verify exact class/method names against the OpenFHE v1.5.1 headers and bundled src/pke/examples/ before compiling, since parameter-object field names in particular have moved across OpenFHE releases.

#include "openfhe.h"
using namespace lbcrypto;

int main() {
    // -- 1. Parameters: multiplicative depth drives the modulus chain length (Definition 9.3) --
    CCParams parameters;
    parameters.SetMultiplicativeDepth(2);      // one multiply + one add in our toy circuit; pad for safety
    parameters.SetPlaintextModulus(65537);    // t: a prime supporting batching (Ch.13)
    parameters.SetScalingModSize(50);         // bit-size of each prime in the switching chain, per level
    // ScalingTechnique controls whether mod-switching after multiply is automatic or manual --
    // verify current default and available options (e.g. FIXEDMANUAL vs FIXEDAUTO) for BGV
    // against the current OpenFHE docs before relying on either behavior.

    CryptoContext cc = GenCryptoContext(parameters);
    cc->Enable(PKE);
    cc->Enable(KEYSWITCH);
    cc->Enable(LEVELEDSHE);

    // -- 2. Key generation --
    KeyPair keys = cc->KeyGen();
    cc->EvalMultKeyGen(keys.secretKey);     // relinearization key, realizes Definition 8.5's construction over BGV

    // -- 3. Encode + encrypt --
    std::vector v1 = {7, 0, 0, 0};
    std::vector v2 = {6, 0, 0, 0};
    Plaintext p1 = cc->MakePackedPlaintext(v1);
    Plaintext p2 = cc->MakePackedPlaintext(v2);

    auto c1 = cc->Encrypt(keys.publicKey, p1);
    auto c2 = cc->Encrypt(keys.publicKey, p2);

    // -- 4. Homomorphic multiply (tensor + relinearize, §9.2); modulus switch applied
    //    per the chosen ScalingTechnique -- automatically, or via an explicit ModReduce call --
    auto c_prod = cc->EvalMult(c1, c2);

    // -- 5. Homomorphic add (§9.2, additive noise growth) --
    auto c_result = cc->EvalAdd(c_prod, c1);

    // -- 6. Decrypt --
    Plaintext p_result;
    cc->Decrypt(keys.secretKey, c_result, &p_result);
    p_result->SetLength(1);
    std::cout << "result[0] = " << p_result << " (expected 49)\n";
    return 0;
}

Comparing parameter footprints (Ch. 8 vs. Ch. 9, illustrative): for a matched shallow circuit (multiplicative depth 1–2, 128-bit security target), expect BFV-in-SEAL and BGV-in-OpenFHE to land in the same order of magnitude for ring dimension ($n=8192$ is typical at this depth in both) and total coefficient-modulus bit-length, because both are solving the same noise-vs-security equation (Chapter 7) with mechanisms of matched asymptotic efficiency. Do not treat any specific bit-count as a verified constant across library versions — the right exercise is to run Artifact 8.1 and Artifact 9.1 side by side at the same target depth and security level, log the actual coeff_modulus/ScalingModSize totals each library selects, and compare directly; that measured comparison is more trustworthy than any number printed in a book. Likely — order-of-magnitude claim; treat any specific figure as illustrative unless you have measured it yourself against the exact library versions in use.

Automatic vs. manual modulus switching

Both major BGV-capable libraries expose a choice about whether modulus switching after multiplication happens automatically inside EvalMult/multiply, or must be invoked explicitly by the caller (historically closer to HElib's original design, where the user calls something like a modulus-reduce step by hand). Which behavior is the current default, and what the relevant configuration flag is named, has changed across OpenFHE releases — verify against the current documentation/examples for the exact version pinned in your project before assuming either automatic or manual behavior; getting this wrong silently produces a ciphertext that has grown one relinearized-but-not-switched level larger than intended, which will not fail loudly until noise budget is later exhausted. Verify

Key Takeaways
  • BGV writes its decryption relation as $m+te \pmod q$ — the message in the low-order bits mod $t$, noise as an explicit multiple of $t$ above it — as opposed to BFV's $\Delta m + v$ scaled-up embedding.
  • Modulus switching from $q$ to $q'$ (Definition 9.2) preserves the message exactly (via careful rounding to the correct residue class mod $t$) while scaling the noise down by approximately the same factor $q'/q$ the modulus shrinks by.
  • Performing one modulus switch immediately after each multiplication converts §7.3's exponential-in-depth noise growth into linear growth, at the cost of a pre-planned chain $q_L>\cdots>q_0$ whose length $L$ is the circuit's fixed depth budget.
  • BGV and BFV solve the identical problem (Chapter 7's Corollary 7.1) with different mechanisms — one an explicit maintenance operation, one baked into multiplication — and have comparable asymptotic parameter costs; the practical choice between them is usually driven by library support and API ergonomics rather than a fundamental efficiency gap.
  • OpenFHE implements BGV via CryptoContextBGVRNS with the same SetMultiplicativeDepth-driven parameter model used for its BFV and CKKS contexts; whether modulus switching after EvalMult is automatic or requires an explicit call is a version-dependent detail to verify, not assume.
  • The most reliable way to compare BFV and BGV parameter footprints for a real workload is to run both at matched depth and security level and read off what each library actually selects, not to rely on any single quoted number.
Chapter 10

CKKS — Approximate Arithmetic, the ML Workhorse

Learning Objectives
  • Articulate the conceptual break CKKS makes with exact schemes: noise is folded into the arithmetic itself, not merely tolerated.
  • Derive the CKKS encoding map from the canonical embedding introduced in Chapter 2, and explain the role of the scale $\Delta$.
  • State precisely what encryption, decryption, addition, and multiplication do to the encoded message and to the scale.
  • Master the rescaling discipline — the single engineering habit that separates working CKKS code from silent garbage.
  • Compare how TenSEAL, SEAL, and OpenFHE expose (or hide) rescaling, and know which one to reach for at which stage of your own work.
  • Trace CKKS back to its authors and its home institution, CryptoLab, and understand why that provenance matters for reading the literature.

Every scheme in Part II so far — BFV in Chapter 8, BGV in Chapter 9 — has been in the business of hiding noise from the message. The plaintext lives in the low-order bits or a separate CRT channel; noise lives in the high-order bits; decryption is a rounding operation that throws the noise away and recovers the message exactly. CKKS, introduced by Cheon, Kim, Kim, and Song in their 2017 Asiacrypt paper "Homomorphic Encryption for Arithmetic of Approximate Numbers," breaks this separation on purpose. It does not hide noise from the message — it makes noise part of the message. Certain

This is the single conceptual leap you must make to use CKKS correctly, and it is worth stating before any equation: in CKKS, the decrypted value is not $x$, it is $x + e$ for some small $e$ that depends on encryption noise, encoding rounding, and — critically — every rescaling operation your circuit performs. CKKS gives up exactness in exchange for encrypting real and complex numbers directly, at a cost structure (SIMD packing, native multiplication) competitive with BFV/BGV. For machine learning, where weights are floats, activations are floats, and a model that is accurate to $2^{-20}$ relative error is indistinguishable from an exact one, this trade is not a compromise — it is the correct tool. That is why CKKS is the scheme underneath essentially every FHE inference pipeline you will find in production today: TenSEAL, OpenFHE's CKKS backend, HEAAN, and the CKKS mode of most vendor SDKs.

10.1 From Exact Rings to Approximate Reals

Recall the RLWE setting from Chapter 2: ciphertexts live in $R_q = \mathbb{Z}_q[X]/(X^N+1)$, a ring of dimension $N$ (a power of two). BFV and BGV encode a message vector into this ring via CRT-batching (Chapter 9), which requires the plaintext modulus $t$ to split appropriately and only ever represents integers mod $t$. CKKS instead asks: can we encode a vector of $N/2$ complex numbers into a ring element such that ring addition and multiplication approximate coordinatewise addition and multiplication of the complex vector? The answer is yes, via the same canonical embedding you met in Chapter 2 — but now run in reverse, and combined with a fixed-point scaling trick.

10.1.1 The Canonical Embedding, Revisited

The canonical embedding $\sigma: \mathbb{R}[X]/(X^N+1) \to \mathbb{C}^N$ evaluates a polynomial $m(X)$ at the $N$ primitive $2N$-th roots of unity $\zeta^j$, $j$ odd, $0 \le j < 2N$, where $\zeta = e^{\pi i / N}$. Because $m(X)$ has real coefficients, these evaluations come in $N/2$ complex-conjugate pairs. This means the image of $\sigma$ restricted to real polynomials is exactly the conjugate-symmetric subspace $\mathbb{H} \subset \mathbb{C}^N$, a real vector space of dimension $N$ — but parameterized by only $N/2$ free complex numbers (the rest are forced by conjugate symmetry). Certain

Be careful with the indexing, because two conventions are in circulation and they pair different slots. If you label slots $z_0,\dots,z_{N-1}$ by increasing odd exponent ($\zeta^1, \zeta^3, \dots, \zeta^{2N-1}$), the symmetry is $$z_j = \overline{z_{N-1-j}},$$ pairing first-with-last: $z_0$ with $z_{N-1}$, $z_1$ with $z_{N-2}$, and so on. (Check it for $N=8$: the exponents are $1,3,5,7,9,11,13,15$, and since $\zeta^{16}=1$ we have $\zeta^{15}=\overline{\zeta^{1}}$, so slot 0 pairs with slot 7.) If instead you index by the odd exponent itself over $\mathbb{Z}^*_{2N}$, as the original CKKS paper does, the same statement reads $z_{-j} = \overline{z_j}$, i.e. $z_{2N-j} = \overline{z_j}$. Writing $z_j = \overline{z_{N-j}}$ under the first convention is simply wrong — it would pair $z_1$ with $z_7$ rather than $z_6$ at $N=8$.

This is exactly the slack CKKS exploits. Given a target vector of $N/2$ complex plaintext values $\mathbf{z} = (z_1, \ldots, z_{N/2}) \in \mathbb{C}^{N/2}$, extend it to a conjugate-symmetric vector in $\mathbb{H}$ by appending $\overline{z_i}$, then pull it back through $\sigma^{-1}$ to obtain a real polynomial. Since $\sigma$ is a ring isomorphism onto its image, coordinatewise complex multiplication of the $z_i$ corresponds — up to the conjugate-symmetric embedding — to polynomial multiplication mod $X^N+1$. This is precisely the SIMD structure Chapter 13 will exploit for batching: one ciphertext, $N/2$ independent complex lanes, one polynomial multiplication operates on all lanes simultaneously.

10.1.2 Discretization: the Scale $\Delta$

$\sigma^{-1}(\mathbf{z})$ is a polynomial with real (generally irrational) coefficients. RLWE ciphertexts need integer polynomial coefficients in $R_q$. CKKS bridges this with a fixed-point encoding: choose a scale factor $\Delta$ (typically $2^{40}$ to $2^{60}$), compute

$$m(X) = \left\lfloor \Delta \cdot \sigma^{-1}(\mathbf{z}) \right\rceil \in R,$$

rounding each coefficient to the nearest integer, then reduce mod $q$ to place $m(X)$ in $R_q$. The rounding error introduced here is bounded by roughly $1/2$ per coefficient in the canonical-embedding norm, which translates to a relative encoding error of order $1/\Delta$ per plaintext slot. This is the price of admission for approximate arithmetic: even before encryption, you have already lost some precision. Certain

Definition 10.1 — CKKS Encoding

Given a slot vector $\mathbf{z} \in \mathbb{C}^{N/2}$ and scale $\Delta \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$, the CKKS encoding is $$\mathsf{Encode}_\Delta(\mathbf{z}) = \left\lfloor \Delta \cdot \sigma^{-1}(\mathbf{z}) \right\rceil \bmod q \; \in R_q.$$ Decoding inverts this: $\mathsf{Decode}_\Delta(m) = \sigma(m) / \Delta \in \mathbb{C}^{N/2}$ (discarding the conjugate half).

10.2 Encryption and the Arithmetic of Approximate Ciphertexts

Once the message is an element $m \in R_q$, CKKS encryption is ordinary RLWE encryption exactly as in Chapter 2/8: sample $\mathbf{a} \leftarrow R_q$ uniformly, small error $e \leftarrow \chi$, and output $\mathsf{ct} = (b, a) = (-a \cdot s + e + m, \; a)$ under secret $s$. There is nothing scheme-specific about CKKS encryption itself — the entire CKKS machinery lives in the encoding map and in how the evaluator tracks scale through homomorphic operations. Certain

Decryption computes $m' = b + a \cdot s = m + e \pmod q$, then decoding divides by $\Delta$:

$$\mathsf{Decode}_\Delta(m') = \sigma(m)/\Delta + \sigma(e)/\Delta \approx \mathbf{z} + \frac{\sigma(e)}{\Delta}.$$

This is the headline fact of the chapter, stated precisely: CKKS decryption never returns exactly $\mathbf{z}$; it returns $\mathbf{z}$ plus an error term that is the encryption/computation noise divided by the scale. Making $\Delta$ large relative to the noise budget shrinks this term — that is the entire precision knob in CKKS, and it trades directly against the number of levels available (larger $\Delta$ eats more of the coefficient modulus $q$ per level).

10.2.1 Homomorphic Addition

Adding two ciphertexts encrypted under the same scale $\Delta$ is coordinatewise ciphertext addition, exactly as in any RLWE scheme: $\mathsf{ct}_1 + \mathsf{ct}_2$ decrypts (up to noise) to $m_1 + m_2$, which decodes to $\mathbf{z}_1 + \mathbf{z}_2$ still at scale $\Delta$. Addition is cheap in noise growth, as always — the interesting part of CKKS is multiplication.

10.2.2 Homomorphic Multiplication and the Scale Explosion

Multiplying $\mathsf{ct}_1$ (encrypting $m_1$ at scale $\Delta_1$) by $\mathsf{ct}_2$ (encrypting $m_2$ at scale $\Delta_2$), using the standard RLWE ciphertext-multiplication-then-relinearize procedure from Chapter 2, yields a ciphertext encrypting $m_1 \cdot m_2$ — which decodes to $\mathbf{z}_1 \odot \mathbf{z}_2$ (Hadamard/elementwise product) at scale $\Delta_1 \cdot \Delta_2$. Certain

Fact 10.1 — Scale Multiplies, It Does Not Add

If both operands carry scale $\Delta$, the product carries scale $\Delta^2$. If left unmanaged across a circuit of multiplicative depth $L$, the scale grows to $\Delta^{2^L}$ (or $\Delta^{L+1}$ depending on the topology) within a handful of multiplications, quickly exceeding any usable modulus $q$. This is not a corner case — it is the default behavior of raw ciphertext multiplication, and it is why CKKS cannot be used "like BFV with floats" without an explicit corrective step.

10.3 The Rescaling Discipline

The corrective step is rescaling. After a multiplication produces a ciphertext at scale $\Delta^2$ (encrypting message $m_1 m_2$ modulo the current coefficient modulus $q_\ell$), rescaling divides the ciphertext coefficients by (approximately) $\Delta$ and simultaneously drops the ciphertext to the next smaller modulus $q_{\ell-1}$ in the RNS chain:

$$\mathsf{ct}' = \left\lfloor \frac{q_{\ell-1}}{q_\ell} \cdot \mathsf{ct} \right\rceil \bmod q_{\ell-1}.$$

In the standard RNS-CKKS parameterization, the moduli $q_\ell$ are chosen so that $q_\ell / q_{\ell-1} \approx \Delta$, making rescaling simultaneously (a) a division that returns the scale to approximately $\Delta$, restoring precision headroom, and (b) a modulus drop that consumes exactly one level from the multiplicative-depth budget set up at parameter-generation time (mirroring the "levels are the budget" framing from Chapter 9's leveled-BGV discussion). Certain This dual nature — rescale is precision management and level consumption in a single call — is what makes CKKS engineering feel different from BFV/BGV, where noise and levels are managed by separate mechanisms (modulus switching vs. plaintext modulus).

The #1 Source of CKKS Bugs: Scale/Level Mismatch

Two ciphertexts can only be added or multiplied together if they carry the same scale and the same level (i.e., they are defined modulo the same $q_\ell$). This is not a performance suggestion — libraries either raise a hard error or, worse, silently produce garbage decryption if you violate it.

Concretely, the mismatch arises in two common patterns:

  • Depth-imbalanced circuits. If one branch of your computation graph does two multiplications before merging with a branch that did only one (e.g., $a \cdot b \cdot c + d$ where $d$ was never multiplied), the branches are at different levels and different scales when you try to add them. You must manually rescale/level-align the shallower branch down (or use OpenFHE/SEAL's explicit modulus-switch-without-multiply calls) before combining.
  • Constant multiplication without rescale. Multiplying a ciphertext by a plaintext constant (e.g., scaling by a learned weight) still multiplies the scale by $\Delta$ if the constant is CKKS-encoded at scale $\Delta$. Forgetting to rescale afterward is the most common single line-of-code bug in CKKS ML pipelines.

The practical discipline, regardless of library: after every ciphertext-ciphertext or ciphertext-plaintext multiplication, rescale before doing anything else with that ciphertext, and track level/scale bookkeeping as carefully as you track tensor shapes in ordinary ML code. Certain

10.3.1 Consequences for Circuit Design

Because rescaling both fixes precision and drains the level budget, CKKS circuit design is a genuine co-optimization between numerical accuracy and multiplicative depth — the same tension you will meet again in Chapter 12 on bootstrapping (bootstrap to refresh levels) and throughout Part III on encrypted ML (polynomial-approximate activations to bound depth). A rectangular circuit — every branch multiplies the same number of times before merging — is far easier to manage than a ragged one, which is why production CKKS pipelines (including OpenFHE-based ViT/CNN inference stacks) go to real effort to balance multiplicative depth across branches, inserting no-op rescales or level drops purely to keep operands aligned.

10.4 CKKS Across Libraries: Hidden, Explicit, and Semi-Automated

The three libraries most FHE practitioners touch expose rescaling at three different points on the automation spectrum. Knowing which regime you are in changes how you debug.

10.4.1 TenSEAL v0.3.16 — Rescale Hidden

TenSEAL (OpenMined's Python wrapper around Microsoft SEAL) exposes a high-level `CKKSVector`/`CKKSTensor` API in which arithmetic operators (`+`, `*`) automatically relinearize and rescale internally after each multiplication. Certain This is excellent for building intuition fast — you write `y = x * x + x` and it works — but it means the scale/level bookkeeping described above is happening for you, invisibly. The pedagogical cost: you can use CKKS correctly in TenSEAL without ever internalizing why a deep circuit eventually fails when the level budget (set via `coeff_mod_bit_sizes`) runs out. Push past TenSEAL to SEAL or OpenFHE once the conceptual model above is solid — you need to see the rescale calls to debug real pipelines.

10.4.2 Microsoft SEAL v4.4 — Rescale Explicit

SEAL's CKKS evaluator requires you to call `relinearize_inplace` and `rescale_to_next_inplace` yourself after every multiplication, and to manually align scales/levels (via `mod_switch_to_inplace`) before combining ciphertexts from unbalanced branches. Likely — verify exact call names against the current `native/examples/4_ckks_basics.cpp` This is the "see every rescale" regime: painful at first, but it is where the discipline in the warning box above becomes muscle memory. SEAL has no bootstrapping (a fact we return to in Chapter 12), so in SEAL the level budget set at parameter generation is a hard, non-negotiable ceiling — there is no escape hatch if you mis-budget depth.

10.4.3 OpenFHE v1.5.1 — Explicit with Selective Automation

OpenFHE's CKKS (`CryptoContextCKKSRNS`) sits between the two: you still call `EvalMult`, and rescaling is exposed via explicit context flags — OpenFHE supports both a manual mode and an "automatic rescaling" mode (`ScalingTechnique::FLEXIBLEAUTO` or similar) that rescales after every multiply for you, while still requiring you to reason about level budget through `SetMultiplicativeDepth` at parameter time. Likely — verify exact enum/flag names against current OpenFHE examples, they have moved across releases OpenFHE is also the library in this trio with native CKKS bootstrapping (Chapter 12) and general functional bootstrapping as of v1.4, so it is the natural home once your circuit's depth requirement exceeds what a leveled parameter set can support.

LibraryRescaleBootstrappingBest for
TenSEAL v0.3.16Automatic, hiddenNo (SEAL-based)Fast intuition, prototyping
SEAL v4.4Fully manualNoLearning the discipline by hand
OpenFHE v1.5.1Manual or auto-flaggedYes (incl. functional)Production, deep circuits

10.5 Provenance: CryptoLab and the Original CKKS Authors

CKKS is named for its four authors — Jung Hee Cheon, Andrey Kim, Miran Kim, and Yongsoo Song — whose 2017 paper originated the scheme. Cheon is also a co-founder of CryptoLab, a Seoul, South Korea-based company built around commercializing CKKS-based HE, and the original reference implementation, HEAAN ("HE for Arithmetic of Approximate Numbers"), is maintained by the SNU cryptography lab associated with this group. Certain HEAAN is worth reading — not shipping — specifically because it is the closest thing to source-of-truth CKKS: when a downstream library's rescaling semantics or bootstrapping precision claims seem surprising, HEAAN's implementation and the original paper are the tiebreaker. OpenFHE's CKKS RNS variant and its bootstrapping pipeline (Chapter 12) build directly on follow-on work by this lineage and by the Cheon-Han-Kim-Kim-Song RNS-CKKS and bootstrapping papers.

10.6 A Numeric Feel for the Precision Budget

It is worth working through the arithmetic of a precision budget once by hand, because "the scale is a knob" is an empty statement until you see what it costs. Suppose a coefficient modulus chain split as $\{60, 40, 40, 40, 40, 40, 40, 60\}$ — six "inner" 40-bit primes plus two 60-bit primes bookending the chain, for a total of $60 + 6\times 40 + 60 = 360$ bits (a typical SEAL/OpenFHE convention: the bottom prime sets the final decryption precision floor, the top prime is oversized to absorb the initial encoding/encryption error, and each inner prime is consumed by one rescale). With $\Delta = 2^{40}$ matched to the inner primes, this parameter set supports exactly six multiplications (six rescales, one per inner prime) before the chain is exhausted. Likely — exact bit-length conventions vary by library defaults and by the security-parameter table (poly_modulus_degree, target security level) in use; treat this as an illustrative, not universal, split

Each rescale removes a factor of $\approx \Delta$ from both the scale and the modulus, so after $k$ rescales the ciphertext carries scale $\approx \Delta$ still (by design — that is what rescaling restores), but the remaining modulus has shrunk from 360 bits to roughly $360 - 40k - 60$ bits (accounting for the reserved top prime), leaving less room for noise to be distinguishable from signal. This is why circuit designers do not simply ask "how many multiplications does my circuit need" but "how many multiplications, at what scale, leaving how many bits of final precision" — a three-way negotiation between depth, $\Delta$, and target accuracy that has no free variable. Doubling $\Delta$ (say from $2^{40}$ to $2^{80}$, split across two inner primes per level) roughly halves the number of multiplications the same total modulus budget supports, while proportionally improving per-multiplication precision. There is no way to get both more depth and more precision from a fixed modulus budget — you are always trading one against the other, exactly as levels-vs-precision trades against levels-vs-bootstrap-frequency in Chapter 12.

10.6.1 CKKS vs. BFV/BGV: A Precision Contrast Worth Naming

It is easy to conflate "CKKS has noise" with "BFV/BGV don't" — both have encryption noise (Chapter 2), and both consume it via multiplication. The difference is what happens to the noise relative to the message at decryption time. BFV/BGV place the message in a separate algebraic channel from the noise (the message lives mod $t$, the noise lives in the "extra room" between $t$ and $q$) and decryption performs an operation — divide-and-round by $q/t$ — that exactly zeroes the noise contribution provided it stayed below a threshold; the output is bit-exact or the scheme has failed outright (garbage, detectable as such). CKKS deliberately never performs that separating step: the scale $\Delta$ plays a structurally similar role to $t$, but decryption's division by $\Delta$ does not zero the noise, it merely shrinks it proportionally. The result is always "close," never structurally guaranteed exact, and there is no failure mode that announces itself — a CKKS computation that has drifted too far from correct simply returns a plausible-looking, wrong float. This is the deepest reason CKKS pipelines need independent numerical validation (comparing against a plaintext reference computation, as both artifacts below do) in a way BFV/BGV pipelines, once correctness is established, typically do not.

10.7 Worked Artifact: Encrypted Polynomial Evaluation, Two Ways

The following two skeletons evaluate the same toy circuit, $f(x) = ax^2 + bx + c$, on an encrypted float vector — once in TenSEAL (rescale hidden), once in OpenFHE (rescale explicit) — and measure the decryption error against the plaintext computation. This is the smallest circuit that already exhibits the full rescale/level lifecycle: one multiplication (for $x^2$), one plaintext-ciphertext multiplication (for $a \cdot x^2$ and $b \cdot x$), and an addition of differently-scaled branches that must be reconciled before summing.

Artifact 10.1 — TenSEAL: $ax^2+bx+c$ and Measured Error [Python, stable API]
import tenseal as ts
import numpy as np

# Parameters: poly_modulus_degree sets N (slots = N/2); coeff_mod_bit_sizes
# sets the RNS chain -- inner entries ~ how many multiplies (levels) survive.
ctx = ts.context(
    ts.SCHEME_TYPE.CKKS,
    poly_modulus_degree=8192,
    coeff_mod_bit_sizes=[60, 40, 40, 60],   # 2 inner levels -> depth budget ~2 mults
)
ctx.global_scale = 2 ** 40
ctx.generate_galois_keys()
ctx.generate_relin_keys()

a, b, c = 0.5, -1.2, 3.0
x_plain = np.linspace(-2.0, 2.0, 4096)          # fits in N/2 = 4096 slots
x_enc = ts.ckks_vector(ctx, x_plain.tolist())

# One multiplicative level: x*x. TenSEAL relinearizes + rescales internally here.
y_enc = a * (x_enc * x_enc) + b * x_enc + c

y_dec = np.array(y_enc.decrypt())
y_true = a * x_plain**2 + b * x_plain + c

err = np.abs(y_dec - y_true)
print("max abs error:", err.max())               # expect ~1e-3 to 1e-5 range
print("mean abs error:", err.mean())

Verified pattern — this TenSEAL usage matches the library's stable public API. Exact error magnitudes depend on `global_scale`, `poly_modulus_degree`, and input range; always measure empirically rather than assuming a fixed error bound.

Artifact 10.2 — OpenFHE: Same Circuit, Explicit Rescaling [C++, SKELETON — verify against current openfhe-development CKKS examples]
// SKELETON -- confirm exact API against src/pke/examples/simple-real-numbers.cpp
// and advanced-real-numbers.cpp in the current openfhe-development repo before compiling.

#include "openfhe.h"
using namespace lbcrypto;

CCParams<CryptoContextCKKSRNS> parameters;
parameters.SetMultiplicativeDepth(2);      // one mult for x*x, one for a*(x*x) -- budget it explicitly
parameters.SetScalingModSize(50);          // ~ bits per level, analogous to Delta's bit-length
parameters.SetBatchSize(4096);

auto cc = GenCryptoContext(parameters);
cc->Enable(PKE);
cc->Enable(KEYSWITCH);
cc->Enable(LEVELEDSHE);

auto keys = cc->KeyGen();
cc->EvalMultKeyGen(keys.secretKey);        // relinearization key

std::vector<double> xVals = /* linspace(-2, 2, 4096) */;
Plaintext ptX = cc->MakeCKKSPackedPlaintext(xVals);
auto ctX = cc->Encrypt(keys.publicKey, ptX);

double a = 0.5, b = -1.2, c = 3.0;

// x * x  -> scale becomes Delta^2, one level consumed at multiplication;
// explicit rescale brings scale back down and drops the RNS chain by one modulus.
auto ctXX = cc->EvalMult(ctX, ctX);
ctXX = cc->Rescale(ctXX);                  // EXPLICIT -- the step TenSEAL hides

// a * (x*x): plaintext-ciphertext multiply also needs a rescale.
auto ctAXX = cc->EvalMult(ctXX, a);
ctAXX = cc->Rescale(ctAXX);

auto ctBX = cc->EvalMult(ctX, b);
ctBX = cc->Rescale(ctBX);

// ctAXX is now at a DIFFERENT LEVEL than ctBX and the constant c
// (ctAXX went through two rescales, ctBX through one). Before adding,
// they must be level-aligned -- e.g. via EvalAddMany's internal alignment,
// or explicit level-reduce calls on the shallower branches. This exact
// alignment step is the crux of Section 10.3's warning box, made concrete.
auto result = cc->EvalAdd(cc->EvalAdd(ctAXX, ctBX), c);

Plaintext resultPt;
cc->Decrypt(keys.secretKey, result, &resultPt);
resultPt->SetLength(4096);
auto decoded = resultPt->GetRealPackedValue();
// Compare decoded[i] against a*x[i]^2 + b*x[i] + c, same error measurement as Artifact 10.1.

Verify before compiling — the level-alignment call (whether `EvalAdd` auto-aligns levels in current OpenFHE or requires explicit `LevelReduce`/`AdjustLevelsAndDepthToOne`) has changed across OpenFHE releases; check `src/pke/examples/` in the version you are pinned to before trusting this skeleton verbatim.

Running both artifacts side by side is the fastest way to internalize Section 10.3: the TenSEAL version "just works," while the OpenFHE version forces you to name every rescale and confront the level-alignment step the moment your circuit branches. If you only ever run the TenSEAL version, you will not be equipped to debug a mismatched-scale error the first time you meet one in production code — which is precisely why the CKKS pipelines referenced throughout this book (e.g., encrypted CNN/ViT inference in Part III) are built on OpenFHE rather than TenSEAL.

Key Takeaways
  • CKKS treats the decrypted value as $x + e$, not $x$ — noise is part of the output, not something decryption removes. This is correct and sufficient for ML, wrong for anything requiring bit-exact results.
  • Encoding uses the canonical embedding (Chapter 2) run in reverse, scaled by $\Delta$: $\mathsf{Encode}_\Delta(\mathbf{z}) = \lfloor \Delta \cdot \sigma^{-1}(\mathbf{z}) \rceil$.
  • Multiplication multiplies scales ($\Delta_1 \Delta_2$), not adds them — left unmanaged, scale explodes within a few multiplications.
  • Rescaling is the corrective operation: it divides out $\Delta$ and drops one level from the RNS chain, simultaneously. This dual role — precision restoration and depth consumption — is CKKS's defining engineering fact.
  • Operands must share scale and level before combining. This mismatch is the number one source of CKKS bugs.
  • TenSEAL hides rescale (fast intuition, poor debugging training), SEAL exposes it fully (no bootstrapping, hard depth ceiling), OpenFHE exposes it with optional automation and native bootstrapping.
  • CKKS traces to Cheon-Kim-Kim-Song (2017) and CryptoLab/HEAAN as the reference lineage — useful to know when downstream library behavior needs a ground-truth check.
Chapter 11

TFHE and Programmable Bootstrapping

Learning Objectives
  • Explain the TFHE philosophy inversion: bootstrapping as the primary operation, not a rare, expensive last resort.
  • Trace the FHEW/TFHE lineage and know what each paper contributed.
  • Describe blind rotation at a conceptual level and explain why it makes lookup-table evaluation possible.
  • Articulate why TFHE — uniquely among the schemes in this book — can natively evaluate non-polynomial functions, and what constraint this ability trades against.
  • Navigate the Zama ecosystem: TFHE-rs, the Concrete compiler, and Concrete ML, and know which layer to touch for which task.
  • Build and reason about a quantized encrypted classifier via Concrete ML.

Chapter 10 ended with CKKS treating noise as an unavoidable companion to the message. TFHE (also called CGGI, for Chillotti-Gama-Georgieva-Izabachène) takes a different attitude toward the other expensive resource in FHE: bootstrapping. Every scheme we have discussed so far treats bootstrapping (Chapter 12) as a costly emergency procedure, to be avoided by careful depth budgeting whenever possible. TFHE inverts this. In TFHE, bootstrapping is not an occasional refresh — it is the mechanism by which every nonlinear gate is computed. You bootstrap after essentially every operation, by design, and the entire scheme is engineered to make that individual bootstrap fast (tens of milliseconds on CPU, sub-millisecond on GPU) rather than rare. Certain

11.1 The TFHE Philosophy

Recall the mental model from the theory reference: additions are cheap, multiplications spend the noise budget, and bootstrapping is the expensive refuel you try to minimize. CKKS and BGV/BFV pipelines are architected around depth budgets precisely because bootstrapping there is slow (seconds, sometimes tens of seconds for a full CKKS bootstrap — Chapter 12) and only restores levels; it does not, by itself, let you compute anything you couldn't already compute with a leveled circuit of sufficient depth.

TFHE's bootstrap is different in kind, not just degree: it is fast enough (single-digit to double-digit milliseconds per gate on CPU) to run after every binary gate in a Boolean circuit, or after every LUT evaluation in the integer variant, and — this is the important part — the bootstrap itself can apply an arbitrary function as it refreshes the ciphertext. This dual-purpose bootstrap is called programmable bootstrapping (PBS), and it is why TFHE occupies a completely different niche from CKKS: not "arithmetic on encrypted reals" but "arbitrary functions on encrypted small integers, gate by gate."

11.2 Lineage: FHEW to TFHE

The line runs through two landmark papers:

  • FHEW (Ducas–Micciancio, 2015). "FHEW: Bootstrapping Homomorphic Encryption in Less Than a Second" introduced the accumulator-based bootstrapping technique — encoding the ciphertext's message as a rotation amount and using that rotation to select from a table encoded in an accumulator polynomial — that reduced bootstrap latency from the tens of seconds typical of Gentry-style bootstrapping to under a second. This accumulator/blind-rotation idea is the direct ancestor of TFHE's bootstrap. Certain
  • TFHE (Chillotti–Gama–Georgieva–Izabachène, 2016, journal version 2020 in Journal of Cryptology). "TFHE: Fast Fully Homomorphic Encryption over the Torus" reformulated the FHEW approach over the real torus $\mathbb{T} = \mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$, introduced a more efficient external product between an LWE/GSW-style ciphertext and an RLWE ciphertext, and drove bootstrap latency down to the millisecond range — the regime that makes "bootstrap after every gate" practical rather than merely possible. Certain

The two names — FHEW and TFHE — are still both used in the literature and in library APIs (OpenFHE, for instance, exposes both an FHEW-style scheme object and a TFHE-style one, referring to closely related but distinguishable parameterizations of this same accumulator-bootstrapping family). Likely — the precise distinctions FHEW vs. TFHE draw at the API level are library-specific; verify against current OpenFHE FHEW/BinFHE documentation if you need the exact boundary

11.3 Blind Rotation, Conceptually

The mechanism that makes programmable bootstrapping work is worth understanding at the level of "what problem does it solve," even without full derivation (the fine-grained algebra of the accumulator, the external product, and the RGSW ciphertext format is beyond this book's scope and is best read directly from the TFHE paper or a survey such as Micciancio-Polyakov's TFHE deep dive).

Start from an LWE ciphertext encrypting a small integer $i \in \{0, 1, \ldots, p-1\}$ — think of $i$ as an index, or equivalently as the (noisy, encrypted) phase of a point on the torus. The goal of blind rotation is: given a plaintext lookup table $\mathsf{LUT} = (\mathsf{LUT}[0], \ldots, \mathsf{LUT}[p-1])$ known to the evaluator, produce an encryption of $\mathsf{LUT}[i]$ — without the evaluator ever learning $i$.

Definition 11.1 — Blind Rotation (Conceptual)

Encode the table as coefficients of a plaintext polynomial (the test polynomialcontrolled rotations of the accumulator polynomial — each controlled rotation is itself computed via an external product between the accumulator (an RLWE ciphertext) and an RGSW encryption of a bit of the secret key (precomputed once, at key-generation time, as the bootstrapping key). After processing all coefficients, the accumulator's constant term is (an encryption of) exactly $\mathsf{LUT}[i]$ — the table has been "rotated into place" by an amount that depended on the encrypted $i$, without decrypting $i$ at any point. A final key-switch converts the result back to the ciphertext format used elsewhere in the pipeline.

The crucial structural fact, and the reason this chapter exists in a book about ML: the LUT can encode any function from the small domain $\{0,\ldots,p-1\}$ to plaintext values — there is nothing "polynomial" or "linear" required of the function being tabulated. ReLU, sign, step functions, comparisons, min/max, arbitrary S-boxes, even a coarse sigmoid — all of these are just tables, and blind rotation evaluates any of them homomorphically in one bootstrap. Certain This is the capability CKKS structurally lacks (Chapter 10): CKKS can only ever compute polynomials of its inputs, so ReLU/sigmoid/softmax require polynomial approximation, with all the range and depth costs that entails. TFHE sidesteps the approximation problem entirely for these functions — at the cost described next.

11.4 The Constraint: Small Domains

The LUT's domain size $p$ cannot be large. Every element of the domain corresponds to one "position" the blind rotation must be able to select, and the cost of blind rotation — as well as the noise the process introduces, and the size of the bootstrapping key — scales with the plaintext modulus/domain size. In practice, TFHE-family schemes operate over small integers, typically a handful of bits (2–8 bits per LUT input is the common working range; wider integers are built by composing multiple bootstraps, e.g., via chained/tree-based table lookups over several encrypted digits). Certain

This is the trade the theory reference names directly: TFHE buys arbitrary-function evaluation at the price of small integer domains and a per-operation bootstrap latency, whereas CKKS buys wide dynamic range and SIMD throughput at the price of being polynomial-only. Concrete ML's central engineering problem (Section 11.6) is managing exactly this constraint — squeezing real neural-network and tree-model computations through a pipeline whose accumulators must stay within a bounded bit-width.

11.5 Chaining Gates: the Boolean, Shortint, and Integer APIs

A single PBS bootstrap, as described above, evaluates one LUT over one small encrypted integer. Real programs need more than one gate, chained. TFHE-family libraries expose this at three levels of abstraction, and it is worth knowing the ladder because it maps directly onto a speed/expressiveness trade you choose explicitly:

  • Boolean gates. The most granular level — encrypted AND/OR/XOR/NOT/MUX over single encrypted bits, each gate internally a PBS call.
