This is the closest analogue to ordinary Boolean-circuit FHE and the natural target for compiling arbitrary digital logic (e.g., an encrypted comparator built from a ripple-borrow subtractor), at the cost of one bootstrap per gate and circuits that can require thousands of gates for anything nontrivial.
  • Shortint. Small encrypted integers (commonly 2–4 bits) with native encrypted arithmetic (add, multiply, comparisons) built directly as single PBS calls with the arithmetic operation folded into the LUT — e.g., encrypted 4-bit addition is one bootstrap whose table already encodes "add and reduce," not four chained bit-level adders. This is the level at which TFHE's LUT flexibility (Section 11.3) actually pays for itself: an operation that would be a multi-gate circuit in the Boolean API collapses to a single bootstrap.
  • Integer. Wider integers (8, 16, 32+ bits) built by composing multiple shortint "blocks," each carrying a few bits, with carry propagation handled by additional PBS calls between blocks. This is the API level Concrete ML's compiler output and most application code target — it looks like ordinary integer arithmetic, with the bit-width/block-count trade-off (Section 11.7) happening underneath.

The throughline across all three levels is the same fact from Section 11.4 restated operationally: wider integers do not make bootstrapping impossible, they make it more bootstraps (more blocks, more carry-propagation PBS calls), which is why bit-width remains the dominant cost lever no matter which API layer you enter TFHE-rs at.

11.6 The Zama Ecosystem

Zama is the company that has done the most to turn TFHE from a cryptographic construction into an engineering platform with a normal software development experience. Its stack has layers, and it matters which layer you reach for:

LayerWhat it isWho touches it
TFHE-rs (v1.x; 1.7.0 current)Pure-Rust implementation of the TFHE scheme itself — Boolean gates, shortint, and integer APIs over encrypted data, with a CUDA GPU backend and an FPGA backend (Zama's Homomorphic Processing Unit, targeting AMD Alveo V80 boards). Note the 1.0 release (early 2025) came with an API-stability commitment, and the HPU backend shipped in the 1.x line — pinning the old 0.11 puts you on a pre-stability API without itSystems/crypto engineers building custom circuits or new higher-level tools
Concrete compiler (v2.10)Compiles a numerical Python program (via an MLIR-based dialect) into an FHE circuit over TFHE-rs, handling bit-width analysis and LUT synthesis automaticallyAnyone who wants "write NumPy-ish code, get FHE" without hand-rolling the LUT/bootstrap graph
Concrete ML (v1.8)sklearn/PyTorch-compatible ML layer on top of the Concrete compiler: quantization-aware training/inference, model `.compile()`, encrypted `.predict(..., fhe="execute")`ML engineers who want encrypted inference without touching crypto APIs at all

TFHE-rs's GPU backend is a meaningful part of the current performance story: CUDA acceleration has been reported to substantially reduce bootstrap latency relative to CPU, and Zama's published roadmap targets further latency reduction (into the sub-millisecond range per bootstrap on high-end GPUs) as the throughput bottleneck for gate-by-gate bootstrapping is precisely what limits how large a TFHE circuit can run in practical time. Likely — exact throughput/latency figures move release to release; treat any specific number as a snapshot, not a spec, and re-benchmark on your own hardware

Zama's institutional trajectory is also relevant context for anyone evaluating the ecosystem's staying power: the company raised a $73M Series A and a $57M Series B (2025), reaching a valuation above $1B and becoming — by its own and press characterization — the first FHE-focused "unicorn." Likely — funding figures and characterizations are current as of the sources checked; re-verify if citing precise numbers in a time-sensitive context This matters practically because it is a signal (not a guarantee) about the library's maintenance trajectory — a consideration worth weighing the same way you'd weigh any dependency's backing when building a production pipeline on it.

11.7 Concrete ML as the ML Interface to TFHE

Concrete ML is deliberately built to look like scikit-learn (and, for neural networks, to accept PyTorch models trained with quantization-aware training). The central engineering fact to internalize, echoing Section 11.4: the entire feasibility question for a Concrete ML model is bit-width. Model weights, activations, and — critically — intermediate accumulator sums (e.g., the running sum inside a dot product, or inside a tree's split accumulation) must all be representable within the supported integer bit-width, documented in the ~16-bit range for accumulators, with the practical sweet spot considerably narrower for anything but the shallowest models. Certain, per the coaching reference's verified gotcha

This is what the `n_bits` parameter controls: it sets the quantization bit-width applied to weights/inputs during `.fit()`/`.compile()`. Lower `n_bits` gives smaller LUTs, fewer bits through the accumulator, faster encrypted inference — and lower accuracy. Higher `n_bits` gives better fidelity to the floating-point model at the risk of the accumulator overflowing the supported bit-width, which is a compile-time failure, not a silent accuracy loss — Concrete ML will refuse to build the circuit rather than produce wrong ciphertexts. This is a meaningfully different failure mode than CKKS's silent scale mismatch (Chapter 10): TFHE's bit-width constraint fails loudly, at compile time, which is a genuine ergonomic advantage for iteration speed even though it means more retuning of `n_bits`/model depth to get something that compiles at all.

Tree ensembles (decision trees, random forests, and gradient-boosted trees like XGBoost) are consistently the best fit for this constraint: they have shallow accumulation depth per path and comparisons — exactly the kind of function TFHE's LUT machinery evaluates natively and cheaply — dominate their inference cost. Deep, wide neural networks are harder: every additional layer and every additional channel widens the accumulator, and getting a nontrivial CNN through Concrete ML typically requires careful quantization-aware training (via Zama's Brevitas-based QAT tooling) rather than naive post-training quantization.

11.8 Deciding Between TFHE and CKKS for an ML Workload

Chapter 10 and this chapter together should leave you with a working decision procedure, since the two schemes are the two live options for most real encrypted-ML deployments (BFV/BGV, Chapters 8–9, are the right call specifically when the answer must be bit-exact — most ML inference does not require that). The procedure is not "which scheme is better" — it is "which scheme's native operation matches the workload's dominant cost":

  • Wide linear algebra, few nonlinearities, floats. A transformer's attention and feed-forward matmuls, a CNN's convolutions, a linear/logistic regression scorer — dominated by multiply-accumulate over real-valued tensors, with nonlinearities (softmax, GELU, layer norm) appearing comparatively rarely and needing polynomial approximation (Part III) regardless of scheme. CKKS's SIMD packing (Chapter 13) amortizes the linear-algebra cost beautifully across thousands of slots per ciphertext; this is why CKKS dominates encrypted deep-learning inference benchmarks.
  • Narrow, decision-heavy, exact. Tree ensembles, rule-based scoring, fraud-detection thresholds, encrypted database predicates — dominated by comparisons and branches over small integers, exactly TFHE's native LUT operation, and exactly the case Section 11.6's bit-width constraint is easiest to satisfy (small feature counts, shallow trees). This is why Concrete ML's tree-model support is more mature and more broadly deployed than its deep-CNN support.
  • Mixed workloads. A pipeline with both a substantial linear-algebra backbone (an embedding model) and a genuinely nonlinear, comparison-heavy head (a decision layer) is the case OpenFHE's CKKS $\leftrightarrow$ FHEW/TFHE scheme-switching machinery, gestured at in Chapter 12, exists to serve: run the linear backbone in CKKS, switch representations, run the decision head in TFHE, switch back if needed. This is more implementation complexity than either scheme alone, and is worth reaching for only once profiling shows the mixed cost genuinely justifies it, rather than as a default architecture.

A useful gut-check question when a workload's shape is ambiguous: does the dominant per-inference cost come from summing many products, or from making many small decisions? Summing-many-products workloads want CKKS's wide SIMD lanes; many-small-decisions workloads want TFHE's cheap, exact, per-decision bootstrap.

11.9 Worked Artifact: Encrypted XGBoost via Concrete ML

Artifact 11.1 — Concrete ML: XGBoost with n_bits=6, Compile, Encrypted Inference, Accuracy Comparison [Python, stable API — verify import path/kwargs against current concrete-ml release]
from concrete.ml.sklearn import XGBClassifier
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.metrics import accuracy_score
import numpy as np

# Any tabular classification dataset works here; shape assumed (n_samples, n_features).
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.2, random_state=0)

# n_bits=6 quantizes inputs/weights to 6 bits -- the key feasibility/speed lever
# discussed in 11.6. Too high and the accumulator can exceed the supported
# bit-width at compile time; too low and accuracy degrades.
model = XGBClassifier(n_bits=6, n_estimators=50, max_depth=4)

# .fit() trains the underlying XGBoost model in the clear, then quantizes it.
model.fit(X_train, y_train)

# .compile() builds the FHE circuit: traces the quantized model through the
# Concrete compiler, synthesizes the LUTs for every nonlinear/comparison op
# (tree splits!), and checks the accumulator bit-width constraint. This is
# where an n_bits that is too aggressive will fail loudly, at compile time.
model.compile(X_train)

# Baseline: clear-text (unencrypted, but quantized) prediction, for comparison.
y_pred_clear = model.predict(X_test)

# fhe="simulate" runs the quantized-and-noise-simulated pipeline WITHOUT paying
# for real encrypted execution -- use this first to sanity-check accuracy impact.
y_pred_sim = model.predict(X_test, fhe="simulate")

# fhe="execute" runs genuine encrypted inference: inputs are encrypted client-side,
# the compiled FHE circuit runs entirely on ciphertexts, results are decrypted
# client-side. This is the real, slow, "actually homomorphic" path.
y_pred_fhe = model.predict(X_test[:20], fhe="execute")   # small slice -- FHE execution is orders of magnitude slower than clear/simulate

acc_clear = accuracy_score(y_test, y_pred_clear)
acc_sim = accuracy_score(y_test, y_pred_sim)
acc_fhe = accuracy_score(y_test[:20], y_pred_fhe)

print(f"clear-quantized accuracy:  {acc_clear:.4f}")
print(f"simulate accuracy:         {acc_sim:.4f}")   # should match acc_clear closely
print(f"FHE-execute accuracy (n=20): {acc_fhe:.4f}")  # should match acc_sim on this slice -- FHE is exact w.r.t. the quantized/simulated model

Verify — this reflects the stable sklearn-style Concrete ML pattern documented across recent releases, but always confirm `XGBClassifier` constructor kwargs and the `fhe=` argument's accepted values (`"disable"`/`"simulate"`/`"execute"` naming has been stable but is worth reconfirming) against the docs for the exact `concrete-ml` version pinned in your environment. Note also that FHE-execute accuracy should match simulate accuracy almost exactly on the same inputs — TFHE with PBS is not "approximate" the way CKKS is; once quantized, the encrypted computation reproduces the quantized-clear computation, modulo the (deliberately tiny, parameter-guaranteed) PBS failure probability. The accuracy loss versus a full-precision XGBoost model comes entirely from quantization, not from the FHE evaluation itself.

Key Takeaways
  • TFHE treats bootstrapping as the primary operation, not an occasional refresh — bootstrap-per-gate is affordable because each individual bootstrap is fast (ms-scale).
  • Lineage: FHEW (Ducas-Micciancio, 2015) introduced accumulator-based sub-second bootstrapping; TFHE (Chillotti-Gama-Georgieva-Izabachène, 2016/2020) reformulated it over the torus and pushed it to milliseconds.
  • Blind rotation homomorphically selects $\mathsf{LUT}[i]$ from an encrypted index $i$ via RGSW external products, without revealing $i$ — this is programmable bootstrapping (PBS).
  • Because the LUT can encode any function on a small domain, TFHE natively evaluates non-polynomial functions (ReLU, sign, comparisons) that CKKS/BFV/BGV structurally cannot — the price is a small integer domain per LUT and per-operation bootstrap latency.
  • Zama's stack layers as TFHE-rs (crypto primitive, Rust, GPU/FPGA-accelerated) → Concrete compiler (Python-to-FHE-circuit) → Concrete ML (sklearn/PyTorch-compatible ML layer).
  • Concrete ML's binding constraint is accumulator bit-width (~16-bit ceiling); `n_bits` is the main feasibility/accuracy/speed knob, and tree ensembles are the current sweet spot.
  • Zama's scale (Series A $73M, Series B $57M, $1B+ valuation) is relevant context for evaluating the ecosystem's trajectory, not a substitute for verifying current API behavior yourself.
Chapter 12

Bootstrapping — Turning Leveled HE into Fully HE

Learning Objectives
  • State Gentry's bootstrapping theorem precisely and understand why it is the theoretical hinge between "somewhat/leveled HE" and "fully HE."
  • Describe the cost anatomy of a bootstrap: what is homomorphically evaluated, and why it is expensive.
  • Walk through CKKS bootstrapping's pipeline — ModRaise, CoeffToSlot, EvalSine/EvalCos, SlotToCoeff — and identify where the cost and the approximation error concentrate.
  • Contrast CKKS bootstrapping's cost profile with TFHE's blind-rotation bootstrap from Chapter 11.
  • Make a principled decision between "increase multiplicative depth" and "bootstrap" for a given circuit.
  • Know precisely which libraries do and do not support bootstrapping, and for which schemes.

Chapters 8–10 have all, at some point, gestured at a ceiling: BFV, BGV, and CKKS as we have described them so far are all leveled schemes — parameterized up front for a fixed multiplicative depth $L$, after which the coefficient modulus is exhausted and decryption returns garbage. This chapter is about the theorem that lifts that ceiling, the mechanism that implements it, and — because the mechanism is expensive — the engineering judgment about when to invoke it at all.

12.1 Gentry's Bootstrapping Theorem

Craig Gentry's 2009 breakthrough was not merely "a scheme that supports both addition and multiplication" (Chapter 1 covers why that alone is insufficient once noise growth is accounted for) — it was a theorem showing how to turn a merely somewhat homomorphic scheme (one that can evaluate low-depth circuits before noise overwhelms the ciphertext) into a fully homomorphic one, capable of unbounded-depth computation.

Theorem 12.1 — Bootstrapping (Gentry, 2009, informal statement)

Let $\mathcal{E}$ be a somewhat/leveled homomorphic encryption scheme whose decryption circuit $D_{sk}(\cdot)$ has multiplicative depth $d_{\mathcal{E}}$. If $\mathcal{E}$ can homomorphically evaluate a circuit of depth at least $d_{\mathcal{E}} + 1$ — its own decryption circuit, plus one additional gate — then $\mathcal{E}$ is bootstrappable, and can be transformed into a fully homomorphic scheme: after any operation, homomorphically re-encrypt the ciphertext under a freshly-generated key (or the same key, via a bootstrapping/evaluation key), obtaining a new ciphertext that encrypts the same message with noise reset to a fresh-encryption-like level. Iterating this indefinitely permits circuits of arbitrary depth.

The condition "can evaluate its own decryption circuit plus one gate" is bootstrappability (Gentry 2009, Definition 4.1.3: the scheme's decryption circuit, augmented by one gate, lies inside the family the scheme can evaluate). It is worth keeping this separate from circular security, which the two are often run together: bootstrappability is a pure correctness/capacity property of the scheme, and on its own it already delivers leveled FHE via a chain of independent keys. Circular security is the additional assumption — that the scheme stays secure given an encryption of its own secret key — needed to collapse that chain to a single compact key pair and obtain unbounded depth, and it is what almost every practical deployment in fact relies on. All four families in this book — BGV, BFV, CKKS, TFHE — have been shown bootstrappable under this framework, though the concrete mechanism, and critically the concrete cost, differs enormously by scheme. Certain

12.2 Cost Anatomy: What a Bootstrap Actually Computes

Concretely, bootstrapping works by encoding the secret key $sk$ itself as encrypted data (an encryption of $sk$ under a second key, prepared once at setup time as part of the "bootstrapping key" or "evaluation key"), and then homomorphically evaluating the decryption function on the ciphertext-to-be-refreshed, using that encrypted secret key as one of the inputs. Schematically, for a ciphertext $\mathsf{ct}$ that decrypts (in the clear) via $m = D_{sk}(\mathsf{ct})$, bootstrapping computes

$$\mathsf{ct}_{\text{fresh}} = \mathsf{Eval}\big(D_{(\cdot)}, \; \mathsf{ct}, \; \mathsf{Enc}_{sk'}(sk)\big),$$

i.e., it runs the decryption circuit as a homomorphic circuit, feeding it the noisy ciphertext and an encryption of the key, and produces an encryption (under $sk'$) of the same underlying message $m$ — but now with the noise level of a circuit-evaluated output rather than the original, possibly near-exhausted, noise level of $\mathsf{ct}$. This is why bootstrapping is expensive: you are evaluating a nontrivial circuit — the decryption function — homomorphically, for every ciphertext you want to refresh. Decryption for RLWE-based schemes is, at minimum, an inner product between the ciphertext and the secret key followed by a modular reduction; the modular reduction step is exactly what makes bootstrapping hard to make fast, and it is worth separate attention per scheme.

12.3 CKKS Bootstrapping: Approximating the Reduction

CKKS bootstrapping is the case where the cost anatomy above becomes concrete and instructive, because the modular reduction inside "evaluate decryption homomorphically" is not a polynomial function — and CKKS, as established in Chapter 10, can only homomorphically evaluate polynomials. The entire engineering content of CKKS bootstrapping is finding a good enough polynomial (or trigonometric) approximation to the reduction, and doing so cheaply. Certain

The standard CKKS bootstrapping pipeline, following Cheon-Han-Kim-Kim-Song (2018) and refined in subsequent work, factors into four stages:

Definition 12.1 — CKKS Bootstrapping Pipeline
  1. ModRaise. Raise the ciphertext's modulus back up (conceptually, treat the ciphertext coefficients as integers again rather than residues mod the exhausted $q_0$), so that the message-plus-noise now sits as $m + Iq_0$ for some unknown small integer $I$ — the "wrap-around" the true decryption would need to undo.
  2. CoeffToSlot. Homomorphically apply the (linear) map that moves the plaintext from its coefficient representation into its slot (canonical-embedding) representation — essentially the encoding step from Chapter 10, but run as a homomorphic linear transform on an already-encrypted value, via a sequence of rotations and plaintext-ciphertext multiplications (a homomorphic DFT-like matrix-vector product).
  3. EvalSine / EvalCos (the reduction step). The genuinely hard part: homomorphically compute an approximation to $x \mapsto x \bmod q_0$, which is periodic and is well-approximated, in the region of interest, by a scaled sine or cosine function — hence "EvalSine"/"EvalCos." This trigonometric function is itself evaluated via a polynomial approximation (commonly a Chebyshev-basis minimax polynomial, sometimes accelerated with a double-angle recursion to keep the polynomial degree — and hence the multiplicative depth this step alone consumes — manageable). This stage dominates bootstrapping's runtime and is the accuracy bottleneck: a coarser approximation is cheaper but reintroduces more error into the "refreshed" ciphertext.
  4. SlotToCoeff. The inverse homomorphic linear transform, moving the result back from slot representation to coefficient representation, so that the output ciphertext is usable by subsequent ordinary CKKS operations exactly like any other ciphertext.

Two consequences follow directly from this structure, and both are worth internalizing as design facts rather than implementation trivia:

  • CKKS bootstrapping is itself approximate. Because the modular reduction is approximated by a sine/cosine polynomial rather than computed exactly, bootstrapping does not produce a perfectly noise-free ciphertext — it produces one whose error is dominated by the EvalSine/EvalCos approximation quality, on top of whatever noise was already present. This compounds with the ordinary CKKS approximation error from Chapter 10; precision budgets for CKKS pipelines that bootstrap must account for both.
  • CoeffToSlot/SlotToCoeff and EvalSine consume real multiplicative depth themselves. Bootstrapping is not "free levels" — it costs a nontrivial number of levels internally (commonly cited in the range of roughly ten to twenty, heavily parameter- and implementation-dependent) to execute the pipeline, meaning any parameter set supporting bootstrapping must reserve enough of its own modulus chain just to run the bootstrap itself, on top of whatever depth the "useful" circuit between bootstraps needs. Likely — the exact level cost is a strong function of chosen sine-approximation degree and CoeffToSlot/SlotToCoeff optimization (e.g., sparse packing), and moves across implementations; do not treat any single number as fixed
12.3.1 OpenFHE's Bootstrapping and Sparse-Secret Encapsulation

OpenFHE implements this pipeline for CKKS (`EvalBootstrapSetup`/`EvalBootstrapKeyGen`/`EvalBootstrap` in its public API — verify exact call names against the current release's CKKS bootstrapping examples), and as of v1.4 extends it in two directions relevant to a production reader: general functional bootstrapping using CKKS (bootstrapping combined with evaluating an arbitrary function during the refresh, in the spirit of TFHE's PBS but inside the CKKS numeric model), and a sparse-secret encapsulation mode, which lets a scheme use a dense-secret parameter set for its "useful" homomorphic capacity while performing the bootstrap itself under an encapsulated sparse-secret regime — reported to yield performance improvements (commonly cited around a factor of a few) versus bootstrapping directly under a dense secret, while retaining the security margin of the dense parameterization. Likely — this reflects the OpenFHE v1.4 release notes as checked; confirm exact parameter API (secret-key distribution enum values, e.g. `SPARSE_ENCAPSULATED`) against the version you build against

12.4 Bootstrapping BGV and BFV: The Same Theorem, a Different Reduction

It is worth briefly closing the loop on the exact schemes from Chapters 8–9, since the theorem in Section 12.1 applies to them too, and the contrast with CKKS sharpens what makes CKKS's version specifically hard. BGV bootstrapping (implemented in HElib, notably) follows the same "homomorphically evaluate the decryption circuit" recipe, but because BGV's decryption is over an exact modular-arithmetic message space rather than a continuous real-valued one, the analogous "reduction" step is evaluated as an actual low-degree Boolean/arithmetic circuit rather than approximated by a transcendental function — there is no sine/cosine substitute needed, because the target function genuinely is a polynomial (or is decomposed into one) over the relevant finite ring. This makes BGV bootstrapping conceptually more straightforward than CKKS's, though not necessarily cheaper in absolute terms — the "digit extraction" procedures HElib uses to implement the modular reduction homomorphically are themselves a nontrivial, multi-step circuit with their own depth cost. BFV bootstrapping is comparatively rare in practice: SEAL implements BFV without any bootstrapping path at all (Section 12.6), and the BFV bootstrapping constructions that exist in the literature are generally considered less mature and less deployed than either BGV's HElib-based approach or CKKS's OpenFHE/HEAAN-based approach. Likely — the relative maturity ranking of BGV vs. BFV bootstrapping reflects the literature and library landscape as commonly characterized; a from-scratch survey of the latest academic constructions could shift specifics The practical upshot for a reader choosing a scheme under a hard depth constraint: if you need exact arithmetic and unbounded depth, BGV (via HElib) is the more traveled path than BFV.

12.5 TFHE Bootstrapping: The Other Cost Model Entirely

It is worth explicitly contrasting CKKS bootstrapping against the TFHE bootstrap covered in Chapter 11, because the two could not have more different cost profiles despite sharing the name "bootstrapping":

CKKS bootstrappingTFHE bootstrapping (blind rotation)
What it computesApproximate the (polynomial-incompatible) mod-reduction via sine/cosine polynomial, on a SIMD-packed vector of slots at onceSelect $\mathsf{LUT}[i]$ via accumulator rotation, driven by RGSW external products, one small integer at a time
Typical latencyHundreds of milliseconds to low seconds (heavily parameter/hardware dependent)Single-digit to double-digit milliseconds on CPU; sub-millisecond reported on high-end GPUs
AmortizationOne bootstrap refreshes an entire SIMD-packed ciphertext (up to $N/2$ slots at once)One bootstrap handles one (or a small packed group of) small-integer value(s)
When invokedRarely — only when a leveled circuit's depth budget is insufficientRoutinely — effectively after every nonlinear gate/LUT, by design
Is the result approximate?Yes — approximation error from the sine/cosine fit compounds with CKKS's existing approximationNo, modulo a deliberately tiny, parameter-controlled PBS failure probability — the output is exact w.r.t. the quantized computation

Neither cost model is strictly better — they are optimized for different circuit shapes. A wide, shallow, numerically-continuous circuit (a linear layer, a convolution, most of a transformer's attention matmuls) amortizes beautifully under CKKS's rare-but-batched bootstrap; a narrow circuit dense in nonlinear decision points (tree splits, comparisons, exact bit-level logic) fits TFHE's frequent-but-cheap bootstrap far better. This is precisely the axis along which OpenFHE's scheme-switching machinery (moving a ciphertext between CKKS and FHEW/TFHE representations mid-circuit) is designed to let a single pipeline use each scheme where it excels — noted here as a signpost; scheme-switching mechanics are outside this chapter's scope.

12.6 When to Bootstrap vs. When to Add Depth

The decision between "just parameterize for more depth" and "bootstrap" is a genuine engineering trade, not a default. Weigh it along three axes:

Bootstrap-or-Deepen Checklist
  • Latency cost. A CKKS bootstrap typically costs somewhere in the range of 10x–100x a single homomorphic multiplication in wall-clock time (the exact multiple is parameter- and implementation-dependent — measure it on your own pipeline before trusting a rule of thumb). If your circuit's depth is only modestly beyond what a leveled parameter set supports, increasing the coefficient modulus (accepting a larger, slower — but still leveled and bootstrap-free — parameter set) is very often cheaper in total wall-clock time than bootstrapping once. Likely — the crossover point is workload-specific
  • Precision cost. As Section 12.3 established, CKKS bootstrapping is itself approximate. A pipeline that bootstraps many times accumulates approximation error from every bootstrap on top of ordinary CKKS rounding error — for numerically sensitive computations, this can matter more than the latency.
  • Ceiling reality. If the circuit's depth genuinely has no fixed bound — an iterative algorithm, a loop whose length depends on encrypted data, a server that must support arbitrarily many sequential client requests on the same ciphertext state — no leveled parameter set, however large, suffices. This is the case bootstrapping (or TFHE's per-gate model) exists to solve, and no amount of "just add more levels" avoids it.

Practical default for CKKS ML pipelines (the dominant use case in Part III): budget the circuit's exact multiplicative depth (Chapter 10's discipline), choose the largest leveled parameter set that keeps latency acceptable, and reach for bootstrapping only when the model architecture (very deep networks, or architectures with data-dependent iteration) makes a fixed depth budget structurally insufficient — not as a default step in every pipeline.

12.7 What Each Library Actually Supports

SEAL Has No Bootstrapping — At All, For Any Scheme

Certain, verified Microsoft SEAL implements BFV, BGV, and CKKS, all strictly as leveled schemes — there is no bootstrapping code path for any of them. In SEAL, the multiplicative depth fixed by your `coeff_modulus` choice at context creation is a hard, permanent ceiling for the lifetime of that context. If your circuit needs more depth than your parameters support, the only SEAL-native fix is to choose bigger parameters (more RNS primes, larger $N$, slower everything) — there is no escape hatch. This is precisely why OpenFHE (or HEAAN, for CKKS specifically) is the correct tool once a circuit's depth requirement is open-ended or simply larger than is comfortable to parameterize for directly, and it is why TenSEAL — built on SEAL — inherits the same hard ceiling despite hiding the rescale mechanics (Chapter 10).

LibraryBootstrapping support
Microsoft SEAL v4.4None, any scheme
TenSEAL v0.3.16None (SEAL-based)
OpenFHE v1.5.1Yes — CKKS (incl. functional bootstrapping, sparse-secret encapsulation, v1.4+), and FHEW/TFHE-style bootstrapping for its binary-FHE module
HEAANYes — CKKS, from the scheme's original authors
TFHE-rs / ConcreteYes — bootstrapping (PBS) is the scheme's core per-operation mechanism, as covered in Chapter 11

12.8 Bootstrapping in a Client-Server Deployment

One architectural point is easy to miss from the theorem statement alone: bootstrapping requires the bootstrapping key (the encrypted secret key material from Section 12.2), which — like the relinearization and Galois keys of Chapter 13 — is generated once, client-side, at key-generation time, and handed to the server as part of the evaluation-key bundle. The server never sees the secret key itself; it only ever holds an encryption of it (under a key that, depending on the construction, may be the same secret key re-encrypted, or a second key generated for exactly this purpose). This matters for two practical reasons worth stating explicitly for anyone designing a deployment around bootstrapping rather than merely reading about it:

  • Bootstrapping key size is a real deployment cost. A CKKS bootstrapping key bundle (covering the CoeffToSlot/SlotToCoeff linear-transform keys plus whatever key-switching material EvalBootstrap needs internally) is typically substantially larger than the "ordinary" relinearization and rotation keys a leveled-only pipeline needs, sometimes by a large constant factor, since the CoeffToSlot/SlotToCoeff stage itself is implemented as a sequence of rotations (Chapter 13) each needing its own key material. In a client-server setting where the client generates and transmits keys once, this is a one-time bandwidth cost; in a setting where keys must be regenerated per session, it can dominate the interaction's overall cost.
  • Circular security is a real assumption, not a formality. The server holding an encryption of the client's own secret key (even indirectly, via the bootstrapping key's construction) is precisely the "key-dependent message" scenario flagged in Section 12.1 — standard IND-CPA security does not, by itself, guarantee safety when the adversary (the server) holds encryptions of functions of the secret key. Practical FHE schemes bootstrap safely because they are conjectured (and, for some constructions, proven under specific models) to satisfy circular security, but this is worth knowing as an assumption baked into every bootstrapping-capable deployment, not a solved non-issue.

12.9 Worked Artifact: OpenFHE CKKS Bootstrapping Setup Skeleton

Artifact 12.1 — OpenFHE: CKKS Bootstrapping for a Circuit Exceeding Leveled Depth [C++, SKELETON — verify against current openfhe-development CKKS bootstrapping examples]
// SKELETON -- confirm exact API against src/pke/examples/advanced-ckks-bootstrapping.cpp
// (or the current equivalent) in the openfhe-development repo before compiling.
// This sketches the shape of the setup, not a guarantee of exact call signatures.

#include "openfhe.h"
using namespace lbcrypto;

CCParams<CryptoContextCKKSRNS> parameters;

// The "useful" circuit depth you want available BETWEEN bootstraps -- e.g. a
// few transformer blocks' worth of matmuls -- is separate from, and smaller
// than, the total depth the parameter set must support once you add the
// depth bootstrapping itself consumes (Section 12.3's CoeffToSlot / EvalSine
// / SlotToCoeff pipeline).
uint32_t levelsAvailableAfterBootstrap = 10;   // depth budget for YOUR circuit between refreshes
std::vector<uint32_t> levelBudget = {4, 4};    // CoeffToSlot / SlotToCoeff level cost -- tunable precision/speed knob
uint32_t approxBootstrapDepth = 8;             // levels consumed by ModRaise + EvalSine/EvalCos, approx-degree dependent

uint32_t depth = levelsAvailableAfterBootstrap + approxBootstrapDepth
                  + levelBudget[0] + levelBudget[1];   // total parameter depth must cover it ALL

parameters.SetMultiplicativeDepth(depth);
parameters.SetScalingModSize(50);
parameters.SetSecretKeyDist(SPARSE_ENCAPSULATED);       // v1.4+ sparse-secret encapsulation for cheaper bootstrap
parameters.SetRingDim(1 << 16);                        // bootstrapping typically needs a larger ring than pure-leveled circuits

auto cc = GenCryptoContext(parameters);
cc->Enable(PKE);
cc->Enable(KEYSWITCH);
cc->Enable(LEVELEDSHE);
cc->Enable(ADVANCEDSHE);       // EvalSum/EvalPoly and friends
cc->Enable(FHE);               // bootstrapping is gated by FHE, not ADVANCEDSHE
cc->Enable(FHE);               // explicit FHE-capability flag in recent OpenFHE releases

uint32_t numSlots = 1 << 15;
cc->EvalBootstrapSetup(levelBudget, {0, 0}, numSlots);   // configures CoeffToSlot/SlotToCoeff plans

auto keys = cc->KeyGen();
cc->EvalMultKeyGen(keys.secretKey);
cc->EvalBootstrapKeyGen(keys.secretKey, numSlots);       // the "encrypted decryption key" material bootstrap needs

// ... encrypt at full depth, run your circuit until levels are nearly
// exhausted, then:
auto ctRefreshed = cc->EvalBootstrap(ctNearlyExhausted);
// ctRefreshed now has levelsAvailableAfterBootstrap levels of budget again;
// continue the circuit, bootstrapping again whenever the budget nears zero.

Verify before compiling — `EvalBootstrapSetup`/`EvalBootstrapKeyGen`/`EvalBootstrap` signatures, the `SPARSE_ENCAPSULATED` enum name, the exact `Enable(...)` flags required for bootstrapping, and the recommended ring-dimension/level-budget defaults have all shifted across OpenFHE releases — treat this as a structural map of the setup, and confirm every call against `src/pke/examples/` in the exact version (v1.5.1 or whatever you have pinned) before trusting it to compile.

Key Takeaways
  • Bootstrapping theorem: a scheme that can homomorphically evaluate its own decryption circuit plus one gate can be iterated indefinitely, turning leveled/somewhat-HE into fully HE.
  • Mechanically, bootstrapping homomorphically evaluates decryption using an encryption of the secret key — it is a genuine, expensive circuit evaluation, not a magic reset.
  • CKKS bootstrapping's hard part is that modular reduction is not a polynomial; the ModRaise → CoeffToSlot → EvalSine/EvalCos → SlotToCoeff pipeline approximates it, and EvalSine/EvalCos dominates cost. The result is itself approximate.
  • OpenFHE v1.4+ adds general functional CKKS bootstrapping and sparse-secret encapsulation for cheaper bootstraps.
  • TFHE's bootstrap (blind rotation) is a completely different cost model: cheap per-operation, exact (up to negligible PBS failure probability), applied routinely rather than rarely.
  • Decide bootstrap-vs-deepen by weighing latency (10-100x a multiply, workload-dependent), precision cost (CKKS bootstrap is approximate), and whether the circuit's depth is genuinely unbounded.
  • SEAL has no bootstrapping for any scheme — a hard, verified ceiling inherited by TenSEAL. OpenFHE and HEAAN are the CKKS-bootstrapping-capable options; TFHE-rs/Concrete bootstrap by design.
Chapter 13

Packing, SIMD, Rotations, Relinearization, and Key Switching

Learning Objectives
  • Understand SIMD batching as the primary throughput lever in RLWE-based FHE, and how slot count is determined by scheme and ring dimension.
  • Compare packing layouts for matrix-vector multiplication — row-major, column-major, diagonal — and derive why the diagonal method is preferred.
  • Explain the Halevi-Shoup baby-step giant-step optimization for diagonal packing and what it trades.
  • Define rotation via Galois automorphisms and understand the operational requirement (precomputed Galois keys) it imposes.
  • Explain relinearization as ciphertext-size compression after multiplication, and why skipping it is not merely inefficient but breaks subsequent operations.
  • Recognize key switching as the general primitive underlying both relinearization and rotation.
  • Know FHERMA as a community resource for battle-tested implementations of exactly these building blocks.

The first three chapters of this quartet were about what each scheme computes — CKKS's approximate reals, TFHE's programmable LUTs, and the bootstrap that lets computation continue indefinitely. This closing chapter of Part II is about the machinery that makes any of that computation fast and structured enough to run real workloads: how one ciphertext carries many values at once, how those values move between positions, and the two housekeeping operations — relinearization and key switching — that keep ciphertexts in a shape the rest of the pipeline can consume.

13.1 SIMD Batching: The Primary Throughput Lever

Every RLWE-based scheme in this book packs more than one plaintext value into a single ciphertext, and every homomorphic operation on that ciphertext acts on all packed values simultaneously — single instruction, multiple data, exactly as in the vectorized-CPU sense of the term. This is not an optional optimization bolted on afterward; it is close to the entire reason RLWE-based FHE is fast enough to use at all, as opposed to plain LWE-based schemes operating one scalar at a time.

  • CKKS packs up to $N/2$ complex slots per ciphertext (Chapter 10's canonical-embedding structure — $N/2$ free complex values, the rest forced by conjugate symmetry).
  • BFV/BGV batch-encode up to $N$ integer slots via CRT-style plaintext-ring factorization (Chapter 9).

In both cases, one homomorphic multiplication costs roughly the same wall-clock time whether it is applied to a ciphertext with one meaningful slot or one with all $N/2$ (or $N$) slots full. The practical consequence for anyone building an ML pipeline: a poorly packed ciphertext — one that uses 8 of its 4096 available slots — wastes essentially all of the computation you're paying for. Packing strategy is therefore not a minor implementation detail; it is frequently the single largest determinant of an FHE pipeline's throughput, easily dwarfing constant-factor gains from parameter tuning.

13.2 Packing for Matrix-Vector Multiplication

The canonical case where packing strategy matters most for ML is the linear layer: computing $W\mathbf{x}$ for a plaintext (or encrypted) weight matrix $W \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ and an encrypted input vector $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^n$. Because SIMD operations act elementwise across slots, and cross-slot combination requires rotation (Section 13.3), how you place $\mathbf{x}$'s entries and $W$'s entries into ciphertext slots determines how many rotations the multiplication costs — which in turn typically dominates its latency, since rotations (via key switching, Section 13.4) are considerably more expensive than a plaintext-ciphertext multiply or an add.

13.2.1 Naive Layouts: Row-Major and Column-Major

A row-major layout encrypts each row of $W$ as its own plaintext (or ciphertext) vector aligned with $\mathbf{x}$'s packing, computes $m$ elementwise products $W_{i,:} \odot \mathbf{x}$, then reduces each to a scalar via a sequence of rotate-and-add steps (a "rotation sum," using $\log n$ rotations per row via the standard doubling trick) to get $\sum_j W_{i,j} x_j$. This costs $m$ elementwise multiplications and $m \log n$ rotations — one full rotation-sum per output row, which becomes expensive as $m$ grows, since rotations are the costly primitive here.

A column-major layout instead broadcasts each entry $x_j$ across a full ciphertext, multiplies by column $W_{:,j}$ (packed once per column), and accumulates across columns with plain additions rather than rotations — but constructing the broadcast of $x_j$ itself typically requires either rotations (to isolate then replicate slot $j$) or an alternate encoding, so the rotation cost does not vanish, it moves.

13.2.2 The Diagonal Method

The layout that dominates in practice, due to Halevi and Shoup — both quoted titles, "Algorithms in HElib" (CRYPTO 2014) and "Faster Homomorphic Linear Transformations in HElib" (CRYPTO 2018), are theirs, building on the earlier CRT/SIMD plaintext-slot batching of Smart and Vercauteren ("Fully Homomorphic SIMD Operations", eprint 2011/133), packs $W$ along its diagonals rather than its rows or columns. Define

$$\mathbf{d}_k = (W_{0,k}, W_{1,k+1 \bmod n}, W_{2,k+2 \bmod n}, \ldots, W_{n-1,k+n-1 \bmod n}), \quad k = 0, \ldots, n-1$$

(for square $W$; the rectangular case tiles similarly). Each diagonal $\mathbf{d}_k$ is packed as one plaintext vector. The matrix-vector product is then computed as

$$W\mathbf{x} = \sum_{k=0}^{n-1} \mathbf{d}_k \odot \mathsf{rot}(\mathbf{x}, k),$$

i.e., one rotation of $\mathbf{x}$ per diagonal, one elementwise plaintext-ciphertext multiply, and one accumulating add — for a total of $n$ rotations and $n$ multiplications, instead of row-major's $m \log n$ rotations. For square-ish matrices where $n \approx m$, this is already a clear win once $n$ is much larger than $\log n$; the deeper win is that it also parallelizes better in the SIMD sense (every diagonal-times-rotated-$\mathbf{x}$ term uses the ciphertext's full slot width productively). Certain — this is the standard Halevi-Shoup construction

13.2.3 Baby-Step Giant-Step: Reducing Rotation Count Further

The diagonal method's $n$ rotations can itself be reduced. Halevi and Shoup's baby-step giant-step (BSGS) optimization factors $n \approx n_1 \cdot n_2$ and rewrites the sum by grouping diagonals into $n_2$ "giant step" blocks of $n_1$ "baby step" rotations each:

$$W\mathbf{x} = \sum_{g=0}^{n_2 - 1} \mathsf{rot}\!\left( \sum_{b=0}^{n_1-1} \mathbf{d}_{g n_1 + b}' \odot \mathsf{rot}(\mathbf{x}, b), \; g n_1 \right),$$

where the inner sum reuses only $n_1$ "baby step" rotations of $\mathbf{x}$ (computed once, shared across all $n_2$ giant-step groups) and the outer sum applies $n_2$ "giant step" rotations to the partial results — reducing the total rotation count from $O(n)$ to $O(n_1 + n_2) = O(\sqrt{n})$ with a good choice of factorization, at the cost of needing precomputed Galois keys for a slightly larger, structured set of rotation offsets rather than a simple sequential range. Certain — standard BSGS-for-diagonal-packing result This is the optimization underlying most production "encrypted linear layer" implementations, including the matrix-vector building blocks referenced in Section 13.5's FHERMA discussion, and is the pattern Artifact 13.1 sketches directly.

13.3 Rotations: Galois Automorphisms

Mechanically, "rotating slots" is implemented via a Galois automorphism of the plaintext ring: the map $X \mapsto X^k$ for $k$ coprime to $2N$ (equivalently, $k$ odd) is a ring automorphism of $R_q = \mathbb{Z}_q[X]/(X^N+1)$, and — crucially — under the canonical embedding (Chapter 2, Chapter 10), applying this automorphism to a ciphertext's underlying polynomial corresponds to a cyclic rotation (and, for some $k$, a conjugation/complex-conjugate permutation) of the slot vector. Because a Galois automorphism does not commute with the secret key the way addition/plaintext-multiplication do, applying it to a ciphertext produces something that is not yet a valid encryption under the original secret key — it must be corrected via a key-switching step (Section 13.4), which is why every rotation offset you intend to use must have a corresponding Galois key (also called a rotation key) generated in advance, at the same time as the rest of your evaluation keys. Certain

Forgetting a Galois Key Is a Runtime Error, Not a Compile-Time One

Every library in this book generates Galois keys for a specific, enumerated set of rotation offsets — not "all possible rotations." TenSEAL's `generate_galois_keys()`, SEAL's `GaloisKeys` object, and OpenFHE's `EvalRotateKeyGen(secretKey, {offsets...})` all require you to name the offsets your circuit will use, ahead of time. If your circuit later calls `rotate(ct, k)` for an offset $k$ you did not include, the result is a runtime failure (or, in the worst case, a decryption that silently returns garbage because the required key material simply isn't there) — not something the compiler or type system catches for you. Certain This is the single most common "it worked in my unit test but broke in the full pipeline" bug in packed-ciphertext code: the unit test exercised a subset of rotation offsets, and the full circuit — especially after a BSGS-style optimization changes which offsets it needs — exercises a superset. Always derive the required offset set programmatically from the circuit definition (e.g., all $\{b\}$ and $\{g n_1\}$ values from Section 13.2.3's BSGS decomposition) rather than hand-enumerating it, precisely to avoid this drift.

13.4 Rotate-and-Sum: Reduction Along Slots

Before leaving rotations, it is worth naming the one rotation pattern that recurs in almost every packed-vector algorithm, including both the diagonal method above and the common "sum all slots" reduction needed whenever a computation collapses a packed vector to a scalar (a dot product, an average, an attention-score normalization). Given a ciphertext with $n$ meaningful slots, the rotate-and-sum (or "rotation sum") pattern computes the total via $\lceil \log_2 n \rceil$ rotations and additions, using the standard doubling trick:

$$\mathsf{ct} \leftarrow \mathsf{ct} + \mathsf{rot}(\mathsf{ct}, n/2); \quad \mathsf{ct} \leftarrow \mathsf{ct} + \mathsf{rot}(\mathsf{ct}, n/4); \quad \ldots \quad \mathsf{ct} \leftarrow \mathsf{ct} + \mathsf{rot}(\mathsf{ct}, 1),$$

after which every slot of the final ciphertext holds the same value: the sum of the original $n$ slots. This is logarithmic rather than linear in $n$ precisely because each step doubles the "reach" of the partial sum already accumulated in each slot — after $k$ steps, slot $i$ holds the sum of $2^k$ originally-adjacent slots. The pattern appears constantly in encrypted ML: computing a dot product as elementwise-multiply-then-rotate-and-sum, computing a batch mean, or collapsing per-head attention scores. Because it is $O(\log n)$ rotations rather than $O(n)$, it is cheap enough to use liberally — but each of those $\log n$ rotations still needs its Galois key generated ahead of time (the powers-of-two offsets $n/2, n/4, \ldots, 1$), which is a second, independent reason (beyond the diagonal-packing offsets of Section 13.2) that real pipelines derive their required Galois-key offset set programmatically rather than by hand.

13.5 Relinearization

A freshly encrypted RLWE ciphertext is a pair $(c_0, c_1) \in R_q^2$, satisfying $c_0 + c_1 s \approx m$ under secret $s$ (Chapter 2). Multiplying two such ciphertexts — via the tensor/Kronecker-style ciphertext multiplication that computes $(c_0, c_1) \otimes (c_0', c_1')$ — produces a three-component object $(d_0, d_1, d_2)$ satisfying $d_0 + d_1 s + d_2 s^2 \approx m m'$: the degree in $s$ has grown from 1 to 2, matching the fact that we multiplied two degree-1 expressions in $s$. Certain

Relinearization uses a precomputed relinearization key (also called an evaluation key or eval-mult key — an encryption of $s^2$ under $s$, in the simplest formulation, or an RNS-decomposed variant thereof for numerical stability) to collapse $(d_0, d_1, d_2)$ back down to a standard two-component ciphertext $(d_0', d_1')$ satisfying $d_0' + d_1' s \approx m m'$ — same message, same (up to relinearization's own small noise contribution) noise level, but back to the canonical size. Certain

Skipping Relinearization Is a Cost Choice, Not a Correctness One

It is often said that you must relinearize after every multiplication or the ciphertext becomes unusable. That overstates it, and the overstatement obscures a real optimisation. A three-component ciphertext is perfectly well-formed: decryption evaluates $d_0 + d_1 s + d_2 s^2$, and SEAL's own 1_bfv_basics.cpp deliberately computes on and decrypts size-3 ciphertexts without relinearizing, noting that decryption still works. OpenFHE likewise exposes EvalMultNoRelin precisely so you can defer it.

What skipping it actually costs is growth: sizes compose, so multiplying a size-3 by a size-3 gives size-5, and both the ciphertext memory and the cost of every subsequent operation grow with size — quadratically for further multiplications. Noise grows faster too. The legitimate use of deferral is to batch a sum of products (multiply pairs without relinearizing, add the size-3 results, relinearize once at the end), which saves key-switching operations.

Two caveats keep this from being free. Rotations and other Galois operations, and some higher-level library entry points, do assume the canonical two-component form and will reject an oversized ciphertext. And in CKKS the relinearization must still happen before the level bookkeeping gets confusing. So the practical default remains "relinearize after each multiply" — but as an engineering default about cost and API compatibility, not because the alternative is mathematically broken. Certain

13.6 Key Switching: The General Primitive

Both operations above — relinearization and rotation — are special cases of a single underlying primitive: key switching, which re-expresses a ciphertext valid under one key (or one "virtual" key, like $s^2$, or a permuted key like $s(X^k)$ under a Galois automorphism) as a ciphertext valid under a different target key, using a precomputed key-switching key that encrypts the relationship between the two. Certain

Definition 13.1 — Key Switching (Schematic)

Given a ciphertext component $c$ valid "under" some key $s_{\text{old}}$ (i.e., contributing a term $c \cdot s_{\text{old}}$ to the decryption relation) and a key-switching key $\mathsf{ksk}_{s_{\text{old}} \to s_{\text{new}}}$ — an encryption, under $s_{\text{new}}$, of $s_{\text{old}}$ (typically RNS/gadget-decomposed for noise control) — key switching computes a new ciphertext component that contributes the equivalent term under $s_{\text{new}}$ instead, at the cost of adding a small, controlled amount of additional noise. Relinearization is key switching from "$s^2$" to "$s$"; rotation's correction step is key switching from "$s(X^k)$" to "$s$."

Recognizing key switching as the shared primitive is more than a taxonomic nicety: it is why relinearization keys, Galois/rotation keys, and (in multi-party or scheme-switching settings) inter-scheme key-switching keys all have the same basic shape (gadget-decomposed encryptions of a key relationship) and the same basic cost driver (the gadget decomposition's number of digits trades noise growth against key size and switching cost) — so intuition built debugging one transfers directly to the others.

13.6.1 The Gadget Decomposition Trade-Off

The one implementation detail worth naming explicitly, because it surfaces as a real tunable parameter in both SEAL and OpenFHE: a key-switching key cannot simply be "an encryption of $s_{\text{old}}$ under $s_{\text{new}}$," because the noise a single such encryption would contribute, once multiplied through the ciphertext coefficients during switching, would overwhelm the message. The standard fix decomposes the ciphertext component being switched into several smaller "digits" in a different base (a base-$B$ or RNS-based decomposition — the same family of gadget-decomposition ideas used throughout lattice cryptography for exactly this noise-control purpose), switches each digit separately against its own, correspondingly-scaled key-switching key component, and sums the results. More digits means finer decomposition, which means less noise added per switch, but also means the key-switching key itself is larger (proportional to the digit count) and each switch touches more key material, so it is slower. Modern RNS-CKKS/BGV implementations typically fold this decomposition into the same RNS representation already used for the coefficient modulus chain (a "hybrid key switching" technique, following Han-Ki and related RNS key-switching literature), which is why, in practice, you rarely choose the digit count directly — it falls out of the RNS modulus chain you already specified for leveled-depth reasons (Chapter 9, Chapter 10) — but it is worth knowing this coupling exists the next time a parameter change aimed purely at "more depth" also visibly changes rotation/relinearization latency. Likely — the exact coupling between RNS chain design and key-switching digit count is implementation-specific across SEAL/OpenFHE/HElib; the qualitative trade-off (finer decomposition = less noise, more key material, more switching work) is the stable, library-independent fact

13.7 FHERMA: Community-Verified Building Blocks

FHERMA is a challenge/competition platform, jointly developed by Fair Math and the OpenFHE community, where participants submit FHE implementations of specific, well-defined components — comparison, sorting networks, matrix operations, and other primitives exactly like the packed matrix-vector multiplication this chapter has built up to — which are then automatically benchmarked and ranked. Certain The resulting solutions (collected in an accompanying open-source components library, `polycircuit`) are a genuinely useful reference point precisely because they are implementations that have been competitively optimized and automatically verified against test cases, rather than illustrative-only skeletons — worth consulting when the BSGS matrix-vector pattern in Artifact 13.1 needs to become genuinely production-tuned rather than merely correct. Likely — platform specifics (challenge categories, current leaderboard content) evolve; check fherma.io and the `UCF-ML-Research/FHERMA` / `fairmath/polycircuit` repositories directly for current state

13.8 Worked Artifact: Packed Matrix-Vector Multiplication with BSGS

Artifact 13.1 — OpenFHE: Encrypted Matrix-Vector Multiplication, Diagonal Packing + BSGS Rotations [C++, SKELETON — verify against current OpenFHE linear-transform examples]
// SKELETON -- confirm exact API against src/pke/examples/ (e.g. the CKKS linear-transform /
// EvalLinearWSum-adjacent examples, and OpenFHE's EvalFastRotation / hoisted-rotation helpers)
// in the current openfhe-development repo. Hand-rolled BSGS is shown for pedagogy; recent
// OpenFHE releases may expose a higher-level matrix/linear-transform API that supersedes this.

#include "openfhe.h"
#include <cmath>
using namespace lbcrypto;

// Assume W is n x n, x is length n, both fit in available slots (n <= N/2).
int n = 128;
int n1 = static_cast<int>(std::ceil(std::sqrt(n)));   // baby-step count
int n2 = static_cast<int>(std::ceil((double)n / n1)); // giant-step count

CCParams<CryptoContextCKKSRNS> parameters;
parameters.SetMultiplicativeDepth(2);   // one mult (diag * rotated-x) + headroom
parameters.SetScalingModSize(50);
parameters.SetBatchSize(n);
auto cc = GenCryptoContext(parameters);
cc->Enable(PKE); cc->Enable(KEYSWITCH); cc->Enable(LEVELEDSHE);

auto keys = cc->KeyGen();
cc->EvalMultKeyGen(keys.secretKey);

// Collect ALL rotation offsets the BSGS decomposition needs, up front --
// this is exactly the discipline the Section 13.3 warning box demands.
std::vector<int32_t> rotIndices;
for (int b = 0; b < n1; ++b) rotIndices.push_back(b);            // baby steps
for (int g = 0; g < n2; ++g) rotIndices.push_back(g * n1);       // giant steps
cc->EvalRotateKeyGen(keys.secretKey, rotIndices);

// Encrypt x.
std::vector<double> xVals = /* length-n input vector */;
auto ctX = cc->Encrypt(keys.publicKey, cc->MakeCKKSPackedPlaintext(xVals));

// Precompute the n diagonals of W as plaintexts (Section 13.2.2's d_k), then
// group them per Section 13.2.3's BSGS regrouping: d'_{g,b} = d_{g*n1+b}
// rotated by -g*n1 so it aligns with the un-rotated baby-step x.
std::vector<std::vector<Plaintext>> diagPt(n2, std::vector<Plaintext>(n1));
// ... fill diagPt[g][b] from W, applying the -g*n1 rotation to the raw diagonal
//     values BEFORE encoding (a plaintext-side rotation, done in the clear).

// Baby step: precompute rotated x for b = 0..n1-1 ONCE, reuse across all giant steps.
std::vector<Ciphertext<DCRTPoly>> xRot(n1);
for (int b = 0; b < n1; ++b)
    xRot[b] = (b == 0) ? ctX : cc->EvalRotate(ctX, b);

// Giant step: for each g, accumulate the inner baby-step sum, then rotate
// the partial result by g*n1 and accumulate into the final output.
Ciphertext<DCRTPoly> result;
for (int g = 0; g < n2; ++g) {
    Ciphertext<DCRTPoly> inner;
    for (int b = 0; b < n1; ++b) {
        if (g * n1 + b >= n) break;               // guard for n not exactly n1*n2
        auto term = cc->EvalMult(xRot[b], diagPt[g][b]);
        inner = (b == 0) ? term : cc->EvalAdd(inner, term);
    }
    auto giantTerm = (g == 0) ? inner : cc->EvalRotate(inner, g * n1);
    result = (g == 0) ? giantTerm : cc->EvalAdd(result, giantTerm);
}

// result now encrypts W*x, using n1 + n2 ~ O(sqrt(n)) rotations total
// instead of the naive O(n) diagonal-method rotation count.
Plaintext resultPt;
cc->Decrypt(keys.secretKey, result, &resultPt);

Verify before compiling — this is a hand-rolled, pedagogical BSGS implementation to make Section 13.2.3's math concrete in code; current OpenFHE releases may expose hoisted-rotation primitives (batching several rotations of the same ciphertext to amortize key-switching cost, sometimes called "fast rotation" or rotation hoisting in the literature and in EvalFastRotationPrecompute-style APIs) that should replace the naive per-rotation calls shown here in any performance-sensitive deployment. Check `src/pke/examples/` and the FHERMA `polycircuit` repository (Section 13.6) for current, competitively-optimized reference implementations of this exact primitive before shipping.

Key Takeaways
  • SIMD packing — $N/2$ complex slots (CKKS) or $N$ integer slots (BFV/BGV) per ciphertext, one op touching all of them — is the primary throughput lever in RLWE-based FHE; underfilled slots waste most of the paid-for computation.
  • For matrix-vector multiplication, diagonal packing (Halevi-Shoup) beats row-/column-major layouts, costing $O(n)$ rotations instead of $O(m \log n)$; baby-step giant-step further reduces this to $O(\sqrt{n})$ rotations.
  • Rotation is implemented via Galois automorphisms $X \mapsto X^k$; every offset used requires a precomputed Galois/rotation key, or the circuit fails at runtime — not compile time.
  • Multiplication grows a ciphertext from 2 to 3 components; relinearization (via a relin/eval-mult key) compresses it back to 2. Skipping it breaks, rather than merely slows, subsequent operations.
  • Key switching is the general primitive under both relinearization (switch from $s^2$ to $s$) and rotation's correction step (switch from $s(X^k)$ to $s$) — one mental model, one cost driver, for both.
  • FHERMA (Fair Math + the OpenFHE community) is a competitively-verified source of reference implementations for exactly these building blocks — worth consulting once a skeleton needs to become production-grade.
Part III

FHE Engineering

From theory to production: parameter selection, library mastery, and performance optimization.
Chapter 14

Parameter Selection and Security Standards

Learning Objectives
  • Read and apply the HomomorphicEncryption.org Security Standard v1.1 table to map $(N, \log_2 q)$ to a security level.
  • Reason explicitly about the security–performance–functionality tradeoff triangle instead of copy-pasting a tutorial's parameters.
  • Derive a concrete CKKS parameter set — ring dimension, coefficient modulus chain, scale — for a circuit of known multiplicative depth.
  • Understand RNS/CRT decomposition well enough to explain why every FHE library's parameter API takes a list of prime bit-sizes, not a single modulus size.
  • Use the lattice-estimator to validate parameters that fall outside the standard table (non-uniform secrets, custom noise distributions, novel schemes).

14.1 Parameters are a security decision wearing a performance costume

Every FHE tutorial you have read hands you a parameter block — poly_modulus_degree = 8192, coeff_mod_bit_sizes = [60, 40, 40, 60] — and moves on to the interesting cryptography. This is backwards. The parameter block is the cryptography. Every other chapter in this book has treated $N$ (the ring dimension) and $q$ (the ciphertext modulus) as fixed background; this chapter is about how those numbers get chosen, and why choosing them wrong is a silent failure mode — your code compiles, your ciphertexts decrypt, your demo works, and your deployment is insecure. Certain

The reason this is subtle is that RLWE hardness is a function of the ratio between $N$ and $\log_2 q$, not either one in isolation. A larger ring dimension $N$ makes the lattice problem harder (good for security) at fixed $q$. A larger modulus $q$ makes it easier (bad for security) at fixed $N$, because it shrinks the "distinguishing gap" the LWE noise has to hide inside. Every other design constraint you care about pulls one of these two knobs: multiplicative depth wants a bigger $q$ (more primes in the chain, more levels), and both latency and ciphertext size scale essentially linearly (well, $N\log N$ for the NTT-based ops) in $N$. Parameter selection is the simultaneous solution of a security inequality and a functionality equation, and the entire discipline of "concrete FHE parameters" is a decision procedure for that joint solve.

14.2 The HomomorphicEncryption.org Security Standard v1.1

Since 2017, a series of open community workshops — organized initially by Microsoft, Duality, and a rotating cast of academic and industrial co-hosts — has maintained a public reference table mapping RLWE/LWE parameters to security levels. The document, commonly called "the HomomorphicEncryption.org standard" or informally "the HES table," is what every production library defaults to when you don't specify raw parameters yourself: SEAL's CoeffModulus::BFVDefault, OpenFHE's automatic parameter selection in GenCryptoContext, and TenSEAL's context presets all consult a version of this table under the hood. Certain

Definition — What the standard actually specifies

For a ring dimension $N$ (a power of two), a ciphertext modulus $q$, a secret-key distribution (the standard's reference setting is uniform ternary, coefficients in $\{-1,0,1\}$), and an error distribution (discrete Gaussian with a fixed standard deviation, conventionally $\sigma \approx 3.2$), the table gives the maximum $\log_2 q$ for which the best known attacks (primal/dual lattice reduction via BKZ, and its refinements) require at least $2^{\lambda}$ operations, for $\lambda \in \{128, 192, 256\}$.

The commonly cited (and, since the standard's 2018 publication, remarkably stable) reference values for uniform-ternary-secret RLWE at 128-bit classical security are approximately:

N       max log2(q)  for 128-bit security
2^10       27
2^11       54
2^12      109
2^13      218
2^14      438
2^15      881

Verify — treat this table as the shape of the relationship (roughly linear in $N$ on a log-log plot, doubling $\log_2 q$'s budget each time $N$ doubles) and pull the exact current numbers from the standard's published PDF or from your library's built-in table before shipping; the community has discussed refinements to the secret-distribution assumptions (sparse and Hamming-weight-bounded secrets used by some CKKS bootstrapping configurations shift the safe $\log_2 q$ budget down) at recent workshops without yet displacing this table as the default reference.

The standard is a living document, kept current through periodic community meetings that track the state of lattice cryptanalysis (each incremental improvement to BKZ sieving, or to the accounting of the "hybrid" primal/dual attacks, can in principle move the safe boundary). Recent meetings in this series: the 7th Workshop on the HE Security Standard (Salt Lake City, October 2024), the 8th (Istanbul, March 2025), and the 9th (Seoul, March 2026). Likely — confirm exact dates/venues against the homomorphicencryption.org meeting archive if citing them, since workshop scheduling is announced incrementally. The applied side of the field runs on a parallel track: WAHC (the Workshop on Encrypted Computing & Applied Homomorphic Cryptography) is co-located with ACM CCS 2026 in The Hague on November 19, 2026, and remains the primary applied-FHE venue for parameter-selection and implementation papers that feed back into the standard. Likely

Warning — the standard bounds a specific attack model, not "security" in the abstract

The table assumes the reference secret/error distributions above. If your library or your own code deviates — a sparse secret for faster bootstrapping, a smaller-than-reference error standard deviation to save a few bits of $\log q$, a non-power-of-two cyclotomic ring — the table no longer applies and you must re-derive the security level with a dedicated estimator (§14.5). "I used the standard table" is only a valid security argument if your actual parameters match its assumptions exactly. Certain

14.3 The tradeoff triangle

Every concrete parameter decision is a point inside a triangle whose three vertices pull in mutually incompatible directions:

  • Security wants large $N$, small $\log_2 q$ — more lattice dimensions per bit of modulus.
  • Performance wants small $N$, small $\log_2 q$ — every homomorphic operation is $O(N \log N)$ per prime in the RNS chain, so both factors independently drive latency and memory.
  • Functionality (multiplicative depth, i.e. how many sequential multiplies your circuit needs before decryption) wants large $\log_2 q$ — more primes in the chain means more levels to spend.

Performance and security agree on wanting small $q$; functionality is the odd vertex out, demanding a bigger $q$ to buy depth. Security and functionality together force $N$ upward: once your circuit's depth fixes a minimum $\log_2 q$, the security table forces a minimum $N$ to match it, and that $N$ is usually far larger than what raw throughput would have chosen on its own. This is the mechanism by which "I need a deeper circuit" silently becomes "my ring dimension quadrupled and my ciphertexts are now megabytes." Certain

The parameter-selection algorithm, stated as a fixed point

Given a circuit of multiplicative depth $D$: (1) choose a coefficient modulus chain with $D{+}1$ or more primes, summing to $\log_2 q$ bits, sized to give the numerical precision you need; (2) look up (or estimate) the minimum $N$ that keeps $(N, \log_2 q)$ at your target security level; (3) check that $N$ is large enough to provide the SIMD slot count ($N/2$ for CKKS) your workload wants; (4) if $N$ from step 2 is larger than needed for step 3, you have slack — use it, or shrink $q$ back down. This is rarely a single pass: adding levels for numerical safety margin can push $N$ into the next power-of-two bracket, which then gives you free extra slots, which changes your batching strategy.

14.4 Concrete CKKS parameter derivation: given depth $D$, choose $N$, the modulus chain, and $\Delta$

The recipe, worked through explicitly:

  1. Fix the scale $\Delta$. $\Delta$ sets the fixed-point precision: encoding error is roughly $O(1/\Delta)$ relative to the message. A common choice is $\Delta = 2^{40}$, giving roughly 40 bits of precision per coordinate before any error accumulation — enough headroom for most ML inference workloads whose final answer only needs single-precision-float accuracy. Higher-precision applications (financial computation, iterative algorithms that compound error) push toward $2^{45}$–$2^{50}$.
  2. Build the modulus chain. The standard structure is $q = q_0 \cdot q_1 \cdots q_{D-1} \cdot q_L$, where: $q_0$ (the "first" or "last" prime, depending on which end of the chain you count from) is sized larger than $\Delta$ — typically 55–60 bits; each $q_i$ for $i = 1 \ldots D-1$ is sized to match $\Delta$ (so rescaling by that prime removes almost exactly one factor of $\Delta$ and keeps the scale stable across levels); and the remaining oversized prime (55–60 bits) is the special prime, reserved for key-switching. For a depth-$D$ circuit you need $D$ scale-sized primes plus the two bookend primes: $D + 2$ primes total, though many libraries let the last "level" be consumed implicitly and you'll see $D+1$ in practice depending on convention.
  3. Get the direction of travel right. Two points here are easy to invert and both matter when you debug a level error. First, the special prime is the key-switching auxiliary prime — the extra oversized modulus that gives the hybrid key-switch its headroom — not $q_0$; it is never a compute level and never consumed by a rescale, which is exactly why the spendable levels number $\text{len(chain)} - 2$ and not $\text{len(chain)} - 1$. Second, a fresh ciphertext starts at the top of the chain, modulo the product of all the primes, and each rescale drops a prime, walking the ciphertext down toward $q_0$. So $q_0$ is not where encoding precision is held before the first rescale — it is what remains at the end, and it is oversized so the final result still carries enough bits to decrypt at usable precision after all the rescaling has happened. Sizing $q_0$ as though it were the starting point is a common way to end up with a chain that decrypts to noise on the last layer.
  4. Sum the bit-sizes to get the required $\log_2 q$.
  5. Look up the minimum $N$ at your target security level (§14.2) that supports that $\log_2 q$; round up to the next available ring dimension (always a power of two).
  6. Check the slot budget. $N/2$ complex slots must be $\geq$ your largest batching requirement. If not, you cannot shrink $N$ below the security floor — but you also cannot get more slots than $N/2$ without going to a larger $N$, so a slot deficit forces the next $N$ up regardless of what security demanded.
Example — depth-5 circuit, target 128-bit security

Suppose a circuit needs 5 sequential multiplies. Chain: $q_0 = 60$ bits, five scale primes at 40 bits each ($\Delta = 2^{40}$), $q_L = 60$ bits. Total: $60 + 5{\times}40 + 60 = 320$ bits. From the §14.2 table, $N=2^{13}$ (8192) supports up to 218 bits at 128-bit security — not enough. $N=2^{14}$ (16384) supports up to 438 bits — sufficient, with 118 bits of unused headroom you could spend on a deeper circuit or a larger scale. Final parameters: $N = 16384$, 7-prime chain $[60,40,40,40,40,40,60]$, $\Delta = 2^{40}$, 8192 usable complex slots.

14.5 RNS representation and why coeff_mod_bit_sizes is a list

Every production CKKS/BFV/BGV implementation represents the single large modulus $q$ not as one giant multi-precision integer but as a product of small, machine-word-sized primes: $q = q_0 \cdot q_1 \cdots q_L$, each $q_i$ typically 30–60 bits so it fits in a 64-bit word with room for intermediate products. By the Chinese Remainder Theorem, arithmetic mod $q$ decomposes into independent, parallel arithmetic mod each $q_i$ — this is the Residue Number System (RNS) representation, and it is the single most important implementation technique that makes FHE practical on commodity CPUs. Without it, every ciphertext coefficient would be a multi-precision integer with hundreds of bits, and every multiplication would require slow big-integer arithmetic; with it, each "limb" is a native machine word amenable to fast modular multiplication and, critically, the Number Theoretic Transform (NTT) for $O(N \log N)$ polynomial multiplication. Certain

This is precisely why coeff_mod_bit_sizes (SEAL, TenSEAL) or the internal prime chain construction (OpenFHE's SetScalingModSize + depth, which builds the chain for you) is a list of bit-sizes rather than one integer: you are specifying the RNS decomposition directly, prime by prime. This list does triple duty: it fixes $\log_2 q$ (hence security, via §14.2), it fixes the number of levels $D+1$ (hence functionality), and — because rescaling operates by dropping the top prime off the chain — the order and size of primes in the list determines the scale trajectory through the computation (this becomes the central topic of Chapter 16). Each prime must additionally satisfy $q_i \equiv 1 \pmod {2N}$ so that the NTT of the required size exists in $\mathbb{Z}_{q_i}$ — libraries search for suitable primes near your requested bit-size automatically; you rarely pick the exact prime values yourself. Certain

Warning — depth budget vs. numerical budget are the same list, easy to conflate

Adding a prime to the chain buys you one more multiplicative level and changes where precision sits. Beginners often "fix" a precision problem by bumping every prime's bit-size uniformly, which silently pushes $\log_2 q$ past the security boundary for the current $N$ without any error — the library will happily encrypt, compute, and decrypt with an insecure parameter set unless you re-check §14.2 after every change to the chain. Certain

14.6 The lattice-estimator

When your parameters deviate from the standard table's reference assumptions — sparse or Hamming-weight-bounded secrets (common in bootstrapping-oriented CKKS configurations to keep bootstrapping cheap), non-standard error distributions, hybrid key-switching structures, or ring dimensions/moduli the published table doesn't tabulate — the correct move is not to guess but to run the lattice-estimator, the Sage-based tool maintained by Martin Albrecht and collaborators (successor to the earlier "LWE Estimator"), which implements cost models for the full menu of known lattice attacks: primal and dual uSVP attacks, the BKZ cost models (core-SVP, and refined sieving-based estimates), the "hybrid" combinatorial-lattice attacks relevant to sparse/small secrets, and more. Certain — this is the tool the standard itself is calibrated against, and it is the reference implementation the community cites when proposing table revisions at the workshop meetings in §14.2.

Practically: you feed it $(n, q, \sigma)$ (or an LWE/RLWE instance description) and a choice of attack cost model, and it returns an estimated bit-security level. For RLWE parameters you map the ring dimension $N$ to the LWE dimension $n=N$ under the standard reduction. Running it is a Sage/Python invocation against the estimator's public GitHub repository; treat exact function names as Verify against the current repo before scripting against it, since the API has evolved across the estimator's revisions (the maintainers periodically restructure the cost-model interface as new attacks are added).

Artifact — Parameters for 3-layer NN inference, verified at 128-bit security

Task. Encrypted inference through a 3-layer feedforward network: $\text{Linear}_1 \to \text{Act} \to \text{Linear}_2 \to \text{Act} \to \text{Linear}_3$, with each activation a degree-3 polynomial approximation (e.g. a least-squares fit to GELU/ReLU over the expected pre-activation range).

Step 1 — depth accounting. Each Linear layer, evaluated via ciphertext–plaintext multiplication against diagonal-encoded weight matrices (Chapter 17's BSGS diagonal method), consumes one level per matrix (plaintext multiplication still triggers a rescale, though it needs no relinearization since one operand is unencrypted). Each degree-3 polynomial activation, evaluated as $a x^3 + bx$ via $x^2$ (1 mult) then $x^3 = x^2\!\cdot\! x$ (1 mult), consumes 2 levels. Total: $1 + 2 + 1 + 2 + 1 = 7$.

Step 2 — modulus chain. $\Delta = 2^{40}$. Chain: [60, 40, 40, 40, 40, 40, 40, 40, 60] — one 60-bit bookend prime, 7 forty-bit scale primes (one per level), one 60-bit closing prime. $\log_2 q = 60 + 7{\times}40 + 60 = 400$ bits.

Step 3 — security lookup. At 128-bit security, $N=2^{13}$ supports up to 218 bits (insufficient); $N=2^{14}$ supports up to 438 bits (sufficient, 38 bits of margin). Choose $N = 16384$.

Step 4 — slot check. $N/2 = 8192$ slots — ample for hidden-layer widths in the low thousands; batch multiple inference requests per ciphertext to use the remaining slots if the network is narrower.

Step 5 — validate. Confirm $(N=16384, \log_2 q = 400)$ against the current lattice-estimator run (not just the static table) if the secret-key distribution used by your library build differs from uniform-ternary — OpenFHE and SEAL both default to uniform ternary secrets for CKKS, so the static table check above is normally sufficient, but always re-run the estimator after changing any non-default security setting (secret distribution, custom $\sigma$, ring type).

Result: $N=16384$, chain [60,40,40,40,40,40,40,40,60], $\Delta=2^{40}$, 128-bit security, 7 usable multiplicative levels, 8192 slots. This is the parameter set carried forward into Chapter 16's worked implementation.

Key Takeaways
  • Parameters are a joint solution to a security inequality (the HomomorphicEncryption.org table, a function of $N$ and $\log_2 q$) and a functionality equation (depth $D$ fixes a minimum number of chain primes).
  • The tradeoff triangle — security, performance, functionality — means deeper circuits don't just cost more levels; they force $N$ upward, which multiplies every other cost.
  • RNS/CRT decomposition is why every library's modulus API is a list of prime bit-sizes: that list simultaneously encodes $\log_2 q$, the level count, and (via chapter 16) the scale trajectory.
  • Deviate from the standard table's reference assumptions (secret distribution, error std-dev) only with a lattice-estimator run in hand, not by inspection.
Chapter 15

The Library Landscape

Learning Objectives
  • Place the major FHE libraries — OpenFHE, Microsoft SEAL, TenSEAL, HEaaN, Concrete ML, Lattigo, Apple's swift-homomorphic-encryption, Google HEIR, HElib — correctly on the scheme/language/maturity map.
