  • Know which library to reach for given a task's scheme requirement, bootstrapping need, language, and production-readiness constraints.
  • Recognize each library's characteristic gotcha before it costs you a debugging session.
  • Compare implementation verbosity and mental-model overhead across the three tiers: high-level Python wrapper, mid-level C++ with explicit maintenance, and full leveled/bootstrapped C++.

15.1 The map, at a glance

The FHE library ecosystem in 2026 has consolidated around a small number of serious production/research options, each occupying a distinct niche. This is a healthier state than five years ago, when nearly every research group shipped its own bespoke implementation of BFV or CKKS; today the field has converged on OpenFHE as the de facto general-purpose C++ engine, with a ring of language-specific wrappers, scheme-specialist libraries, and ML-ergonomic frameworks built either on top of it or alongside it. Likely

LibraryLanguageSchemesBootstrappingNiche
OpenFHE v1.5.1C++ (Python bindings)BGV, BFV, CKKS, FHEW, TFHEYes (incl. CKKS) + scheme switchingProduction, research, most feature-complete

TenSEAL v0.3.16Python (wraps SEAL)CKKS, BFVNoFast tensor-level CKKS prototyping

Concrete ML v1.8Python (on TFHE)TFHE (CGGI)Yes (programmable)Sklearn/XGBoost/QAT-NN with zero crypto code

HElib v2.3.0C++BGV, CKKSYes (BGV)Historical/legacy — superseded by OpenFHE

15.2 OpenFHE — the production default

OpenFHE (current release v1.5.1, April 2026) is the direct successor to PALISADE and is built by many of the same researchers behind HElib, HEAAN, and FHEW — it is, credibly, the single most feature-complete open-source FHE library in existence: BGV, BFV, and CKKS with full leveled operation, approximate bootstrapping for CKKS (which SEAL entirely lacks), plus the discretized-LWE FHEW and CGGI-style TFHE schemes for bit-level gate bootstrapping. Be precise about the scope here: OpenFHE provides bootstrapping for CKKS and for the FHEW/CGGI family, not for BGV or BFV — those remain leveled-only in OpenFHE, notwithstanding that BGV/BFV bootstrapping exists in the literature and in HElib. OpenFHE further offers and — distinctively — scheme switching, the ability to move a ciphertext between CKKS and FHEW/TFHE representations mid-computation so that a circuit can use CKKS for its linear-algebra-heavy segments and TFHE-style bootstrapped LUTs for its non-polynomial segments (comparisons, ReLU, argmax) without a round-trip through the client. Certain

Duality Technologies — co-founded by Vinod Vaikuntanathan, one of BGV's co-inventors — is OpenFHE's commercial steward, funding and directing much of its ongoing development. The project has also been a beneficiary of DARPA's DPRIVE program (the TREBUCHET effort, $14.5M) aimed at hardware acceleration of FHE, and Duality has announced a partnership with Oracle targeting government and defense deployments. Python bindings are distributed via pip install openfhe, lowering the barrier for the C++ core considerably. Certain

Gotcha — the Enable(...) capability model

OpenFHE's CryptoContext requires you to explicitly Enable() each capability layer you intend to use (PKE, KEYSWITCH, LEVELEDSHE, ADVANCEDSHE for advanced SHE operations such as EvalSum and EvalPoly, FHE for bootstrapping itself, SCHEMESWITCH) before generating keys or calling the corresponding operations — forgetting the right Enable call is the single most common first-hour stumbling block, producing a runtime error that reads like a missing feature rather than a missing flag. The bundled src/pke/examples/ directory in the current release is the canonical, living API reference; treat any code sample (including the skeletons in this book) as needing a cross-check there before it goes into production. Likely

15.3 Microsoft SEAL — the leveled-only workhorse

SEAL (current: v4.4, July 2026) implements BFV, BGV, and CKKS with no bootstrapping in any scheme — depth is a hard ceiling set entirely by your parameter choice at context creation. It is MIT-licensed, extremely well-tested, and for years was the field's default teaching implementation because its API makes every maintenance operation (relinearize, rescale, mod-switch) an explicit call rather than something hidden inside a wrapper. Treat the bundled native/examples/ as the living reference. Certain

Upgrade if you are pinned below 4.4. The SEAL maintainers flag 4.4 as a critical security update: "All users should upgrade to version 4.4.0 or later as soon as possible," citing fixes for untrusted-input handling, native memory safety, and managed/native interoperability. Anything still pinned to the 4.1 line — as older tutorials and this book's earlier drafts were — should be moved. Note the practical consequence for FHE specifically: untrusted-input handling matters because a server evaluating a ciphertext is by construction processing data it cannot inspect.

15.4 TenSEAL — CKKS at tensor granularity

TenSEAL (v0.3.16, February 2025), maintained by OpenMined, wraps SEAL behind a NumPy-flavored Python API (CKKSVector, CKKSTensor, BFVVector) that hides relinearization and rescaling entirely — arithmetic operators trigger the correct maintenance calls internally. This makes it the fastest path to a working encrypted dot-product or matrix-vector multiply, and the worst path to actually learning scale/level management, since the mechanism Chapter 16 is entirely about is invisible here by design. Certain

15.5 HEaaN — CKKS from its inventors

HEaaN, developed by CryptoLab (South Korea), is a C++ CKKS-only library built by Jung Hee Cheon and collaborators — the original authors of the CKKS scheme itself — and is consequently the reference implementation researchers turn to when validating a CKKS technique's numerical behavior against the scheme's originating team's own engineering choices. It supports bootstrapping, and CryptoLab ships it as part of a small product family: HEaaN Library (the core SDK), HEaaN Private AI (an inference-oriented stack), and HEaaN PQC (post-quantum cryptography tooling). CryptoLab has partnered with Macrogen on genomics workloads and with Niobium on encrypted LLM inference, has an integration path with IBM's HElayers, and has raised on the order of $16M in funding. Certain Documentation is comparatively sparse relative to OpenFHE's — read HEaaN to understand CKKS internals from the source, not as a first production choice unless you're already inside CryptoLab's ecosystem.

15.6 Concrete ML — TFHE without writing cryptography

Concrete ML (v1.8, January 2025), from Zama, sits on top of Zama's Rust-based Concrete TFHE compiler and exposes a scikit-learn-shaped API: linear models, tree ensembles (including XGBoost), and quantization-aware-trained neural networks all compile to an FHE circuit via a single .compile() call, with fhe="simulate" available for fast accuracy dry-runs before paying for real encrypted execution. The binding constraint throughout is TFHE's small accumulator width — roughly a 16-bit integer ceiling on accumulator size — so quantization bit-width (n_bits) is the primary lever engineers tune, and deep or wide networks require careful quantization-aware training (Zama's Brevitas-based QAT tooling) to avoid accumulator overflow at compile time. Recent releases have added a hybrid fine-tuning path for LLMs — running most of the model in the clear and only sensitive layers under FHE — together with a Low Rank Approximation API to keep the encrypted portion's compute tractable. Zama itself has become the field's clearest commercial signal: an FHE-focused unicorn valued above $1B, having raised on the order of $73M plus a further $57M, with a developer community reported above 5,000. Certain

Gotcha — accumulator overflow is a compile-time wall, not a runtime warning

If your network's intermediate sums exceed the supported accumulator bit-width, model.compile() fails outright rather than degrading gracefully. Diagnosing this means inspecting per-layer accumulator size estimates before compiling, and it's the reason tree ensembles (bounded, shallow accumulation) are Concrete ML's sweet spot while deep custom architectures need deliberate QAT design. Certain

15.7 Lattigo — Go-native, multiparty-first

Lattigo v6 (August 2024), maintained by Tune Insight SA, implements BFV, BGV, CKKS, and RGSW along with dedicated multiparty variants of each — a distinguishing feature, since most other libraries treat multiparty/threshold FHE as an afterthought. Being pure Go, it compiles to WebAssembly, making it the natural choice when the deployment target is a browser or a Go-native backend service rather than a C++ toolchain. Certain

15.8 Apple swift-homomorphic-encryption — BFV for on-device PIR

Apple's swift-homomorphic-encryption (August 2024) implements BFV in Swift and is purpose-built for Private Information Retrieval — the specific, narrow application of looking up a value in a server-held database without revealing the query index. It ships as the mechanism behind iOS 18's Live Caller ID Lookup, where the phone needs to check a number against Apple's directory without Apple learning which number was queried. It is a strong example of FHE deployed at genuine consumer scale, but its scope is intentionally narrow (single-scheme, single-application-pattern) rather than a general leveled-computation toolkit. Certain

15.9 Google HEIR — compiler infrastructure, not a runtime

HEIR is an MLIR-based compiler project (ongoing) aiming to do for FHE what LLVM-based ML compilers did for tensor programs: take a high-level, scheme-agnostic description of a computation and lower it through a sequence of FHE-aware dialects to scheme-specific, parameter-selected, optimized code targeting backends like OpenFHE. It exposes a Python frontend for authoring the source programs. Treat HEIR as compiler infrastructure you might target or contribute to, not a library you drop into an application today the way you would OpenFHE or TenSEAL. Certain

15.10 HElib — the historical BGV/CKKS reference, now superseded

HElib (last release v2.3.0, July 2023) was, for years, the reference BGV implementation and an early CKKS implementation; several of its core contributors moved on to build OpenFHE, which has functionally superseded it for new work. Worth reading for its BGV internals and its influence on the field's terminology, but not a first choice for a new project. Certain

15.11 OpenMined — the ecosystem around the libraries

OpenMined is not itself an FHE library but the nonprofit community that maintains TenSEAL alongside PySyft (current: v0.9.5, February 2025), a framework for "Remote Data Science" built around the concept of Datasites — governed remote endpoints that let a researcher run approved computations against data they never directly access, combining FHE with other privacy techniques (differential privacy, secure aggregation) as appropriate to the task. OpenMined is a partner in NAIRR (the National AI Research Resource), backed in part by $35M of NSF funding, positioning it as an access point for privacy-preserving-ML tooling in academic research contexts. Certain

15.12 Choosing among them

Need exact CKKS bootstrapping / scheme switching / production C++?        -> OpenFHE
Learning leveled CKKS/BFV by hand, no bootstrap needed, C++ fine?         -> Microsoft SEAL
Fastest path to encrypted tensor ops, Python, prototyping?                -> TenSEAL
Studying CKKS internals from the scheme's own authors?                    -> HEaaN
ML engineer, want sklearn/XGBoost/NN with zero crypto code?               -> Concrete ML
Go shop, need multiparty FHE or WASM deployment?                          -> Lattigo
Building on-device PIR for an Apple platform?                             -> swift-homomorphic-encryption
Building FHE compiler tooling / multi-backend lowering?                   -> HEIR
Federated / remote-data-science orchestration around FHE?                 -> OpenMined (PySyft)
Artifact — the same encrypted dot product, three libraries

Task: encrypt two length-4 vectors, compute their dot product homomorphically, decrypt. Compare what each library forces you to think about.

TenSEAL (Python, wraps SEAL) — runnable, stable API Certain
import tenseal as ts

ctx = ts.context(ts.SCHEME_TYPE.CKKS, poly_modulus_degree=8192,
                  coeff_mod_bit_sizes=[60, 40, 40, 60])
ctx.global_scale = 2 ** 40
ctx.generate_galois_keys()   # needed internally for the sum-reduction in dot()

a = ts.ckks_vector(ctx, [1.0, 2.0, 3.0, 4.0])
b = ts.ckks_vector(ctx, [5.0, 6.0, 7.0, 8.0])
result = a.dot(b)            # relin + rescale + rotate-and-add, all internal
print(result.decrypt())      # ~ [70.0]

Five lines of actual homomorphic logic. No visible rescale, relinearize, or rotation call — TenSEAL's dot() does a multiply, a relinearize, a rescale, and a $\log_2 4 = 2$-step rotate-and-add tree internally. This is the fastest way to get a correct answer and the worst way to learn what a "level" costs you.

Microsoft SEAL (C++) — SKELETON, verify against native/examples/4_ckks_basics.cpp
// SKELETON — confirm exact signatures against the current SEAL release's examples.
EncryptionParameters parms(scheme_type::ckks);
parms.set_poly_modulus_degree(8192);
parms.set_coeff_modulus(CoeffModulus::Create(8192, {60, 40, 40, 60}));
SEALContext context(parms);

KeyGenerator keygen(context);
auto secret_key = keygen.secret_key();
PublicKey public_key;    keygen.create_public_key(public_key);
RelinKeys relin_keys;    keygen.create_relin_keys(relin_keys);
GaloisKeys galois_keys;  keygen.create_galois_keys(galois_keys);

Encryptor encryptor(context, public_key);
Evaluator evaluator(context);
Decryptor decryptor(context, secret_key);
CKKSEncoder encoder(context);
double scale = pow(2.0, 40);

Plaintext pt_a, pt_b;
encoder.encode(vector{1.0,2.0,3.0,4.0}, scale, pt_a);
encoder.encode(vector{5.0,6.0,7.0,8.0}, scale, pt_b);
Ciphertext ct_a, ct_b; encryptor.encrypt(pt_a, ct_a); encryptor.encrypt(pt_b, ct_b);

Ciphertext ct_prod;
evaluator.multiply(ct_a, ct_b, ct_prod);
evaluator.relinearize_inplace(ct_prod, relin_keys);
evaluator.rescale_to_next_inplace(ct_prod);

// manual rotate-and-add tree to sum the 4 slots
Ciphertext ct_sum = ct_prod;
for (int step = 1; step < 4; step *= 2) {
    Ciphertext rotated;
    evaluator.rotate_vector(ct_sum, step, galois_keys, rotated);
    evaluator.add_inplace(ct_sum, rotated);
}
Plaintext pt_result; decryptor.decrypt(ct_sum, pt_result);
vector result; encoder.decode(pt_result, result);   // result[0] ~ 70.0

Every maintenance step from TenSEAL's hidden dot() is now explicit: the relinearize, the rescale, and the manual rotate-and-add loop implementing the same tree-summation trick used in Chapter 17. This is the exact code TenSEAL runs for you — SEAL just makes you write it.

OpenFHE (C++) — SKELETON, verify against src/pke/examples/ in the current release
// SKELETON — confirm exact signatures against the current openfhe-development examples.
CCParams parameters;
parameters.SetMultiplicativeDepth(1);
parameters.SetScalingModSize(40);
parameters.SetBatchSize(4);
auto cc = GenCryptoContext(parameters);
cc->Enable(PKE); cc->Enable(KEYSWITCH); cc->Enable(LEVELEDSHE);

auto keys = cc->KeyGen();
cc->EvalMultKeyGen(keys.secretKey);
cc->EvalRotateKeyGen(keys.secretKey, {1, 2});   // offsets for the rotate-and-add tree

auto pt_a = cc->MakeCKKSPackedPlaintext(std::vector{1.0,2.0,3.0,4.0});
auto pt_b = cc->MakeCKKSPackedPlaintext(std::vector{5.0,6.0,7.0,8.0});
auto ct_a = cc->Encrypt(keys.publicKey, pt_a);
auto ct_b = cc->Encrypt(keys.publicKey, pt_b);

auto ct_prod = cc->EvalMult(ct_a, ct_b);        // relinearize handled internally
ct_prod = cc->EvalRotateSum(ct_prod, 4);        // [Guessing on exact helper name] — verify;
                                                 // otherwise compose EvalRotate + EvalAdd manually as in SEAL above

Plaintext result; cc->Decrypt(keys.secretKey, ct_prod, &result);
result->SetLength(1);   // ~ 70.0

OpenFHE lands in between: EvalMult auto-relinearizes (unlike SEAL's explicit call), but rotation keys must still be generated per-offset up front, and whether a single "rotate-sum" convenience call exists for your installed version is exactly the kind of API detail this book keeps flagging as Verify rather than asserting — check the current src/pke/examples/ directory before shipping.

Comparison: TenSEAL: ~5 lines, hides everything, best for building intuition and fastest iteration. SEAL: ~25 lines, every maintenance operation explicit, best for learning the mechanism. OpenFHE: ~20 lines, partially automated (relinearization) and partially manual (rotation keys, chain construction), and the only one of the three with bootstrapping and scheme-switching available if the circuit later needs to go deeper than the leveled budget allows. Runtime performance for a single small dot product is dominated by fixed costs (context setup, key generation) in all three; the differences that matter appear at scale, in Chapter 17.

Key Takeaways
  • OpenFHE is the closest thing the field has to a default production choice: broadest scheme coverage, only major library with CKKS bootstrapping and scheme switching, actively funded (DARPA DPRIVE/TREBUCHET, Duality/Oracle).
  • SEAL remains the best teaching implementation for explicit leveled-CKKS mechanics precisely because it automates nothing, but its depth ceiling is hard — no bootstrapping, ever.
  • TenSEAL trades away the scale/level lesson for speed of iteration; use it to build intuition, then graduate to SEAL or OpenFHE to actually manage depth by hand.
  • Concrete ML and TFHE solve a different problem (arbitrary non-polynomial functions via programmable bootstrapping) at a different cost (small integer accumulators) — it is not a CKKS competitor so much as a complementary tool for exactly the non-polynomial ops CKKS can't do natively.
  • Lattigo, Apple's library, and HEIR are not interchangeable with the above — they solve narrower problems (multiparty/Go/WASM, on-device PIR, compiler infrastructure) extremely well within their scope.
Chapter 16

CKKS Scale and Level Management in Practice

Learning Objectives
  • Internalize and apply the three governing rules of CKKS scale/level bookkeeping.
  • Diagnose the three most common CKKS bugs from their symptoms: forgotten rescale, level mismatch across computation-graph branches, precision loss from an undersized scale.
  • Draw and read a level diagram for a computation graph to verify convergence before writing code.
  • Distinguish OpenFHE's four scaling techniques — FIXEDMANUAL, FIXEDAUTO, FLEXIBLEAUTO, FLEXIBLEAUTOEXT — and know which to reach for.

16.1 The three rules

Chapter 14's theory reference already stated the core fact: multiplying two CKKS ciphertexts at scale $\Delta$ produces a result at scale $\Delta^2$, and combining (adding, subtracting, comparing levels for) two ciphertexts requires them to agree on both scale and level. Everything in this chapter is the practice of keeping that invariant true across an entire computation graph, not just a single operation. It compresses into three rules that, if you obey them mechanically, eliminate essentially all CKKS correctness bugs that aren't algorithmic (i.e., not counting "your polynomial approximation is bad outside its fit range" — that's a different failure mode).

Rule 1 — Rescale after every multiply

Immediately after any ciphertext–ciphertext or ciphertext–plaintext multiplication, rescale. This divides the scale back down by (approximately) the last chain prime and drops one level. Skip it and the scale compounds multiplicatively across subsequent multiplies — $\Delta \to \Delta^2 \to \Delta^4 \to \ldots$ — quickly exceeding the numeric range the modulus chain can represent, corrupting the ciphertext irrecoverably (not a graceful error; garbage on decrypt). Certain

Rule 2 — Match scale and level before combining

Before any add/sub/comparison-of-ciphertexts operation, both operands must sit at the identical scale and the identical level (i.e., the same remaining chain prime, at the same position). Two branches of a computation graph that took different numbers of multiplies to reach the combination point will, in general, disagree on level even if their nominal scale looks the same — you must explicitly bring the shallower branch down to match, via a level-drop-only rescale (multiply by an encoded plaintext $1$ at the target level, in libraries that expose this) or, in OpenFHE, via EvalMultConst/Compress-style level-adjustment calls, or by restructuring the graph so both branches multiply the same number of times. Certain

Rule 3 — Know the circuit's depth before choosing parameters

This is Chapter 14's derivation turned into a discipline: draw the computation graph, count the longest chain of sequential multiplies (Rule 2 means you must count the longest branch, not the average), and only then pick the modulus chain length. Picking parameters first and discovering the circuit is one level too deep midway through implementation is the single most common cause of late-stage FHE rewrites. Certain

16.2 Bugs, from symptom back to cause

Bug 1 — Forgotten rescale

Symptom: results are wildly, non-deterministically wrong — not "slightly off," but garbage of an entirely different magnitude — typically appearing a couple of operations after the offending multiply, not at the multiply itself (the corrupted ciphertext still "works" for a step or two before the scale mismatch trips an internal check or the modulus is fully consumed). Cause: a multiply with no following rescale call. Fix: audit every EvalMult/multiply call site for a paired rescale; in libraries with automatic scaling modes (§16.4) this class of bug is structurally eliminated at some cost in manual control.

Bug 2 — Level mismatch from divergent computation-graph branches

Symptom: a specific add or sub call throws (in libraries that check) or silently produces nonsense (in libraries that don't), and the two operands, when inspected, are one or more levels apart. Cause: one branch of the graph — commonly a "skip connection" or a residual path in a neural-network-shaped circuit, or a bias term added after fewer multiplies than the main path — reaches the combination point having consumed fewer levels than the other branch. Fix: insert an explicit level-drop (a no-op rescale against the shallow branch, sometimes literally implemented as multiplying by an encoded constant $1.0$ at the deeper branch's level) so both operands arrive at the same level before combining. This is exactly the class of bug residual/skip connections introduce in encrypted neural network inference, and it is worth drawing the level diagram (§16.3) specifically at every point your graph has more than one incoming edge.

Bug 3 — Precision loss from an undersized scale

Symptom: the circuit runs to completion, decrypts without error, and produces numbers in the right ballpark but wrong in the third or fourth significant figure — often not caught until downstream accuracy metrics degrade, because nothing in the pipeline raised an exception. Cause: $\Delta$ set too small for the required precision, or a modulus chain where the scale primes don't closely match $\Delta$ (so each rescale doesn't cleanly divide out a full factor of $\Delta$, and rounding error accumulates level over level). Fix: raise $\Delta$ (at a cost of one more bit of $\log_2 q$ per level, feeding back into Chapter 14's security/performance tradeoff), and make sure each scale-tier prime in the chain is sized to match $\Delta$ almost exactly, not just "close enough." This bug is the reason a correct-looking decrypted output is not sufficient evidence of a correct implementation — always check against a plaintext reference computation, not just "did it run."

16.3 Level diagrams

Before writing a nontrivial CKKS circuit, draw it as a DAG annotated with the level each edge sits at, starting from level $L$ (the top of the chain, freshly encrypted) and counting down by one at every multiply. This single diagram catches both Bug 2 (any node with incoming edges at different levels is a bug, visible by inspection) and gives you the number in Rule 3 directly (the diagram's minimum level reached is the depth $D$ your parameters must support).

Level (remaining primes)
  7  x  (fresh input, batch-encoded)
  6  Linear1(x)                         <- 1 mult (plaintext weights)
  4  Act1 = a*h^3 + b*h                 <- 2 mults (h^2, then h^2*h), level 6->4
  3  Linear2(Act1)                      <- 1 mult
  1  Act2 = a*h2^3 + b*h2                <- 2 mults, level 3->1
  0  Linear3(Act2)                      <- 1 mult, final output at level 0

  Longest path: 7 -> 0, depth D = 7.  Matches the Chapter 14 artifact's
  7-prime scale chain exactly -- the diagram IS the parameter derivation,
  read the other direction.

If any two edges feeding the same node land on different rows of this diagram, that is Bug 2 waiting to happen — resolve it on paper before it happens in a debugger.

16.4 OpenFHE's scaling techniques

OpenFHE exposes the scale-management discipline itself as a configurable strategy, via CCParams::SetScalingTechnique, with four (five, counting the degenerate no-op) selectable modes. Verify exact enum names and default behavior against the current openfhe-development headers (ScalingTechnique in the scheme parameter sources) before relying on the specifics below in production code — this is precisely the volatile-API territory the fhe-coach discipline flags for verification, though the conceptual distinctions between the modes are stable and worth understanding regardless of exact naming.

ModeRescale behaviorWho tracks scaleWhen to use
FIXEDMANUALYou call rescale explicitly at each point you chooseYou, by handMaximum control; learning; matching a hand-drawn level diagram exactly
FIXEDAUTORescale triggered automatically after each multiplyLibrary, using a uniform per-level scale factorRemoves Bug 1 structurally; simplest automatic mode
FLEXIBLEAUTOAutomatic, with per-level scale correctionLibrary, adaptively adjusting the effective scale at each level to reduce driftRecommended default for most CKKS work — better numerical stability than FIXEDAUTO at comparable cost
FLEXIBLEAUTOEXTAutomatic, with an additional extended precision correctionLibrary, using auxiliary modulus/scale bookkeeping for further precision retentionVery deep circuits or bootstrapping-adjacent workloads where FLEXIBLEAUTO's drift still costs too much precision

The through-line: as you move down this table, the library takes over more of Rules 1 and 2 for you, at the cost of less visibility into exactly when and how rescales happen — useful once you trust the mechanism, actively harmful while you're still building intuition for it (which is why this chapter's artifact below uses FIXEDMANUAL-style explicit calls even though a real deployment would likely choose FLEXIBLEAUTO). Likely

Artifact — 3-layer NN inference in OpenFHE with explicit scale/level management, and a deliberate bug

Building directly on Chapter 14's derived parameters ($N=16384$, chain [60,40,40,40,40,40,40,40,60]$, $\Delta=2^{40}$, depth 7) and the level diagram in §16.3.

Correct version (SKELETON — verify signatures)
// SKELETON -- verify against current openfhe-development CKKS examples.
CCParams parameters;
parameters.SetMultiplicativeDepth(7);
parameters.SetScalingModSize(40);
parameters.SetScalingTechnique(FIXEDMANUAL);   // explicit control for this walkthrough
auto cc = GenCryptoContext(parameters);
cc->Enable(PKE); cc->Enable(KEYSWITCH); cc->Enable(LEVELEDSHE);
auto keys = cc->KeyGen();
cc->EvalMultKeyGen(keys.secretKey);

// Linear1: plaintext-matrix x ciphertext-vector via diagonal method (Ch.17)
auto h1 = EvalLinear(cc, keys, x_ct, W1_diag);      // 1 mult
h1 = cc->Rescale(h1);                               // RULE 1 -- level 7 -> 6

// Act1: degree-3 poly, a*h^3 + b*h
auto h1_sq = cc->EvalMult(h1, h1);  h1_sq = cc->Rescale(h1_sq);   // level 6 -> 5
auto h1_cu = cc->EvalMult(h1_sq, h1); h1_cu = cc->Rescale(h1_cu); // level 5 -> 4
// NOTE: h1 (level 6) and h1_cu (level 4) are now at DIFFERENT levels --
// combining a*h1_cu + b*h1 directly would hit Bug 2. Drop h1 to level 4:
auto h1_matched = cc->LevelReduce(h1, nullptr, 2);   // [Verify exact API] drop 2 levels to match
auto act1 = cc->EvalAdd(cc->EvalMult(h1_cu, coeff_a), cc->EvalMult(h1_matched, coeff_b));
// act1 now correctly at level 4, matching the diagram

// Linear2, Act2, Linear3 follow the identical pattern -- omitted for brevity,
// terminating at level 0 exactly as the §16.3 diagram predicts.
Deliberately buggy version — forgotten rescale (Bug 1)
// BUGGY -- the missing Rescale after h1_sq is the injected bug.
auto h1_sq = cc->EvalMult(h1, h1);
// <-- missing: h1_sq = cc->Rescale(h1_sq);
auto h1_cu = cc->EvalMult(h1_sq, h1);    // multiplies a Delta^2-scale ciphertext
                                          // by a Delta-scale one -> result at Delta^3,
                                          // NOT Delta as every other level assumes.
h1_cu = cc->Rescale(h1_cu);              // divides out only one Delta -- residual Delta^2
                                          // scale corruption propagates from here on.
// Symptom: act1 decrypts to values off by roughly a factor of Delta (~10^12),
// or the run aborts inside a later rescale once the scale drifts outside the
// chain's remaining primes -- exactly Bug 1's signature from §16.2.

The fix is a one-line insertion (the commented-out Rescale call) — the diagnostic value of this exercise is in recognizing the symptom (values off by a power of $\Delta$, or a hard failure a level or two downstream) and tracing it back to a missing rescale rather than assuming the polynomial approximation or the parameters are at fault.

Key Takeaways
  • Three rules cover essentially all CKKS correctness bugs: rescale after every multiply, match scale and level before combining, know your depth before choosing parameters.
  • Skip connections and multi-branch computation graphs are the most common source of level mismatches in practice — draw the level diagram at every merge point.
  • A decrypted result that "looks reasonable" is not proof of correctness — precision-loss bugs from an undersized $\Delta$ pass silently and only show up as downstream accuracy degradation.
  • OpenFHE's FIXEDMANUAL → FIXEDAUTO → FLEXIBLEAUTO → FLEXIBLEAUTOEXT progression trades explicit control for automatic (and increasingly numerically careful) scale bookkeeping; learn on manual, deploy on flexible-auto.
Chapter 17

Performance Profiling and Optimization

Learning Objectives
  • Know where time and memory actually go in a CKKS pipeline, in relative order of magnitude.
  • Apply the three standard depth/operation-count reduction techniques: Paterson–Stockmeyer for polynomial evaluation, baby-step giant-step (BSGS) for linear transforms, and tree reduction for slot summation.
  • Reason about ciphertext and key memory footprints across ring dimensions, and generate only the rotation keys you actually need.
  • Understand what Intel HEXL contributes and where to find it in the major libraries.
  • Know FHERMA as a venue for community-optimized FHE components.

17.1 Where the time goes

Profile any nontrivial CKKS pipeline and the cost breaks down, in rough descending order, as: homomorphic multiplication (ciphertext–ciphertext, dominant — each one is a polynomial multiply plus a relinearization, both $O(N\log N)$ per RNS prime, repeated across the whole modulus chain), rotation (a key-switch operation, comparable in cost to a multiply, and the second-largest line item in any circuit that does linear algebra or cross-slot reduction), encryption and decryption (cheap relative to the above — single-digit operations, not accumulated across a deep circuit), and key generation (a one-time setup cost, potentially the single most expensive operation you run in a session if you generate a full rotation-key set, but amortized across every subsequent operation in that session). Bootstrapping, where used, dwarfs everything else per call — commonly one to two orders of magnitude more expensive than a single multiply, which is precisely why leveled-circuit design (Chapter 16) is preferred whenever the depth budget allows avoiding it. Likely — exact latencies are hardware-, parameter-, and library-version-dependent; treat any specific millisecond figure as an order-of-magnitude guide to be re-measured on your own target hardware, not a portable constant.

17.2 Depth and operation-count reduction

Paterson–Stockmeyer for polynomial evaluation

Evaluating a degree-$d$ polynomial by naive Horner's method costs $d$ sequential multiplies — and, worse for CKKS, $d$ sequential levels, since each multiply-then-rescale consumes one. The Paterson–Stockmeyer algorithm restructures the evaluation using a baby-step/giant-step decomposition of the polynomial itself: write the degree-$d$ polynomial as a sum of $\sqrt{d}$-sized blocks, precompute the "baby step" powers $x, x^2, \ldots, x^{\lceil\sqrt{d}\rceil}$ (needed once, in parallel, at low depth via repeated squaring — itself only $O(\log \sqrt d)$ levels), and combine them against precomputed "giant step" coefficient groups. The result is $O(\sqrt{d})$ total multiplications and, more importantly for CKKS, a multiplicative depth of roughly $O(\log d)$ rather than $O(d)$ — a linear-to-logarithmic improvement in the resource that directly determines your parameter chain length. (Naive Horner's depth is linear in the degree, not exponential; the saving is real but it is $O(d) \to O(\log d)$.) Certain This is the standard technique behind every production library's "evaluate this Chebyshev/minimax polynomial approximation of sigmoid/GELU/etc." routine (e.g. OpenFHE's Chebyshev polynomial evaluation utilities use exactly this decomposition internally).

Baby-step giant-step (BSGS) for rotations

Encrypted matrix-vector multiplication via the diagonal method (encode each diagonal of the weight matrix as a plaintext, multiply against rotated copies of the input vector, sum) naively needs one rotation per diagonal — $O(N)$ rotations for an $N \times N$ matrix, each rotation itself a full key-switch. The BSGS restructuring of the diagonal method — grouping diagonals into $\sqrt{N}$ "giant step" blocks each requiring one rotation, with $\sqrt{N}$ "baby step" rotations shared across all blocks — reduces the total rotation count from $O(N)$ to $O(\sqrt{N})$, at the cost of needing both the baby-step and giant-step rotation offsets available as Galois keys. This is the single highest-leverage optimization for any encrypted linear-algebra-heavy circuit (which is to say, essentially every encrypted neural network layer) and is exactly the technique implicitly assumed by the EvalLinear call in Chapter 16's artifact. Certain

Tree reduction for summation

Summing all $n$ slots of a packed ciphertext (needed, e.g., to finish a dot product, or to reduce across a Linear layer's output before an activation) via $n-1$ sequential rotate-and-add steps is the naive approach; the tree (doubling) reduction instead rotates by $1, 2, 4, 8, \ldots$ and adds at each step, fully summing $n$ slots in $\lceil \log_2 n \rceil$ rotations. This is exactly the loop shown explicitly in Chapter 15's SEAL dot-product artifact, and it generalizes directly: any "reduce across slots" operation in a CKKS pipeline should default to the doubling pattern unless a specific packing layout makes a different reduction cheaper. Certain

17.3 Memory: ciphertexts and keys

A CKKS ciphertext at RNS level with $k$ remaining primes consists of two ring elements (the standard $(c_0, c_1)$ pair), each a length-$N$ vector of $k$ machine words (one word per RNS prime, since each coefficient is CRT-represented). Concretely: at $N=2^{13}$ with a modest chain, a ciphertext runs on the order of a few hundred kilobytes; by $N=2^{16}$ with a long chain (deep circuits, or bootstrapping-capable parameter sets that need many primes), individual ciphertexts can reach the tens of megabytes. Likely — the exact multiplier depends on your library's storage format (some store both the "extended" and "base" RNS bases during certain operations) and should be measured directly with your chosen parameter set rather than assumed.

Keys are the more dangerous line item because they're easy to under-budget: a relinearization key is stored as a gadget-decomposed set of ciphertext-sized components (the decomposition needed for numerically stable key-switching multiplies its raw size by the decomposition's digit count), and a Galois (rotation) key is the same size as a relinearization key, generated separately for every distinct rotation offset your circuit uses. A circuit that naively generates rotation keys for every offset from $1$ to $N/2$ "just in case" can produce a rotation-key set in the gigabytes, dwarfing every ciphertext in the pipeline combined. Certain

Practical rule — generate only the rotation offsets you actually use

Enumerate every distinct rotation offset your circuit calls (including the offsets implied by BSGS's baby-step/giant-step decomposition, §17.2) and pass exactly that set to your library's Galois/rotation-key generation call — in OpenFHE, the explicit offset list argument to EvalRotateKeyGen. Generating "all rotations" or a broad power-of-two superset when only a handful of BSGS-derived offsets are needed is the most common source of unexpectedly large key material and slow startup in an otherwise well-optimized pipeline. Certain

17.4 Intel HEXL: hardware acceleration underneath the libraries

Intel HEXL is a hardware-optimized backend library implementing the core RNS-CKKS/BFV/BGV number-theoretic primitives — the NTT and modular multiplication that dominate an FHE library's inner loop — accelerated with AVX-512, exploiting the IFMA and VBMI2 instruction set extensions available on recent server-class Intel silicon. It is not a standalone FHE library; it is a drop-in acceleration layer, integrated as an optional backend into OpenFHE, Microsoft SEAL, and HElib, typically enabled via a CMake build flag that links HEXL in place of the library's default (portable, scalar or lighter-SIMD) arithmetic backend. Reported speedups on the accelerated primitives are on the order of 2–5x, with the actual end-to-end circuit speedup depending on what fraction of total runtime those primitives occupy for your specific workload (multiply-heavy circuits benefit the most; rotation- or overhead-dominated small circuits less so). Certain If you are deploying on Intel server hardware and haven't checked whether your build links HEXL, that is very likely the single highest-leverage, zero-algorithmic-change performance change available to you.

17.5 FHERMA — community-optimized components

FHERMA is a competition-based platform — developed and run by Fair Math together with the OpenFHE community (see fherma.io and the fairmath/fherma-challenges repository; Fair Math also publishes the resulting component library as polycircuit) — where participants submit optimized implementations of reusable FHE building blocks (comparison, sign evaluation, argmax, and other components that are individually hard to make fast and are needed repeatedly across encrypted-ML pipelines) and the best submissions are benchmarked and made available as reference implementations. Certain For an engineer optimizing a real pipeline, FHERMA is worth checking before hand-rolling a component like comparison-under-CKKS from scratch — there is a reasonable chance a competition-tuned version already exists and outperforms a first-pass implementation by a wide margin, since these are exactly the components where Paterson–Stockmeyer-style depth reduction and BSGS-style operation-count reduction have already been pushed hard by specialists.

Artifact — profiling Chapter 16's NN inference and applying Paterson–Stockmeyer

Setup. Take the 3-layer NN from Chapter 16's artifact and instrument it: wrap each EvalMult, Rescale, and rotation call with timers, run one inference, and tabulate.

Stage                  Mults   Rotations   Levels used   Rel. time
Linear1 (BSGS diag)      1        ~2*sqrt(k1)     1          low
Act1 (Horner, deg 3)     2        0               2          medium
Linear2 (BSGS diag)      1        ~2*sqrt(k2)     1          low
Act2 (Horner, deg 3)     2        0               2          medium
Linear3 (BSGS diag)      1        ~2*sqrt(k3)     1          low
                                                   -----
                                            total: 7 levels

Observation. The two activation evaluations account for 4 of the 7 total levels (well over half the circuit's depth budget) despite being individually "small" polynomials — this is the expected profile: multiplication dominates, and in a network with many layers, repeated low-degree polynomial activations compound into the majority of total depth consumed, even though each one looks cheap in isolation.

Optimization. Replace the naive-Horner degree-3 evaluation with its Paterson–Stockmeyer form. For $d=3$ specifically the naive form is already near-optimal ($\lceil \log_2 3\rceil \approx 2$ levels either way), so the real payoff appears when the activation's polynomial-approximation degree is pushed higher for better accuracy — e.g. a degree-15 minimax fit to GELU. Naive Horner at $d=15$: 14 sequential multiplies, 14 levels — likely exceeding the entire security-budgeted chain from Chapter 14 on its own. Paterson–Stockmeyer at $d=15$: baby-step powers up to $\lceil\sqrt{15}\rceil = 4$ (computed in $\lceil\log_2 4\rceil = 2$ levels via repeated squaring: $x,x^2,x^4$), giant-step combination adding roughly $\lceil\log_2\sqrt{15}\rceil \approx 2$ more levels — landing around 4–5 total levels versus Horner's 14, an approximately 3x depth reduction that turns an infeasible activation into one that comfortably fits inside a modest chain, at the cost of allowing a much better polynomial fit (and therefore better downstream model accuracy) for the same depth budget the naive degree-3 approximation was using.

Result: depth reduction from Paterson–Stockmeyer doesn't just save time on a fixed approximation — it changes what approximation degree is affordable at all, which is usually the more valuable outcome: the same 7-level depth budget from Chapter 14 that only fit two degree-3 activations naively can, restructured this way, fit two much higher-degree (hence more accurate) activations instead.

Key Takeaways
  • Multiplication (and the relinearization/rotation key-switches riding alongside it) dominates CKKS runtime; keygen is a one-time amortized cost; bootstrapping, when used, dwarfs everything else per call.
  • Paterson–Stockmeyer turns $O(d)$-depth polynomial evaluation into $O(\log d)$-depth — the single highest-leverage technique for affording higher-accuracy polynomial activations inside a fixed level budget.
  • BSGS turns $O(N)$-rotation linear transforms into $O(\sqrt N)$-rotation ones; tree reduction turns $O(n)$-rotation slot summation into $O(\log n)$ — both are default-assume techniques for any production encrypted linear algebra.
  • Rotation/Galois keys are the most easily over-provisioned memory cost in a pipeline — generate exactly the offsets your (BSGS-restructured) circuit uses, nothing more.
  • Intel HEXL is free (AVX-512-driven) 2–5x acceleration on the NTT/modmul inner loop, available as a build flag in OpenFHE, SEAL, and HElib — check whether your build links it before optimizing anything algorithmic.
  • FHERMA is a standing venue for community-tuned reference implementations of hard-to-optimize FHE components (comparison, sign, argmax); check it before hand-rolling one from scratch.
Part IV

Privacy-Preserving Machine Learning

Bridging the gap between encrypted computation and practical ML inference, training, and federated systems.
Chapter 18

The Non-Polynomial Barrier

Learning Objectives
  • State precisely what CKKS and BFV can and cannot compute, and why the boundary is exactly "polynomials of the ciphertext."
  • Identify every place a standard neural network forward pass leaves the polynomial world.
  • Enumerate the three engineering strategies used in practice to cross the non-polynomial barrier, and the cost each one pays.
  • Reproduce, numerically, why a degree-4 polynomial fit to $\mathrm{ReLU}$ or $\sigma$ looks fine on paper and fails catastrophically outside its fitting interval.

Every chapter so far has been building toward a single uncomfortable fact, which we now state without euphemism: CKKS and BFV cannot evaluate a neural network. Not "cannot evaluate it efficiently" — cannot evaluate it at all, as written. A ResNet block, a transformer layer, even a two-layer MLP with a $\mathrm{ReLU}$ in the middle, is not a function these schemes can homomorphically apply to a ciphertext. This chapter makes precise why that is true, shows you the failure with your own eyes, and lays out the three strategies the field uses to route around it — strategies that the rest of Part IV will each develop in depth.

18.1 What "only polynomials" actually means

Definition 18.1 — The native operation set of a leveled RLWE scheme

For a leveled scheme $\mathcal{S} \in \{\text{BFV}, \text{BGV}, \text{CKKS}\}$, the set of functions homomorphically evaluable on a ciphertext without leaving the scheme is exactly the set of functions expressible as a finite composition of:

$$\text{Eval}(\mathsf{ct}) \;=\; \sum_{i} c_i \prod_j \mathsf{ct}_{j} \quad \text{(a polynomial in the encrypted inputs, with plaintext coefficients } c_i\text{)}$$

with degree bounded by the multiplicative depth budget $L$ that the parameters admit (Chapter 4 §3). Every $\mathsf{EvalAdd}$ is an addition of coefficients; every $\mathsf{EvalMult}$ of two ciphertexts adds their degrees (so squaring doubles the degree) while consuming one level — which is exactly why a depth-$L$ budget admits polynomials of degree up to $2^L$ rather than merely $L$. Multiplying by a plaintext leaves the degree in the encrypted variable unchanged, but note that in CKKS it still consumes a level, because the scale must be rescaled afterwards. There is no primitive — none, in any of these three schemes — that reads the sign, the magnitude, or the rank of an encrypted value and branches on it.

This is not an implementation gap that a future library release will close. It is a structural consequence of what makes the scheme homomorphic in the first place: the ciphertext is (up to noise) a codeword for evaluating a fixed polynomial relation over the ring $R_q = \mathbb{Z}_q[X]/(X^N+1)$, and decryption recovers exactly the polynomial identity the prover set up. Certain A comparison $x > 0$, a maximum $\max(x, 0)$, a division $x/y$, an exponential $e^x$ — over the reals none of these is a polynomial of any finite degree, so in CKKS, whose plaintexts are real/complex, they are genuinely unreachable by any sequence of $\mathsf{EvalAdd}$/$\mathsf{EvalMult}$ operations.

One qualification, because it matters for scheme choice. In BFV and BGV the plaintext space is the finite ring $\mathbb{Z}_t$, and every function on a finite domain is exactly interpolated by some polynomial (Lagrange interpolation; over $\mathbb{F}_t$ one can also use Fermat's little theorem to write an equality test as $1-(x-y)^{t-1}$). So comparison and sign are not impossible in BFV/BGV — they are impossibly expensive, requiring degree on the order of $t$ and therefore depth on the order of $\log_2 t$, which is why practitioners reach for TFHE or scheme switching instead of interpolating. The barrier in the finite-plaintext schemes is economic; in CKKS it is structural. Both lead to the same engineering conclusion, and the rest of this chapter treats them together.

TFHE is the one member of the FHE family that breaks this rule, and it does so by changing what the "native operation" is. Its programmable bootstrapping (PBS) primitive homomorphically evaluates an arbitrary lookup table as part of noise refresh (Chapter 9). That is a genuinely different computational model — table lookup over a small domain rather than ring arithmetic over a large one — and it is why Chapters 20 and 22 spend so much time on Concrete-ML rather than treating TFHE as a footnote.

18.2 Where the barrier bites a neural network

Write the forward pass of a generic feed-forward network as an alternation:

$$h^{(0)} = x, \qquad z^{(l)} = W^{(l)} h^{(l-1)} + b^{(l)}, \qquad h^{(l)} = \phi\big(z^{(l)}\big)$$

The affine map $z^{(l)} = W^{(l)}h^{(l-1)} + b^{(l)}$ is exactly a polynomial of degree 1 in the encrypted activations — a matrix-vector product is a sum of scalar products, and $\mathsf{EvalMultPlain}$ against the (plaintext, known) weight matrix followed by $\mathsf{EvalAdd}$ implements it directly, at the cost of one multiplicative level per matmul (fewer, if the weights are baked into rotate-and-sum plaintext masks — see Chapter 21). CKKS is, genuinely, an excellent fit for this half of the network.

It is the activation $\phi$ that breaks the pattern, and it breaks it everywhere in the modern deep learning toolbox:

FunctionWhere it appearsWhy it isn't a polynomial
$\mathrm{ReLU}(x) = \max(x,0)$CNNs, MLPspiecewise-linear kink — a sign test
$\sigma(x) = 1/(1+e^{-x})$logistic output, gatestranscendental ($e^x$)
$\tanh(x)$RNN/LSTM gatestranscendental
$\mathrm{softmax}(x)_i = e^{x_i}/\sum_j e^{x_j}$attention, classification headstranscendental + division
$\mathrm{GELU}(x) = x\Phi(x)$transformers (BERT, GPT, ViT)Gaussian CDF, transcendental
$\max(a,b)$, $\arg\max$max-pooling, classification decisionscomparison
$a/b$normalization layers, attention scalingnot polynomial in $b$ (no encrypted reciprocal)

Note what is in the polynomial world already: $\phi(x) = x^2$ (the "square" activation used by CryptoNets and several follow-ups) is trivially a degree-2 polynomial and needs no approximation at all — one $\mathsf{EvalMult}$ per activation, exact up to CKKS's usual numerical noise. We will lean on this fact repeatedly in Chapter 21; it is the one activation function CKKS was, in a sense, made for.

The bottleneck is not the linear algebra

A common misconception among newcomers to encrypted ML is that matrix multiplication is the hard part of encrypted inference — it feels like the computationally heavy piece, so intuition says it must be the FHE-hard piece too. The opposite is true. Matmuls are depth-1 (or depth-log if you chain layers without folding), well-understood, and highly optimized via rotation-based packing (Chapter 21). The activation functions are one-line to write and yet are the entire reason encrypted deep learning is a research area rather than a solved engineering problem. Every design decision in Part IV — network architecture choices, quantization strategy, scheme selection — is downstream of "what do we do about $\phi$."

18.3 Three ways across the barrier

Given that $\phi$ cannot be evaluated as written, there are exactly three families of workaround in use today, and essentially every encrypted-ML system in production or in the literature is a point in this design space.

Strategy 1 — Polynomial approximation, stay in CKKS

Replace $\phi$ with a polynomial $p(x)$ that approximates it well over the range the pre-activations actually take. Evaluate $p$ with $\mathsf{EvalMult}$/$\mathsf{EvalAdd}$ exactly like the linear layers. This keeps the whole network inside a single CKKS circuit — no scheme boundary to cross — at the cost of (a) approximation error that compounds across layers, and (b) additional multiplicative depth, since a degree-$d$ polynomial costs roughly $O(\log d)$ levels via Paterson–Stockmeyer evaluation (Chapter 19, Chapter 21 §5) rather than $d$. This is the strategy behind CryptoNets and its many descendants, and it is the subject of Chapter 19 in full.

Strategy 2 — TFHE / programmable bootstrapping, evaluate exact LUTs

Quantize activations to small integers (typically 2–8 bits) and evaluate $\phi$ exactly as a lookup table via PBS. Every non-linear function — $\mathrm{ReLU}$, sign, comparison, even $\arg\max$ — becomes free in the sense that PBS doesn't care what function it's looking up, only how large the domain is. The cost moves elsewhere: PBS latency per activation (roughly milliseconds per scalar on CPU as of Concrete's current releases Likely), and the discipline of keeping the *linear* accumulations within the bit-width the PBS input domain supports — the accumulator-overflow problem that Chapter 22 is entirely devoted to. Concrete-ML is the production embodiment of this strategy.

Strategy 3 — Scheme switching, CKKS for linear layers + FHEW/TFHE for activations

Run the affine layers in CKKS (fast, SIMD-packed, float-native), convert the ciphertext to an LWE/FHEW representation right before the non-linearity, evaluate the activation exactly via PBS, convert back to CKKS, and continue. OpenFHE implements this as an experimental capability (`CKKS ⇄ FHEW` scheme switching Verify — API surface is still evolving; consult `SCHEME_SWITCHING_CAPABILITY.md` in the current `openfhe-development` tree before writing production code against it). This is the "best of both worlds" strategy on paper — exact activations, SIMD linear algebra — and the "hardest of both worlds" in engineering practice, since you now own the correctness of two schemes and the conversion boundary between them.

The tradeoff in one line

Strategy 1 trades accuracy for staying in one scheme. Strategy 2 trades range/precision (small integers only) for exactness of the non-linearity. Strategy 3 trades engineering complexity for getting the best properties of both. There is no strategy that avoids paying somewhere — this is the central organizing fact of Part IV, and every chapter that follows is really an elaboration of one cell in this trade space.

18.4 The depth–accuracy tradeoff, and why range is everything

Observation 18.2 — Approximation quality is a statement about an interval, not a function

A polynomial $p(x)$ of degree $d$ that approximates $\phi(x)$ well on $[-R, R]$ says nothing about $\phi$ outside that interval — and typically diverges from it badly and fast, since $p$ eventually grows like $|x|^d$ while $\sigma$ and $\tanh$ are bounded and $\mathrm{ReLU}$ is only linear. The single most important design decision in polynomial activation replacement is choosing $R$ correctly — tight enough that the fit is accurate where your data actually lives, loose enough that outlier pre-activations (which will occur, especially early in training-time calibration or on out-of-distribution inputs) don't land the network in the divergent region.

This is why architecture adaptation for encrypted inference (Chapter 21) always includes a calibration pass: run the plaintext network over a representative sample, record the empirical range of every pre-activation tensor, and fit each polynomial to that range — not to $(-\infty, \infty)$, which no finite-degree polynomial can cover, and not to some textbook default like $[-4,4]$ that may be wildly wrong for your particular trained weights.

18.5 Artifact — watching the barrier fail, then approximating across it

Artifact 18.A — ReLU and sigmoid in CKKS: the direct attempt, and two polynomial rescues

Step 1 confirms the barrier by trying the naive thing. Step 2–3 fit and evaluate low- and higher-degree polynomial replacements and show the accuracy/range tradeoff numerically — the full machinery (Chebyshev bases, Paterson–Stockmeyer depth counting) arrives in Chapter 19; here we use the crudest possible least-squares fit on purpose, so the failure mode is visible before the fix is sophisticated.

# tag: verify — TenSEAL's public API is stable, but confirm ts.CKKSVector method names
# against the installed tenseal.__version__ (target: 0.3.16) before relying on this verbatim.
import numpy as np
import tenseal as ts

# Depth budget: Horner evaluation of a degree-d polynomial performs d
# sequential multiplications, so we need d spendable levels -- 8 here,
# because Step 3 goes to degree 8. The number of spendable levels is
# len(coeff_mod_bit_sizes) - 2 (the first and last primes are the special
# and key-switching primes, not levels).
#
# Sizing the ring: this chain totals 60 + 35*8 + 60 = 400 bits. SEAL caps
# the coefficient modulus at 218 bits for N=8192 and 438 bits for N=16384
# at 128-bit security, so N=8192 would raise "encryption parameters are not
# set correctly" and N=16384 is the smallest ring that fits. Note we drop
# the scale to 2**35 to make the 8-level chain fit under that 438-bit cap.
ctx = ts.context(
    ts.SCHEME_TYPE.CKKS,
    poly_modulus_degree=16384,
    coeff_mod_bit_sizes=[60] + [35] * 8 + [60],
)
ctx.global_scale = 2 ** 35
ctx.generate_galois_keys()

x_plain = np.linspace(-6, 6, 16)
enc_x = ts.ckks_vector(ctx, x_plain.tolist())

# --- Step 1: the direct attempt -------------------------------------------
# There is no ts.CKKSVector.relu() or .sigmoid(). This is not a missing
# convenience method -- it is the barrier from Definition 18.1. The only
# operations exposed are +, -, *, matmul, and dot, because those are the
# only ones the scheme can express. Uncommenting the next line is a
# TypeError / AttributeError, not a slow computation:
# enc_relu = enc_x.relu()          # <-- does not exist, and cannot exist

# --- Step 2: degree-4 least-squares polynomial replacement for ReLU -------
def relu(x): return np.maximum(x, 0.0)

R = 6.0                                   # fitting range, cf. Observation 18.2
xs = np.linspace(-R, R, 400)
coeffs4 = np.polyfit(xs, relu(xs), deg=4)          # highest power first
poly4 = np.poly1d(coeffs4)

def eval_poly_ckks(enc_vec, coeffs_desc):
    """Horner's method homomorphically: degree-d costs d sequential EvalMult."""
    c = coeffs_desc
    acc = enc_vec * c[0] + c[1]
    for ci in c[2:]:
        acc = acc * enc_vec + ci     # enc_vec, not the global enc_x
    return acc

enc_relu4 = eval_poly_ckks(enc_x, coeffs4.tolist())
approx4 = np.array(enc_relu4.decrypt())

print("max abs error, degree 4, in-range [-6,6]:",
      np.max(np.abs(approx4 - relu(x_plain))))

# --- Step 3: degree-8 fit, and the out-of-range divergence ----------------
coeffs8 = np.polyfit(xs, relu(xs), deg=8)
enc_relu8 = eval_poly_ckks(ts.ckks_vector(ctx, x_plain.tolist()), coeffs8.tolist())
approx8 = np.array(enc_relu8.decrypt())
print("max abs error, degree 8, in-range [-6,6]:",
      np.max(np.abs(approx8 - relu(x_plain))))

x_outlier = np.array([9.0, -9.0])          # outside the fitting range R=6
p8 = np.poly1d(coeffs8)
print("degree-8 poly at x=9 (true ReLU=9):", p8(9.0))   # diverges hard
print("degree-8 poly at x=-9 (true ReLU=0):", p8(-9.0)) # diverges hard

Running this, degree-4 lands around $1.8\times10^{-1}$ max error on the sampled points in $[-6,6]$ and degree-8 around $9\times10^{-2}$ — a factor of about two, not the order-of-magnitude gain one might expect. That modest return is itself the lesson: ReLU has a corner at the origin, and no polynomial approximates a corner efficiently, so throwing degree at the problem yields sharply diminishing returns (Chapter 19 quantifies this properly; a minimax fit does better than the least-squares fit used here, but the corner still dominates). The cost of that factor of two is steep: as written, Horner performs one sequential multiplication per degree, so degree 8 spends 8 of the chain's levels against degree 4's 4. Evaluated instead via Paterson–Stockmeyer (Chapter 19 §5, Chapter 21 §5), the same degree-8 polynomial needs only $\lceil \log_2 9 \rceil = 4$ levels versus $\lceil \log_2 5\rceil = 3$ for degree 4 — which is exactly why production code never evaluates a high-degree polynomial by naive Horner. The out-of-range evaluation is the important part of the exercise: $p_8(9)$ is not "somewhat wrong," it is wildly wrong, often by an order of magnitude, because an unconstrained degree-8 polynomial is dominated by its leading term far outside the fitting interval. This is Observation 18.2 made concrete, and it is the reason Chapter 19 spends as much time on where you fit as on how you fit.

Key Takeaways
  • CKKS and BFV evaluate polynomials of encrypted inputs only — no comparisons, no branches, no exact division, no transcendental functions. This is structural, not a missing feature.
  • Neural networks alternate polynomial layers (linear/affine — CKKS-native) with non-polynomial layers (activations — CKKS-hostile). The activation is the entire bottleneck of encrypted deep learning.
  • Three strategies exist to cross the barrier: polynomial approximation (stay in CKKS, pay accuracy + depth), TFHE/PBS (exact LUTs, pay bit-width and per-op latency), and scheme switching (best of both, pay engineering complexity).
  • Polynomial approximation quality is meaningless without specifying the fitting range; a well-fit low-degree polynomial in-range beats a high-degree polynomial evaluated out-of-range, every time.
Chapter 19

Polynomial Activation Approximation

Learning Objectives
  • Derive the least-squares polynomial fit via the normal equations, and understand why naive monomial fitting becomes numerically ill-conditioned past modest degree.
  • Explain why the Chebyshev basis is the standard remedy, and use Chebyshev nodes / series truncation to build a near-minimax fit without running Remez.
  • Understand what the Remez exchange algorithm optimizes and why true $L^\infty$ (minimax) fits behave better than $L^2$ fits in the tails.
  • Choose composite/piecewise or degree-mixed strategies appropriately for sigmoid, tanh, GELU, and Swish, and compute the resulting multiplicative-depth cost.

Chapter 18 established that a good polynomial replacement for $\phi$ must be fit to a specific, calibrated range $[-R,R]$. This chapter is about the "fit" itself: given $\phi$ and $[-R,R]$, how do you actually construct the polynomial, how do you control its error, and how do you pay for it in multiplicative depth. There is a well-worn, numerically-informed answer, and it long predates FHE — it is classical approximation theory, and CKKS practitioners are simply its newest and most depth-sensitive customers.

19.1 Least-squares in the monomial basis, and why it goes wrong

The most naive approach: sample $\phi$ at points $x_1,\dots,x_m \in [-R,R]$, and solve for coefficients $c_0,\dots,c_d$ minimizing

$$\min_{c \in \mathbb{R}^{d+1}} \sum_{k=1}^m \Big(\phi(x_k) - \sum_{i=0}^d c_i x_k^i\Big)^2$$

In matrix form, with Vandermonde matrix $V \in \mathbb{R}^{m\times(d+1)}$, $V_{ki} = x_k^i$, the minimizer solves the normal equations:

$$V^\top V\, c = V^\top \phi(x)$$
Why this breaks past degree ~10

$V^\top V$ is a generalized Vandermonde-Gram matrix, and its condition number grows exponentially in $d$ — for equally-spaced sample points on $[-1,1]$ it is known to scale roughly as $O((1+\sqrt2)^{2d})$ Likely (the precise constant depends on the sampling; the qualitative exponential blow-up is the textbook Runge-phenomenon-adjacent fact Certain). By degree 12–15 in double precision, solving the normal equations directly returns coefficients dominated by rounding error, not signal — you'll fit a polynomial that satisfies the normal equations numerically while being a poor approximant of $\phi$ or, worse, blowing up between sample points (Runge's phenomenon, exacerbated by monomial ill-conditioning). This is why no serious FHE activation-approximation pipeline fits directly in the monomial basis at anything beyond toy degree.

19.2 The Chebyshev basis fixes the conditioning

The fix is to change basis, not to change the objective. Chebyshev polynomials of the first kind $T_n(x) = \cos(n \arccos x)$ on $[-1,1]$ (rescale $[-R,R] \to [-1,1]$ via $x \mapsto x/R$) form an orthogonal basis with respect to the weight $1/\sqrt{1-x^2}$, and — critically for FHE purposes — they satisfy the simple three-term recurrence

$$T_0(x)=1,\quad T_1(x)=x,\quad T_{n+1}(x) = 2xT_n(x) - T_{n-1}(x)$$

which is exactly as cheap to evaluate homomorphically as the monomial basis (each step: one $\mathsf{EvalMult}$, one scale-by-2, one $\mathsf{EvalSub}$), but with dramatically better numerical behavior when you truncate a series in this basis versus the power-series basis.

Definition 19.1 — Chebyshev series and Chebyshev nodes

Any continuous $\phi$ on $[-1,1]$ has a Chebyshev series $\phi(x) = \sum_{n=0}^\infty a_n T_n(x)$ with $a_n = \frac{2}{\pi}\int_{-1}^1 \frac{\phi(x)T_n(x)}{\sqrt{1-x^2}}dx$ (with $a_0$ halved). Truncating at degree $d$ gives a near-minimax approximation — provably within a small constant factor of the true minimax error (Chebyshev truncation theorem Certain). In practice you never do the integral: you interpolate $\phi$ at the $d{+}1$ Chebyshev nodes $x_k = \cos\!\big(\frac{(2k+1)\pi}{2(d+1)}\big)$, $k=0,\dots,d$, which clusters sample points near $\pm1$ precisely where equally-spaced sampling under-resolves the function and Runge's phenomenon is worst.

OpenFHE exposes this directly for CKKS: `EvalChebyshevFunction` takes a `std::function` (or `EvalChebyshevSeries` for pre-computed coefficients), a ciphertext, a lower/upper bound, and a polynomial degree, and internally performs the node-sampling, coefficient computation, and Paterson–Stockmeyer-optimized homomorphic evaluation. Verify — confirm the exact signature against `src/pke/examples/function-evaluation.cpp` in the `openfhe-development` repository for your installed release before compiling against it; the function-evaluation surface has evolved across releases.

// SKELETON -- verify signatures against openfhe-development's
// src/pke/examples/function-evaluation.cpp for the version you have installed.
#include "openfhe.h"
using namespace lbcrypto;

CCParams parameters;
parameters.SetMultiplicativeDepth(6);     // enough for a degree ~30-60 Chebyshev fit
                                            // via Paterson-Stockmeyer (see 19.5)
parameters.SetScalingModSize(50);
auto cc = GenCryptoContext(parameters);
cc->Enable(PKE); cc->Enable(KEYSWITCH); cc->Enable(LEVELEDSHE);
auto keys = cc->KeyGen();
cc->EvalMultKeyGen(keys.secretKey);

double lowerBound = -8.0, upperBound = 8.0;
uint32_t polyDegree = 27;                  // odd degree is common for odd functions like tanh

auto sigmoid = [](double x) -> double { return 1.0 / (1.0 + std::exp(-x)); };

Plaintext ptxt = cc->MakeCKKSPackedPlaintext(inputVec);
auto ct = cc->Encrypt(keys.publicKey, ptxt);

auto ctSigmoid = cc->EvalChebyshevFunction(sigmoid, ct, lowerBound, upperBound, polyDegree);
// decrypt & decode as usual

19.3 Minimax approximation via Remez — when $L^2$ isn't good enough

Least-squares (whether monomial or Chebyshev-basis) minimizes the $L^2$ error $\int (\phi - p)^2$. That is the right objective if you care about average-case accuracy. But an encrypted network doesn't get to complain "on average my activations were fine" — a handful of badly-approximated inputs in the tails of the distribution can flip a classification. The objective that actually matters for robustness is the $L^\infty$ (minimax) error:

$$\min_{p \,\in\, \mathcal{P}_d} \; \max_{x \in [-R,R]} |\phi(x) - p(x)|$$
Theorem 19.2 — Chebyshev equioscillation (informal)

The degree-$d$ polynomial $p^\star$ minimizing the $L^\infty$ error over $[-R,R]$ is characterized by its error curve $\phi(x)-p^\star(x)$ equioscillating between at least $d+2$ extrema of equal magnitude and alternating sign. This is a classical result (Chebyshev, refined by de la Vallée Poussin) Certain. It is not generally the same polynomial as the least-squares fit, but a truncated Chebyshev series is a very close, cheap-to-compute approximation to it.

The Remez exchange algorithm finds $p^\star$ (or a numerically-converged approximation to it) iteratively: start from a candidate set of reference points, solve a small linear system enforcing equal-and-alternating error at those points, find the new error extrema, swap them in, and repeat until convergence. `scipy.signal.remez` implements the FIR-filter special case; for general function approximation, the `Chebfun`/`chebpy` ecosystem or a hand-rolled Remez loop over `numpy.polynomial.chebyshev` is the practical route. Whether OpenFHE's `EvalChebyshevFunction` internally runs true Remez or the Chebyshev-truncation near-minimax shortcut is an implementation detail worth checking in the source for your release rather than assuming Verify — for most CKKS activation-replacement use cases the truncated-Chebyshev fit is close enough to minimax that the distinction rarely changes downstream accuracy by more than a fraction of a percent, but if you are chasing the last bit of tail robustness, run Remez explicitly in cleartext and hand OpenFHE the resulting coefficients via `EvalChebyshevSeries`.

Practical comparison: sigmoid on $[-8,8]$, degree 15
MethodMax error (in-range)Error near $x=\pm8$
Monomial least-squaresmoderate, basis ill-conditioned above deg ~12can diverge due to conditioning, not just fit quality
Chebyshev truncationsmall, near-minimaxcontrolled, equioscillating
True Remez minimaxsmallest achievable at this degreetightest tail control

The qualitative ordering here is robust across activation functions Likely; exact numbers depend on $R$, $d$, and $\phi$'s smoothness, which is why the artifact below has you measure it rather than take these on faith.

19.4 Composite and piecewise strategies

A single global polynomial is not the only option, and for functions with sharp local structure it is often not the best one:

  • Different-degree-per-range composition: for $\mathrm{ReLU}$, whose kink sits at the origin, several published constructions use a low-degree polynomial to approximate the sign function near zero (where precision matters most) composed with a scaling trick, rather than fitting the whole $[-R,R]$ range with one high-degree polynomial. This can reduce the required degree substantially versus a single naive global fit Likely — magnitude of the saving depends on $R$ and target accuracy.
  • Composition of low-degree polynomials ($p = p_k \circ \cdots \circ p_1$): composing $k$ polynomials of degree $g$ yields an effective degree $g^k$ at multiplicative depth roughly $k\lceil \log_2 g\rceil$. Note this is not a depth saving over evaluating a single degree-$g^k$ polynomial: $k\log_2 g = \log_2(g^k)$, so the two are the same to within a ceiling. The real advantages are numerical and practical — you never have to construct or store the coefficients of a degree-$g^k$ polynomial (which are numerically catastrophic at large $k$, with huge alternating values), each step is a small well-conditioned evaluation, and for step-like targets the iteration converges to the shape you want far more gracefully than a single monolithic fit. Composition and is the mechanism behind several sign/comparison approximation papers that iterate a simple polynomial many times to sharpen an approximate step function.
  • Function-specific closed forms: $\mathrm{GELU}(x) = x\cdot\Phi(x) \approx x \cdot \sigma(1.702x)$ (the standard Swish-based GELU approximation used even in plaintext deep learning) reduces the transformer activation to one $\sigma$-approximation plus one multiply — reuse your sigmoid polynomial rather than fitting GELU's Gaussian-CDF shape from scratch. $\mathrm{Swish}(x) = x\cdot\sigma(\beta x)$ is structurally identical.
  • Square activation as the zero-cost option: when architecture search is available (i.e., you are training the network with FHE deployment in mind, not retrofitting a pretrained one), replacing $\mathrm{ReLU}$ with $x^2$ or a scaled/shifted variant sidesteps approximation entirely — it costs exactly one $\mathsf{EvalMult}$, exact up to CKKS numerical precision. CryptoNets (Chapter 21) made this choice for precisely this reason.

19.5 Paying for it: Paterson–Stockmeyer depth

Fact 19.3 — Depth of homomorphic polynomial evaluation

Naive (Horner's method) evaluation of a degree-$d$ polynomial on a ciphertext costs $d$ sequential multiplicative levels. The Paterson–Stockmeyer algorithm restructures the evaluation as a two-level scheme — build a "baby-step" set of powers $\{x, x^2, \dots, x^{\lceil\sqrt d\rceil}\}$, then combine them in "giant steps" — reducing the depth to $O(\log d)$, close to $\lceil \log_2(d+1) \rceil$ for well-chosen baby/giant-step splits Certain. It reduces the total number of non-scalar multiplications as well, from $d-1$ under Horner to roughly $\sqrt{2d} + \log_2 d$ — that reduction is the whole point of the 1973 algorithm, and it is a strict improvement on both axes rather than a depth-for-work trade. What it does cost is memory: the baby-step powers must all be kept live. This is what OpenFHE's EvalChebyshevFunction/EvalPolynomial family does internally.

Budget from the library's table, not from the formula. OpenFHE's implementation spends a couple of levels beyond the theoretical $\lceil\log_2(d{+}1)\rceil$ on internal scaling, so sizing SetMultiplicativeDepth from the formula will under-provision and fail at runtime. The authoritative mapping ships with the library in src/pke/examples/FUNCTION_EVALUATION.md:

Chebyshev degreeMultiplicative depth consumed
3–54
6–135
14–276
28–597
60–1198
120–2479
248–49510

So a degree-15 sigmoid — the common default — costs 6 levels, not the 4 that $\lceil\log_2 16\rceil$ predicts. (On the domain $(-1,1)$ specifically, subtract one from each entry.) Add the surrounding layers' levels on top of this when choosing parameters. Certain, verified against the current OpenFHE repository

19.6 Artifact — Chebyshev approximation of $\mathrm{ReLU}$, sigmoid, GELU: error vs. depth

Artifact 19.A — measuring the accuracy/depth frontier
# Cleartext exploration first -- establish which degree you need before
# spending OpenFHE compile/runtime cycles. This mirrors the actual FHE
# ML workflow: calibrate and select degree in numpy, THEN port to CKKS.
import numpy as np
from numpy.polynomial import chebyshev as C

def relu(x):    return np.maximum(x, 0.0)
def sigmoid(x): return 1.0 / (1.0 + np.exp(-x))
def gelu(x):    return x * sigmoid(1.702 * x)     # Swish-based GELU approx (19.4)

def chebyshev_fit(f, R, degree, n_nodes=2000):
    """Fit f on [-R, R] via Chebyshev series truncated at `degree`."""
    x = np.linspace(-R, R, n_nodes)
    xs = x / R                                     # rescale to [-1, 1]
    # numpy's chebfit already uses well-conditioned Chebyshev-domain
    # least squares -- not raw monomial normal equations (19.1's failure mode).
    coeffs = C.chebfit(xs, f(x), degree)
    return coeffs, R

def eval_chebyshev(coeffs, R, x):
    return C.chebval(x / R, coeffs)

def paterson_stockmeyer_depth(degree):
    return int(np.ceil(np.log2(degree + 1)))

R = 8.0
xs_test = np.linspace(-R, R, 500)
xs_tail = np.array([-R - 1, -R - 0.5, R + 0.5, R + 1])   # just outside fit range

for name, f in [("relu", relu), ("sigmoid", sigmoid), ("gelu", gelu)]:
    print(f"\n--- {name} on [-{R},{R}] ---")
    for degree in (4, 8, 15, 27):
        coeffs, _ = chebyshev_fit(f, R, degree)
        approx = eval_chebyshev(coeffs, R, xs_test)
        err_in  = np.max(np.abs(approx - f(xs_test)))
        approx_tail = eval_chebyshev(coeffs, R, xs_tail)
        err_out = np.max(np.abs(approx_tail - f(xs_tail)))
        depth = paterson_stockmeyer_depth(degree)
        print(f"  degree={degree:3d}  P-S depth={depth}  "
              f"max_err_in_range={err_in:.2e}  max_err_just_outside={err_out:.2e}")

Expect the qualitative pattern: for $\mathrm{ReLU}$'s sharp kink, error stays stubbornly high (Gibbs-phenomenon-adjacent oscillation near $x=0$) even at moderate degree, and only tightens meaningfully once degree climbs into the teens-to-twenties; for the smooth $\sigma$ and $\mathrm{GELU}$, error drops fast and near-monotonically with degree, since Chebyshev truncation error is governed by the smoothness of $\phi$ (analytic functions converge geometrically; functions with kinks converge only polynomially). The out-of-range column should visibly worsen relative to in-range error as degree increases — the same divergence from Chapter 18's Artifact 18.A, now quantified per-function. Once you've picked a degree/range pair that clears your accuracy bar, port the chosen $(R, d)$ into `EvalChebyshevFunction(f, ct, -R, R, d)` in OpenFHE (19.2) and set `SetMultiplicativeDepth` to the Paterson–Stockmeyer depth this loop reports, plus whatever depth the surrounding linear layers consume.

Key Takeaways
  • Fit polynomials in the Chebyshev basis, not the monomial basis — monomial normal equations become numerically unusable well before the degrees encrypted ML routinely needs.
  • Chebyshev series truncation gives a near-minimax fit almost for free; true minimax (Remez) tightens the tails further at the cost of an offline optimization step.
  • Homomorphic polynomial evaluation costs $O(\log d)$ multiplicative depth via Paterson–Stockmeyer, not $O(d)$ — this is what makes degree-20-plus activations tractable in a leveled scheme.
  • Match the strategy to the function: exploit closed-form reductions (GELU→sigmoid), use square activation when architecture is co-designed, and always calibrate the fitting range to your model's actual empirical pre-activation distribution.
Chapter 20

Encrypted Inference: Linear Models and Decision Trees

Learning Objectives
  • Implement encrypted logistic regression end-to-end in TenSEAL as the canonical "hello world" of encrypted ML, and account for every multiplicative level it consumes.
  • Explain why decision trees and gradient-boosted trees are the natural fit for TFHE/Concrete-ML rather than CKKS.
  • Reason quantitatively about the accuracy–privacy–performance triangle rather than treating any one axis as free.

With Chapters 18–19 giving us a working answer to "what do we do about the activation," we can finally build something end-to-end. This chapter deliberately starts with the two model families that need the least of that machinery: linear/logistic models (one activation, applied once, at the very end) and decision trees (no activation function at all in the CKKS sense — trees are pure comparison, which routes them to TFHE instead). Between them they cover a surprising fraction of real deployed tabular ML, and they are the right place to build calibrated intuition for cost before Chapter 21 takes on full neural networks.

20.1 Encrypted linear and logistic regression: the "hello world"

A linear model's forward pass is $\hat y = w^\top x + b$ — a dot product, depth 1, no activation, entirely inside CKKS's comfort zone. Logistic regression adds exactly one non-polynomial step: $\hat p = \sigma(w^\top x + b)$. That single $\sigma$ is the entire non-linear budget of the whole model, which is precisely why it is the standard first example in every encrypted-ML tutorial: it isolates Chapter 19's machinery from everything else that can go wrong.

Depth accounting for encrypted logistic regression

$w^\top x$: one $\mathsf{EvalMultPlain}$ (weights are known plaintext, not encrypted) followed by a rotate-and-sum reduction (Chapter 21 covers the packing trick in detail) — this consumes 1 level. It is a common and costly error to budget 0 here on the grounds that a plaintext multiply needs no relinearization. That is true but irrelevant to depth: a plaintext multiply skips relinearization (so it is cheaper in time), yet the product's scale is still the product of the two scales, so CKKS must rescale, and the rescale is what spends the level. Only multiplication by an exact integer or a scale-1 encoding avoids that.
