The joint public key $pk$ is a deterministic combination of all $N$ contributions (in the RLWE-based constructions used by CKKS/BFV, this is typically $pk = (b, a)$ with $b = -a\cdot s + e$ where $s = \sum_i s_i$ is never materialized anywhere). No party, and no coalition smaller than the decryption threshold, ever sees the full secret key $s$ — it exists only implicitly as a sum of shares that no single message ever reconstructs. Certain
  • Homomorphic evaluation is untouched. Anyone (a cloud server, one of the $N$ parties, or an outside client) encrypts under the joint $pk$ and runs arbitrary circuits exactly as in single-key CKKS or BFV — additions, multiplications, rotations, bootstrapping all behave identically, because the ciphertext format under a threshold key is the same as under an ordinary key.
  • Threshold (partial) decryption. To decrypt a result ciphertext $ct$, each participating party $i$ computes a partial decryption $ct \to d_i$ using only its own share $s_i$, and broadcasts $d_i$. A combiner (any party, or a public bulletin board) sums the $d_i$ from at least $t$ parties and adds noise-flooding/smudging terms to erase any information the individual $d_i$ values might otherwise have leaked, to recover the plaintext. If fewer than $t$ parties cooperate, no information about the plaintext is revealed — the scheme degrades gracefully into "no one can decrypt," which is precisely the desired failure mode for a system meant to resist collusion by any minority. Certain
Definition 25.1 — Threshold FHE Correctness and Security

A $(t,N)$-threshold FHE scheme is correct if any $t$ of the $N$ partial decryptions combine to recover the correct plaintext (up to the usual FHE noise), and is secure if any coalition of fewer than $t$ parties, given their own secret shares and arbitrary ciphertexts, learns nothing about the plaintext beyond what is implied by the joint public key. The standard construction achieves $t = N$ ("full threshold") most simply; general $t$-of-$N$ requires either an additional secret-sharing layer (e.g., Shamir shares of $s$ combined with interpolation in the exponent/noise domain) or an interactive combining protocol.

Threshold CKKS and threshold BFV are the two variants that matter in practice, and both are supported in OpenFHE through the Multiparty* family of APIs — MultipartyKeyGen, MultipartyDecryptLead, MultipartyDecryptMain and MultipartyDecryptFusion, gated by Enable(MULTIPARTY) — layered on top of the ordinary CKKS and BFV/BGV crypto contexts. (There is no Threshold* API despite the feature's name; only the example filenames use "threshold", which is a reliable way to waste an afternoon searching for symbols that do not exist.) Certain The key insight that makes this practical rather than a research toy is that the homomorphic evaluation layer needs zero modification — the threshold machinery lives entirely in key generation and decryption, so all the optimizations, bootstrapping procedures, and packing tricks from Parts II–IV of this book carry over unchanged to the multiparty setting.

25.1.1 Noise Flooding in Partial Decryption

A subtlety that trips up first implementations: naively summing partial decryptions $d_i = \langle ct, s_i \rangle$ leaks more than the final plaintext — it can leak information about individual noise terms unless each party adds a large random "smudging" term $e_i^{\text{smudge}}$ to its own partial decryption before broadcasting. The smudging noise must be large enough (typically $2^{40}$–$2^{80}$ times the ciphertext noise) that the combined smudge statistically drowns any residual signal from the individual $s_i$, yet small enough not to corrupt the final rounded plaintext. Sizing this term correctly is a standard but easy-to-miss step; the OpenFHE threshold implementation handles it internally, but if you ever hand-roll a threshold decryption protocol, treat smudging-noise sizing as a security-critical parameter with the same seriousness you give the RLWE noise itself.

25.2 Multi-Key HE: Independent Keys, Joint Evaluation

Threshold FHE assumes the parties agree in advance to share one key. Multi-key HE (MKHE) removes even that assumption: each party $i$ generates its own completely independent $(pk_i, sk_i)$ under a common set of public parameters, and encrypts its own data under its own key with ordinary single-key encryption. The interesting machinery is in the evaluator, who receives ciphertexts $ct_1$ (under $pk_1$), $ct_2$ (under $pk_2$), ..., and must compute a function of the combined plaintexts without ever holding any secret key.

The dominant approach, following López-Alt, Tromer, and Vaikuntanathan's original 2012 construction and its later packed CKKS/BFV-compatible descendants (Chen, Dai, Kim and Song, "Efficient Multi-Key Homomorphic Encryption with Packed Ciphertexts," CCS 2019, eprint 2019/524; and Chen, Chillotti and Song for the TFHE analogue), extends every ciphertext that touches multiple keys into a structure whose size grows linearly with the number of distinct keys involved in its ancestry. Concretely, a "fresh" ciphertext under a single key looks like an ordinary RLWE pair, but the moment you homomorphically combine ciphertexts encrypted under $k$ different keys, the result is represented relative to the concatenated key vector $(s_1, \dots, s_k)$, and its size (and the cost of every subsequent operation on it) grows roughly linearly, sometimes quadratically for multiplication, in $k$. Likely

Property 25.1 — MKHE Cost Scaling

For an MKHE ciphertext whose plaintext is a function of data encrypted under $k$ distinct public keys, ciphertext size scales as $O(k)$ ring elements, homomorphic addition remains $O(k)$, and homomorphic multiplication costs $O(k^2)$ due to the pairwise cross-terms between the $k$ secret-key components introduced by relinearization across keys. This is the central practical constraint on MKHE: it scales gracefully to a handful of parties (2–10) but becomes prohibitive well before $N$ reaches the hundreds, in contrast to threshold FHE, where evaluation cost is entirely independent of $N$.

Decryption in MKHE mirrors threshold decryption structurally — each of the $k$ contributing key holders computes a partial decryption share using its own $sk_i$, and all $k$ shares must be combined to recover the plaintext — but the trust assumption is different in an important way: in MKHE no party had to agree in advance to a joint key, so a party can join a computation ad hoc simply by encrypting under a key it generated unilaterally, and the "committee" that must cooperate to decrypt is exactly the set of parties whose data flowed into the result, not a pre-fixed threshold committee. This makes MKHE the natural fit for ad hoc, session-based collaborations (e.g., an auction where bidders are not known in advance), while threshold FHE fits a standing federation with a known, stable membership (e.g., a fixed consortium of hospitals).

25.3 Proxy Re-Encryption

Proxy re-encryption (PRE) solves a narrower problem than either of the above: Alice has $ct_A = \text{Enc}_{pk_A}(m)$ and wants Bob to be able to decrypt the same plaintext under his own key $sk_B$, without Alice ever learning $sk_B$, without the proxy performing the re-encryption ever learning $m$, and — ideally — without decrypting and re-encrypting in the clear. A PRE scheme lets Alice (or a trusted setup) generate a re-encryption key $rk_{A\to B}$ that a semi-trusted proxy applies directly to $ct_A$ to produce $ct_B = \text{Enc}_{pk_B}(m)$, with the proxy learning nothing about $m$ in the process. Certain

PRE long predates the FHE renaissance — the original constructions (Blaze–Bleumer–Strauss 1998, Ateniese et al. 2006) were built over ElGamal-style groups and support only limited (often just linear, or single-hop) operations. Lattice-based PRE compatible with BFV/BGV/CKKS ciphertexts exists and is useful specifically for the "re-key a ciphertext at rest" operation — for example, rotating which employee's key a database of encrypted records is readable under, without touching the plaintext — but it is not a substitute for threshold or multi-key HE when the actual goal is joint computation across parties' data, since PRE does not by itself let two parties' ciphertexts be combined homomorphically; it only moves a ciphertext from one single-key regime to another. In production FHE toolchains, PRE support is comparatively immature relative to threshold and multi-key APIs, and most practitioners encountering a "convert this ciphertext to a different key" requirement today reach for threshold decrypt-then-re-encrypt as a simpler (if less elegant) substitute rather than a dedicated PRE library. Verify

25.4 Production Implementations

25.4.1 OpenFHE

OpenFHE has shipped threshold FHE support for BGV, BFV, and CKKS since its early releases, and as of v1.5.1 the threshold API is one of the library's most mature multiparty offerings, covering distributed key generation, partial (threshold) decryption with configurable smudging, and both "interactive" (decrypt-then-combine) and "non-interactive" (single round of broadcast) variants for different network topologies. Certain OpenFHE's multiparty layer is built as an extension of the same CryptoContext abstraction used throughout the library, so switching a single-party CKKS pipeline to a threshold one is largely a matter of calling the multiparty key generation functions instead of the ordinary ones and replacing the final Decrypt call with a two-step partial-decrypt-then-fuse sequence — the artifact in §25.5 walks through exactly this.

25.4.2 Lattigo

Lattigo (developed originally at EPFL's LDS lab and now maintained by Tune Insight SA) is a Go-native lattice cryptography library whose defining strength, relative to the C++-first OpenFHE and SEAL, is first-class support for multiparty protocols across BGV, BFV, and CKKS variants — its multiparty subpackages implement distributed key generation, collective key switching, collective bootstrapping, and threshold decryption as core, actively maintained features rather than bolt-ons. Certain Because Go compiles cleanly to WebAssembly, Lattigo-based multiparty protocols can run inside a browser tab, which has made it the library of choice for several federated-analytics and privacy-preserving-statistics products where each "party" is a browser or lightweight edge client rather than a well-provisioned server — a deployment shape OpenFHE's threshold API, being C++-first, supports less directly. Lattigo v6 continues this focus, with cleaner multiparty APIs and support for collective (multiparty) bootstrapping, which threshold-decrypts a ciphertext to a fresh, high-level one across the committee, sidestepping the need for any single party to run single-key bootstrapping. Likely

25.5 Applications

  • Multi-hospital analytics. A consortium of $N$ hospitals each holds patient data that cannot leave institutional boundaries under HIPAA/GDPR. A $(t,N)$ or $(N,N)$ threshold CKKS key lets a coordinating node aggregate encrypted statistics (mean incidence rates, encrypted logistic regression gradients) across all sites; decryption of any result requires cooperation of the hospitals, so no single breached node — including the coordinator — can unilaterally recover any patient's data.
  • Privacy-preserving auctions. Bidders encrypt sealed bids under a joint threshold key (or under their own keys, in an MKHE construction) so that the auctioneer can homomorphically compute the winner and clearing price without ever seeing individual bids, and the result is only released once a quorum of auction participants or overseers cooperates in decryption — preventing both the auctioneer and any single bidder from learning others' bids.
  • Joint ML across siloed data. Threshold CKKS is the natural substrate for cross-silo federated learning variants that want cryptographic (not just statistical) guarantees on gradient aggregation: each participant encrypts local gradients under the shared key, a server sums ciphertexts (homomorphic addition — cheap, no multiplicative depth consumed), and threshold-decrypts only the aggregate, never any individual party's gradient.
Artifact 25.A — Threshold CKKS Decryption in OpenFHE with 3 Parties (SKELETON)
// SKELETON — illustrates OpenFHE's multiparty CKKS API shape.
// Consult the current OpenFHE multiparty examples before running;
// exact function names have shifted across releases.
#include "openfhe.h"
using namespace lbcrypto;

int main() {
    // 1. Parameters — identical to single-party CKKS setup.
    CCParams parameters;
    parameters.SetMultiplicativeDepth(3);
    parameters.SetScalingModSize(50);
    parameters.SetBatchSize(8);

    CryptoContext cc = GenCryptoContext(parameters);
    cc->Enable(PKE);
    cc->Enable(KEYSWITCH);
    cc->Enable(LEVELEDSHE);
    cc->Enable(MULTIPARTY);          // multiparty feature flag

    // 2. Distributed key generation across 3 parties.
    //    Party 1 generates the "lead" keypair; parties 2..N contribute shares.
    KeyPair kp1 = cc->KeyGen();
    KeyPair kp2 = cc->MultipartyKeyGen(kp1.publicKey);
    KeyPair kp3 = cc->MultipartyKeyGen(kp2.publicKey);

    // Joint public key: the final party's public key IS the joint key
    // after each stage folds in the prior parties' contributions.
    PublicKey jointPublicKey = kp3.publicKey;

    // 3. Evaluation keys (relinearization, rotation) are generated
    //    collectively via analogous MultipartyEvalMultKeyGen /
    //    MultipartyEvalAtIndexKeyGen calls, then combined.
    // ... (omitted: standard pattern, see OpenFHE multiparty examples)

    // 4. Any client encrypts under the joint public key exactly as usual.
    std::vector x = {1.5, 2.5, 3.5, 4.5};
    Plaintext pt = cc->MakeCKKSPackedPlaintext(x);
    auto ct = cc->Encrypt(jointPublicKey, pt);

    // 5. Homomorphic evaluation — unmodified single-key CKKS code path.
    auto ctResult = cc->EvalMult(ct, ct);   // e.g. compute x^2

    // 6. Threshold decryption: each party computes a partial decryption
    //    using ONLY its own secret-key share.
    auto partial1 = cc->MultipartyDecryptLead({ctResult}, kp1.secretKey);
    auto partial2 = cc->MultipartyDecryptMain({ctResult}, kp2.secretKey);
    auto partial3 = cc->MultipartyDecryptMain({ctResult}, kp3.secretKey);

    // 7. Fuse partial decryptions — requires ALL (or threshold) shares.
    std::vector> partials = {partial1[0], partial2[0], partial3[0]};
    Plaintext result;
    cc->MultipartyDecryptFusion(partials, &result);

    result->SetLength(x.size());
    // result now holds the decrypted x^2, recoverable only because
    // all 3 parties cooperated in step 6.
    return 0;
}

SKELETON. Illustrates the shape of the protocol — lead/main key generation asymmetry, collective evaluation-key generation, and lead/main partial-decryption asymmetry — not a drop-in program. Consult the current OpenFHE multiparty example directory for exact signatures, which have evolved across major versions.

Key Takeaways
  • Threshold FHE shares one key across $N$ parties and requires $t$ of them to cooperate at decryption time; homomorphic evaluation is byte-for-byte identical to single-key FHE.
  • Multi-key FHE lets each party keep an independent key and combines ciphertexts across keys during evaluation, at a cost that grows with the number of distinct keys touched — $O(k)$ for addition, $O(k^2)$ for multiplication.
  • Proxy re-encryption moves a ciphertext between single-owner keys without exposing the plaintext, but does not itself enable joint computation across parties.
  • OpenFHE (v1.5.1+) is the reference C++ implementation for threshold BGV/BFV/CKKS; Lattigo (Tune Insight SA) is the Go-native, WASM-friendly implementation with the deepest multiparty feature set, including collective bootstrapping.
  • Choose threshold FHE for standing federations with known membership; choose MKHE for small, ad hoc collaborations where parties are not fixed in advance.
Chapter 26

Scheme Switching and Hybrid Computation

Learning Objectives
  • Articulate why no single FHE scheme is best at everything, and how CKKS and TFHE occupy complementary niches.
  • Trace the OpenFHE scheme-switching pipeline end to end: CKKS ciphertext → per-slot extraction → CKKS-to-FHEW conversion → FHEW bootstrap with an arbitrary LUT → FHEW-to-CKKS conversion → repacking.
  • Reason quantitatively about when scheme switching is worth its overhead compared with a polynomial approximation evaluated entirely in CKKS.
  • Identify the sequential slot-extraction bottleneck as the current practical ceiling on scheme switching throughput, and describe the direction of ongoing research to remove it.

Parts II and III of this book presented BFV, BGV, CKKS, and TFHE as separate schemes, each best on its own turf: CKKS for approximate real-valued SIMD arithmetic (dot products, convolutions, matrix multiplies) at a throughput no other scheme matches; TFHE for evaluating arbitrary, even non-polynomial, single-input functions via lookup-table programmable bootstrapping, at a per-bit latency no batched scheme can approach for genuinely nonlinear operations. A real workload — a neural network with linear layers and a ReLU, a decision tree with comparisons, an aggregation pipeline with a division — routinely needs both kinds of operation in the same circuit. Scheme switching is the machinery that lets a single computation move a value from a CKKS ciphertext into a TFHE/FHEW ciphertext, evaluate an arbitrary function there via programmable bootstrapping, and move the result back into CKKS to resume vectorized linear algebra — without ever decrypting. Certain

26.1 Why Two Schemes Instead of One

Recall the two evaluation strategies available for a nonlinear function $f$ under CKKS alone (Chapter 10, Chapter 16): either approximate $f$ with a low-degree polynomial (cheap per evaluation, but the approximation error is uncontrolled outside the fitted domain, and accuracy near a discontinuity like ReLU's kink at zero is fundamentally limited by polynomial degree) or use CKKS's own bootstrapping to refresh levels and iterate a higher-degree approximation, paying bootstrapping's substantial latency. TFHE's programmable bootstrapping (Chapter 14) evaluates any function representable as a lookup table exactly, with error bounded purely by the LWE/RLWE noise budget and completely independent of how "sharp" or discontinuous $f$ is — a step function, ReLU, sign, or an arbitrary S-box costs the same PBS as a smooth polynomial of the same output precision. The catch is that TFHE operates on scalars (or small vectors via the LWE-to-RLWE conversion tricks in Chapter 14), not on SIMD-packed slot vectors, so a naive TFHE-only pipeline pays a per-element bootstrap for every entry of what CKKS would process as a single ciphertext.

Scheme switching is the recognition that these two cost profiles are complementary rather than competing: keep the linear algebra (matrix multiplies, convolutions, dot products, additions) in CKKS where SIMD packing amortizes cost across thousands of slots, and drop out to FHEW/TFHE only for the handful of genuinely nonlinear pointwise operations a circuit needs, paying the PBS cost per element only where it is unavoidable.

26.2 The OpenFHE Scheme-Switching Pipeline

OpenFHE exposes CKKS↔FHEW scheme switching as a first-class capability, following the CKKS-bootstrapping-via-FHEW-PBS line of research (building on PEGASUS — Lu, Huang, Hong, Ma and Qu, IEEE S&P 2021, eprint 2020/1606 — alongside CHIMERA and OpenFHE's own scheme-switching and functional-bootstrapping papers). The pipeline, expressed at the level of the operations you actually call, is:

  1. Slot extraction. A CKKS ciphertext packs up to $N/2$ complex (or real) values into its slots via the canonical embedding. To apply a scalar function to one slot's value using FHEW, that value must first be isolated as its own LWE-style sample. OpenFHE does this with a homomorphic linear transform (effectively a rotate-and-mask sequence, the same machinery used for CKKS bootstrapping's linear layer in Chapter 17) that extracts one coefficient of the underlying RLWE ciphertext into a standalone LWE ciphertext encrypting that single slot's (scaled, rounded) value.
  2. EvalCKKStoFHEW. The extracted LWE sample, still under the CKKS ring's parameters and scale, is converted into a proper FHEW/TFHE-format LWE ciphertext — matching modulus, dimension, and noise conventions expected by FHEW's bootstrapping key. This conversion itself typically consumes one key-switching operation, using a dedicated switching key generated once during setup. Likely
  3. FHEW programmable bootstrapping with an arbitrary LUT (EvalFunc). With the value now a standard FHEW ciphertext, OpenFHE runs ordinary FHEW/TFHE programmable bootstrapping against a lookup table encoding whatever function is needed — ReLU, sign, sigmoid, a comparison, an arbitrary S-box. This is exactly the machinery from Chapter 14, applied per element.
  4. EvalFHEWtoCKKS. The FHEW result is converted back into CKKS format — another key-switch, reversing step 2's conversion, landing the value back as a fresh, full-level CKKS-compatible LWE/RLWE sample.
  5. Repacking. Individually converted slot values are recombined (via the inverse of step 1's linear transform — rotate-and-add) into a single packed CKKS ciphertext, ready to resume ordinary SIMD CKKS evaluation (further matrix multiplies, additions, and so on).

OpenFHE v1.4 and later extended this machinery with dedicated ConvertRLWEToCKKS and ConvertCKKSToRLWE primitives that generalize steps 2–4 into reusable building blocks for functional CKKS bootstrapping — using an FHEW/TFHE bootstrap not merely to refresh CKKS's level budget (as ordinary CKKS bootstrapping does) but to simultaneously apply an arbitrary function as part of the refresh, folding "bootstrap" and "apply nonlinearity" into a single pass. Likely The same release line introduced a faster EvalFastRotation API that OpenFHE's internal slot-extraction/repacking linear transforms lean on, reducing the rotation overhead that dominates steps 1 and 5. Likely

Definition 26.1 — Scheme Switching

Given a ciphertext $ct_A$ under scheme $A$ encrypting message $m$, scheme switching produces $ct_B$ under scheme $B$ encrypting the same $m$ (up to scheme-appropriate precision), without decryption, using a switching key generated once by the key owner during setup. CKKS↔FHEW switching is symmetric: both directions exist and are typically paired within a single circuit that alternates between the two representations around each nonlinear operation.

26.3 Cost Model: When Switching Pays For Itself

Scheme switching is not free, and treating it as a universal replacement for polynomial approximation is a common early mistake. Each round trip (CKKS→FHEW→CKKS) for a single packed ciphertext of $n$ active slots costs, roughly:

  • $O(n)$ rotations for slot extraction and $O(n)$ more for repacking — rotations are themselves not free (each is a key-switch under the hood), and OpenFHE's EvalFastRotation reduces but does not eliminate this cost;
  • $n$ independent FHEW programmable bootstraps, each with latency on the order of the single-bit/single-value PBS cost from Chapter 14 (milliseconds, not the sub-millisecond regime CKKS's packed multiplication enjoys per-slot);
  • two format-conversion key-switches per slot (steps 2 and 4).

Compare this against a pure-CKKS polynomial approximation of the same function: a degree-$d$ polynomial costs $O(\log d)$ multiplicative depth and a handful of packed multiplications for all $n$ slots simultaneously — the entire packed ciphertext is evaluated in one pass, not $n$ separate PBS invocations. For a smooth, well-behaved function over a bounded domain (sigmoid, softmax's exponential component, a polynomial activation), a degree-8 to degree-16 Chebyshev or minimax approximation typically outperforms scheme switching by a wide margin in both latency and implementation simplicity, and is the default choice throughout Part IV of this book.

Scheme switching earns its overhead precisely where polynomial approximation degrades: functions with a genuine discontinuity or a sharp kink that a bounded-degree polynomial cannot track without either large approximation error near the kink or an impractically high degree. ReLU's behavior near zero is the canonical example — a low-degree polynomial approximation of ReLU either rounds off the corner (misclassifying small-magnitude activations) or requires degree high enough to blow the multiplicative depth budget; TFHE's PBS evaluates the exact step function at the cost of one bootstrap per element, independent of how sharp the corner is. Sign, argmax-style comparisons, exact division, and modular reduction (all needed in some decision-tree and pooling operations) are similarly poor polynomial-approximation targets and similarly good scheme-switching targets.

Rule of Thumb 26.1

Use scheme switching when (a) the function is discontinuous, has a sharp kink, or its needed accuracy near a boundary cannot be met by a polynomial of degree the multiplicative-depth budget can afford, and (b) the number of elements requiring the nonlinearity per inference is small relative to the total ciphertext slot count (so the $O(n)$ per-slot PBS cost does not dominate the pipeline). Use polynomial approximation in CKKS otherwise — which, in most CKKS-based ML pipelines built with sigmoid/tanh/softmax-style smooth activations, is the majority of cases.

26.4 The Sequential Bottleneck

The largest practical limitation of scheme switching today is that slot extraction, as implemented, processes slots essentially one at a time (or in small batches via the rotate-and-mask linear transform), so its cost scales linearly with the number of slots that need a nonlinearity applied — there is no packed, SIMD-parallel way to hand $n$ values to FHEW's programmable bootstrap simultaneously the way CKKS multiplication handles $n$ values in one ciphertext operation. For a layer with a few dozen activations this is a manageable, if noticeable, latency addition; for a layer with thousands of activations — the realistic scale of even a small convolutional or transformer layer — the linear-in-$n$ scheme-switching cost can dominate total inference latency and erase the throughput advantage CKKS's packing was supposed to provide in the first place. Likely

This is an active area of research rather than a solved problem. Proposals to address it broadly fall into two camps: (1) batched scheme switching, which tries to amortize the FHEW bootstrapping key and the LWE-to-RLWE conversion machinery across multiple slots per bootstrap call by exploiting structure in how FHEW's blind rotation can, in principle, process short vectors rather than single scalars; and (2) selective switching, which minimizes the number of values sent through the FHEW path by restructuring the network or circuit (e.g., approximating most activations polynomially and reserving exact PBS only for the outputs most sensitive to approximation error, or for a coarsened, lower-resolution version of the activation map). Neither direction has yet closed the gap to CKKS-native per-slot cost, and the honest state of the art as of this writing is that scheme switching remains a targeted tool for a handful of stubborn nonlinearities per circuit, not a general substitute for polynomial approximation at scale. Verify

26.5 Comparing the Two Strategies End to End

DimensionPure polynomial (CKKS-only)Scheme switching (CKKS↔FHEW)
Accuracy near discontinuitiesBounded by polynomial degree; degrades near kinksExact, up to FHEW noise floor, regardless of function shape
Multiplicative depth consumed$O(\log d)$ for degree-$d$ approximation; consumes CKKS level budgetNone consumed in CKKS; FHEW PBS resets its own noise internally
Per-element costAmortized across all $n$ packed slots in one operation$O(n)$ — one PBS per element, no packing benefit
Implementation complexityLow — fit coefficients, call EvalPoly/Chebyshev evaluatorHigh — requires dual crypto-context setup, switching keys, careful scale/modulus bookkeeping
Best suited forSmooth activations, softmax's exponential, large batched layersReLU-like kinks, comparisons, sign, small activation counts
Artifact 26.A — Two-Layer NN: CKKS Linear + FHEW ReLU vs. Pure-CKKS Polynomial ReLU (SKELETON)
// SKELETON — two implementations of the same 2-layer network's
// forward pass, contrasted for latency/accuracy discussion.
// Exact OpenFHE scheme-switching setup calls must be verified
// against current release documentation before use.
#include "openfhe.h"
#include "binfhecontext.h"
using namespace lbcrypto;

// ---- Variant A: pure-CKKS, polynomial ReLU approximation ----
Ciphertext forwardPolyReLU(CryptoContext cc,
                                      Ciphertext x,
                                      Ciphertext W1, Ciphertext b1) {
    auto z1 = cc->EvalAdd(cc->EvalMult(x, W1), b1);   // linear layer 1
    // Minimax/Chebyshev polynomial approximating ReLU on [-R, R],
    // e.g. degree 15, fit offline and loaded as plaintext coefficients.
    std::vector reluCoeffs = /* precomputed */ {};
    auto a1 = cc->EvalPoly(z1, reluCoeffs);            // depth ~ log2(15)
    return a1;   // feed into layer 2 identically
}

// ---- Variant B: CKKS linear layers, FHEW exact ReLU via switching ----
Ciphertext forwardSchemeSwitchReLU(
        CryptoContext ccCKKS,
        std::shared_ptr ccFHEW,
        Ciphertext x, Ciphertext W1, Ciphertext b1) {
    auto z1 = ccCKKS->EvalAdd(ccCKKS->EvalMult(x, W1), b1);

    // 1-2. Extract slots and convert CKKS -> FHEW.
    auto lweCiphertexts = ccCKKS->EvalCKKStoFHEW(z1, /*numSlots=*/8);

    // 3. Exact ReLU via FHEW programmable bootstrap per slot.
    std::vector reluOut;
    for (auto &lwe : lweCiphertexts) {
        auto lut = /* build ReLU LUT for the configured plaintext modulus */;
        reluOut.push_back(ccFHEW->EvalFunc(lwe, lut));
    }

    // 4-5. Convert FHEW -> CKKS and repack into one ciphertext.
    auto a1 = ccCKKS->EvalFHEWtoCKKS(reluOut, /*numSlots=*/8);
    return a1;
}

// Comparison harness (conceptual):
//  - Variant A: single EvalPoly call, ~O(log d) depth, packed across all slots.
//  - Variant B: 8 FHEW bootstraps (one per slot), exact ReLU, zero CKKS depth
//    consumed, but paying extraction + 8x PBS + repacking latency.
//  - For small slot counts (<= tens) and accuracy-critical layers, B often
//    wins on accuracy; for large batched layers, A usually wins on latency.
Key Takeaways
  • Scheme switching (CKKS↔FHEW) lets a single computation combine CKKS's packed linear algebra with TFHE's exact, arbitrary-function programmable bootstrapping.
  • The OpenFHE pipeline is: slot extraction → EvalCKKStoFHEW → FHEW EvalFunc (PBS with an LUT) → EvalFHEWtoCKKS → repacking, with v1.4+ adding ConvertRLWEToCKKS/ConvertCKKSToRLWE for functional bootstrapping and EvalFastRotation to cheapen the linear-transform steps.
  • Switching is worth its cost mainly for discontinuous or sharply kinked functions on a modest number of elements; smooth, large-batch nonlinearities are almost always cheaper as CKKS polynomial approximations.
  • The dominant open problem is that slot extraction is sequential, so switching cost scales linearly with slot count — batched scheme switching is active research, not yet production-ready.
Chapter 27

FHE for Computer Vision, NLP, and Large Language Models

Learning Objectives
  • Trace the lineage of encrypted CNN inference from CryptoNets (2016) to modern encrypted ResNet-scale evaluation, and understand what changed to make it practical.
  • Identify the three transformer components that resist FHE (softmax, layer normalization, and deep sequential decoding) and the mitigation strategy used for each.
  • Give an honest, calibrated assessment of what is and is not currently feasible for encrypted LLM inference, distinguishing shipped capability from stated roadmap.
  • Design (at the architecture level) an encrypted transformer-based text classifier, choosing scheme, packing, and approximation strategy for each layer.

Applying FHE to modern deep learning inference is where the field's theoretical machinery meets its most demanding customer. Convolutional networks were the first serious target, because their operations — convolution, matrix multiply, pooling — are dominated by linear algebra with a small number of well-understood nonlinearities (ReLU, occasionally sigmoid). Transformers raise the bar considerably: attention's softmax, layer normalization's division and square root, and — for generative models — an autoregressive decoding loop whose depth grows with the very ciphertext levels FHE consumes fastest. This chapter surveys where the field stands, scheme by scheme and model class by model class, and is deliberately calibrated to distinguish shipped, benchmarked capability from research prototypes and roadmap claims.

27.1 Encrypted Convolutional Networks: CryptoNets to Today

CryptoNets (Gilad-Bachrach et al., 2016) was the first demonstration that a trained neural network — a small CNN on MNIST — could run end to end on homomorphically encrypted inputs, using the (then-standard) YASHE-family leveled scheme, replacing ReLU and max-pooling with square activations and sum-pooling to keep the whole network polynomial and low-depth. Certain It was slow by today's standards, though the usual one-line summary garbles which number is which: the paper reports roughly 250 seconds per forward pass, and that pass processes a packed batch of about 4,096 images at once — so the amortized cost is around 60 ms per image while the latency for any single image is the full 250 seconds. That gap between throughput and latency is a permanent feature of SIMD-packed FHE, not a quirk of 2016 hardware: if you need one answer quickly, packing buys you nothing and only worked because MNIST-scale CNNs are tiny and the square-activation substitution is tolerable at MNIST's easy accuracy ceiling, but it established the template every encrypted-CNN system since has followed: replace non-polynomial activations with polynomial approximations, pack the batch dimension (and often spatial dimensions) into ciphertext slots for SIMD throughput, and budget multiplicative depth against the number of convolutional layers.

The decade since has moved the frontier from MNIST-scale toy networks to CIFAR-10-scale (ResNet-20, ResNet-32 style architectures) and, in research settings, to ImageNet-scale (ResNet-50/VGG-16) encrypted inference, with the trend driven by three concurrent improvements: better CKKS bootstrapping (Part IV of this book — approximate bootstrapping made deep networks tractable without prohibitive noise-budget rationing), better packing strategies for convolutions (im2col-style repacking, and gap/stride-aware slot layouts that reduce the rotation count each convolution needs), and better polynomial approximations of ReLU (higher-degree, tighter-fit minimax polynomials, and, per Chapter 26, selective scheme switching for the layers most sensitive to the approximation). Representative latencies in the current literature run to low minutes per image for CIFAR-10-scale ResNets on GPU-accelerated CKKS implementations, and hours per image for ImageNet-scale ResNet/VGG evaluation. Likely These numbers move meaningfully year over year as bootstrapping and packing improve, and any specific figure should be treated as a snapshot rather than a hard ceiling.

27.2 Encrypted Transformers

A transformer block decomposes into: linear projections (query/key/value, the output projection, and the feed-forward network's two linear layers) — all of which FHE handles natively and efficiently via packed matrix multiplication; and three operations that do not:

27.2.1 Softmax in Attention

Attention computes $\text{softmax}(QK^T/\sqrt{d_k})V$, and softmax itself is $\text{softmax}(z)_i = e^{z_i} / \sum_j e^{z_j}$ — an exponential (approximable by a polynomial or by CKKS's native $\exp$ approximation used in bootstrapping, Chapter 17) divided by a data-dependent normalizing sum, and division under FHE is itself only approximable (via Newton–Raphson-style iterative reciprocal approximation, or by scheme switching to compute an exact reciprocal via PBS on a bounded-range LUT). The two dominant mitigation strategies in the encrypted-transformer literature are: (1) approximate softmax end to end with a smooth substitute function chosen specifically to be FHE-friendly — e.g., a low-degree polynomial or a $\text{softmax}$-like normalized power function that avoids the division's ill-conditioning — accepting a controlled accuracy loss; or (2) approximate the exponential polynomially but compute the normalizing division via a small number of Newton–Raphson iterations (each iteration is a fixed sequence of multiplications, so its depth cost is known and budgetable in advance, unlike a naive division). Likely

27.2.2 Layer Normalization

Layer norm computes $(x - \mu)/\sqrt{\sigma^2 + \epsilon}$ per token — a mean (cheap, linear), a variance (one multiplication), and an inverse square root, which like division is not polynomial and is instead handled by the same family of mitigations: a fixed number of Newton–Raphson iterations for $1/\sqrt{\cdot}$ (the classic "fast inverse square root" iteration translates directly into a fixed-depth FHE-friendly circuit), a polynomial approximation valid over the range activations are known (or clipped) to occupy, or — for architectures willing to trade a small accuracy hit for FHE-friendliness — substituting layer norm with a normalization variant that avoids the square root altogether (e.g., a scaled $L_1$-style normalization). Likely

27.2.3 Depth and Sequentiality in Generation

The hardest transformer-specific obstacle is not any single nonlinearity but the multiplicative-depth accounting of a full stack: each layer's attention and feed-forward sublayers consume levels, a model with a dozen or more layers accumulates depth requirements that force multiple CKKS bootstraps per forward pass, and autoregressive generation compounds this by requiring the entire deep, multi-bootstrap forward pass to run again for every generated token, with no way to parallelize across the sequential dependency between tokens. This combination — high per-layer depth and an inherently sequential outer loop over tokens — is what separates "an encrypted transformer layer is achievable" from "encrypted LLM inference is a deployed product," and is the central reason the honest state of the art restricts full encrypted evaluation to small models (BERT-tiny/DistilBERT-scale encoders, GPT-2-small-scale decoders) with architectural modifications, rather than to frontier-scale LLMs. Likely

27.3 The LLM Challenge, Stated Plainly

It is worth being direct about the gap between research demonstrations and what "encrypted LLM inference" means in marketing material. A single transformer layer's attention and feed-forward blocks, evaluated homomorphically with FHE-friendly softmax and layer-norm substitutes, is a solved and published capability at small-to-medium model scale. A full frontier LLM — tens of layers, tens of billions of parameters, generating tokens autoregressively — run entirely under FHE with no plaintext exposure at any point, at latency compatible with interactive use, is not a capability that exists in production today; it remains a research and roadmap target, not a shipped system. Verify

The research and industry activity closest to closing this gap includes:

  • CryptoLab and Niobium Systems partnership (announced October 2024). The two companies partnered specifically to pursue encrypted LLM/generative-AI inference by combining CryptoLab's HEaaN CKKS software stack with Niobium's dedicated FHE accelerator hardware, explicitly targeting the latency gap that makes software-only encrypted LLM inference impractical at present. Verify
  • Zama Concrete ML v1.8. Ships hybrid fine-tuning workflows for LLMs (keeping most of a model's weights in plaintext on a trusted server while encrypting only sensitive activations or a small adapter, rather than encrypting the entire model end to end) and a Low-Rank Approximation API aimed at reducing the multiplicative depth and ciphertext volume a fine-tuned adapter's FHE evaluation requires — a pragmatic middle ground between "no privacy" and "fully encrypted end-to-end LLM," and the shape most production encrypted-LLM offerings currently take. Verify
  • Zama's stated roadmap targets on the order of 100x FHE speedup overall. Keep two distinct numbers apart here, because they are routinely conflated: the widely-quoted "500–1000 by end of 2026" figure is confidential blockchain transactions per second (TPS) per chain for the Zama Protocol / fhEVM after GPU migration — it is not LLM tokens per second. For encrypted LLMs, Zama's published ambition is far more modest: on the order of dozens of encrypted tokens per second, and hardware-dependent at that. All of these are roadmap targets rather than shipped benchmarks and should be cited as such. Verify
HONEST Gap Assessment

Feasible today (small models, published/benchmarked): encrypted inference on BERT-tiny/DistilBERT-scale encoders and GPT-2-small-scale decoders, with FHE-friendly substitutes for softmax and layer norm, at latencies of seconds to low minutes per inference on accelerated hardware.

Feasible today (hybrid, production-shipped): LLM workflows that keep the bulk of a model in plaintext and encrypt only a targeted slice — inputs, a fine-tuning adapter, or specific sensitive activations — via hybrid architectures like Concrete ML's LLM fine-tuning tools. This is the pattern behind essentially every production "private LLM" offering as of this writing.

Aspirational (roadmap, not yet shipped): full end-to-end FHE inference of a frontier-scale LLM at interactive latency, with every weight, activation, and token generation step encrypted throughout. Vendor targets exist (e.g. Zama's 500–1000 confidential-blockchain TPS by end-2026 — a throughput figure for encrypted transactions, not LLM tokens) but should be treated as forward-looking statements, not present capability, until independently benchmarked and reproduced.

27.4 Other NLP Tasks Below LLM Scale

Several NLP tasks are considerably more tractable than full LLM inference and are worth distinguishing from the LLM problem specifically:

  • Encrypted text classification. A shallow classifier (logistic regression, a small feed-forward network, or even a single small transformer encoder) over a fixed-length embedding is squarely within the "CryptoNets successor" capability class described in §27.1–27.2 — this is the artifact designed in §27.5.
  • Encrypted embedding lookup. Converting a token or query into its embedding vector while keeping either the query or the embedding table private is a private-information-retrieval-flavored problem (Chapter 22-adjacent territory) rather than a deep-circuit-evaluation problem, and is comparatively cheap — a single homomorphic dot product against a one-hot (or PIR-style) encrypted selector, not a full forward pass.
  • Private information retrieval over document/passage indices. Encrypted semantic search — matching an encrypted query embedding against a plaintext or encrypted corpus of passage embeddings via encrypted dot products/cosine similarity — is a well-studied, comparatively lightweight application relative to full generative inference, since it requires only linear algebra (dot products) with no transformer stack in the loop at query time.
Artifact 27.A — Design Document: Encrypted 4-Layer Transformer Text Classifier

Goal. Classify a fixed-length input sequence (e.g., sentiment or topic classification over a padded/truncated sequence of $L=32$ tokens) using a 4-layer transformer encoder, entirely under encryption, client holding the only decryption key.

Scheme choice. CKKS as the primary scheme (this is a batched, real-valued, linear-algebra-dominated workload), with scheme switching to FHEW reserved for the layer-normalization inverse-square-root step only, per the cost analysis in Chapter 26 — softmax and the feed-forward activation will instead use CKKS-native polynomial approximation, since both are applied across the full $L \times d_{model}$ tensor and a per-element PBS cost would dominate latency.

Packing. Pack each token's $d_{model}$-dimensional embedding into contiguous CKKS slots, with the sequence dimension $L$ laid out across independent ciphertexts (one ciphertext per token) or across a second packing axis if $N/2$ slot budget allows — the choice trades rotation count in attention's $QK^T$ computation against the number of ciphertexts the client must manage; for $L=32$ and typical ring dimensions, one-ciphertext-per-token with $d_{model}$ packed per slot vector is the simpler starting design.

Per-layer plan:

  • QKV projections and output projection: packed matrix-vector products, CKKS native, ~1 multiplicative level each (plus relinearization).
  • $QK^T/\sqrt{d_k}$: packed dot products across token ciphertexts; requires $O(L)$ rotations per query token to align key vectors — the dominant rotation cost in the attention block.
  • Softmax: degree-8–12 polynomial approximation of $\exp$ (reusing the CKKS bootstrapping exponential approximator from Chapter 17) combined with 3–4 Newton–Raphson iterations for the reciprocal of the row sum; fixed depth, no scheme switching.
  • Attention output ($\cdot V$, output projection): packed matrix multiply, CKKS native.
  • Layer norm (×2 per block): mean and variance via packed sums (CKKS native); inverse square root via scheme switching to FHEW with an LUT covering the expected variance range, per Chapter 26 — chosen here specifically because layer norm's $1/\sqrt{\cdot}$ is the sharpest, most approximation-sensitive nonlinearity in the block and only $L$ scalar values (one per token) need the conversion, keeping the $O(n)$ per-slot PBS cost bounded.
  • Feed-forward (2 linear layers + activation): packed matrix multiplies; activation (GELU/ReLU) via CKKS polynomial approximation, consistent with §27.2.2's guidance for large-batch nonlinearities.
  • Classification head: pooled (mean or [CLS]-token) representation, one final packed linear layer to logits; argmax performed client-side after decryption, since it is the one step that legitimately requires plaintext (the classification label itself is the intended output).

Depth budget. Each of the 4 encoder blocks consumes roughly 6–10 multiplicative levels (projections + attention + FFN, excluding the scheme-switched layer-norm step, which resets its own noise). A single CKKS bootstrap between blocks 2 and 3 is the expected minimum; exact placement should be determined empirically against the concrete parameter set chosen (ring dimension, coefficient modulus chain) following the noise-budgeting methodology of Chapter 17.

Explicitly out of scope for this design: autoregressive generation (this is a single-forward-pass classifier, not a decoder), and any claim of ImageNet/frontier-LLM-scale throughput — the design targets the "feasible today" tier of §27.3's gap assessment.

Key Takeaways
  • Encrypted CNN inference has progressed from CryptoNets' MNIST-scale demonstration (2016) to CIFAR-10-scale in low minutes and ImageNet-scale in hours, driven by better bootstrapping, packing, and polynomial approximation.
  • Transformers add three obstacles beyond CNNs: softmax's exponential-plus-division, layer norm's inverse square root, and the compounding depth/sequentiality of autoregressive generation.
  • Small encoder/decoder models (BERT-tiny, GPT-2-small scale) with FHE-friendly substitutes are feasible and published today; full frontier-scale encrypted LLM inference is a roadmap target (Zama: 100x FHE speedup; 500–1000 confidential blockchain TPS by end-2026; dozens of encrypted LLM tokens/sec longer-term — three different metrics that are easy to conflate), not a currently shipped capability — treat vendor timelines as forward-looking statements pending independent benchmarking.
  • Hybrid architectures (Concrete ML LLM fine-tuning, encrypt-only-what's-sensitive) are the practical, production-shipped middle ground today, distinct from full end-to-end encrypted LLM inference.
Chapter 28

Hardware Acceleration, Standardization, and Open Problems

Learning Objectives
  • Survey the DARPA DPRIVE program and its participants' publicly reported results, and situate them against the program's "within 10x of unencrypted" goal.
  • Distinguish the hardware approaches — GPU acceleration, ASICs, and photonic computing — and the companies pursuing each.
  • Describe the standardization and conference landscape (HomomorphicEncryption.org, FHE.org, WAHC) that governs parameter selection and community practice.
  • Survey the commercial and blockchain deployment landscape and calibrate which announcements represent production deployments versus pilots or roadmap commitments.
  • Articulate the field's leading open problems with enough precision to evaluate a proposed research contribution against them.

FHE's central practical obstacle since Gentry's 2009 breakthrough has never been mathematical possibility — it has been cost. Bootstrapping, ciphertext expansion, and the sheer volume of modular arithmetic FHE circuits require have kept homomorphic computation multiple orders of magnitude slower than plaintext computation, which is precisely the gap hardware acceleration, standardization, and the open research agenda surveyed in this closing chapter are collectively trying to close. All figures and program claims in this chapter that concern named companies, dollar amounts, or dated announcements are flagged for verification against primary sources before being cited in any downstream work — this is a fast-moving commercial landscape and the specific numbers here are a snapshot as of this writing (mid-2026), not settled facts of the kind Parts I–IV establish. Verify

28.1 DARPA DPRIVE and the Race to 10x

DARPA's Data Protection in Virtual Environments (DPRIVE) program set an explicit target: FHE hardware acceleration bringing homomorphic computation within roughly 10x the latency of equivalent unencrypted computation — a threshold meant to make FHE practical for latency-sensitive production workloads rather than merely feasible in a batch/offline setting. Several DPRIVE performers have reported results:

  • Intel HERACLES. Intel's DPRIVE-funded FHE accelerator, presented at IEEE ISSCC (International Solid-State Circuits Conference) in February 2026 — ISSCC is held in February each year, reported speedups of roughly 1,074x to 5,547x over a 24-core Xeon baseline on FHE workloads, built around dedicated 32-bit processing datapaths purpose-designed for FHE's modular arithmetic rather than adapted from general-purpose CPU/GPU architectures. Verify
  • Duality TREBUCHET. A $14.5M DARPA-funded ASIC effort by Duality Technologies (the company behind OpenFHE), reported as completing Phase 2 of the DPRIVE program, with benchmarking performed directly against OpenFHE software baselines — a natural fit given Duality's stewardship of OpenFHE itself. Verify
  • SRI International CraterLake-lineage work, reported as pushing effective FHE processing width into the thousands of bits per operation, extending the CraterLake architecture line (originally an MIT/collaborators FHE ASIC design) toward DPRIVE's throughput targets. Verify

The pattern across DPRIVE performers is consistent with the broader thesis of this chapter: three-to-four-orders-of-magnitude speedups over CPU baselines are now demonstrated in silicon or near-silicon prototypes, not merely simulated, which is a materially different claim than the field could make even five years ago, though "within 10x of unencrypted" for arbitrary workloads remains an aspirational finish line rather than a general, achieved result — the reported speedups close much of the gap for the specific FHE-internal operations (NTT, modular multiplication, key-switching) these accelerators target, but end-to-end application latency depends on the full pipeline, not just the accelerated kernel.

28.2 Alternative Hardware Approaches

28.2.1 Photonic Computing — Optalysys

Optalysys pursues a structurally different approach: performing the number-theoretic transform (NTT) — the FFT-like operation at the computational core of every RLWE-based scheme's polynomial multiplication — optically, using laser interference patterns rather than digital arithmetic circuits, on the premise that optical computation can execute the NTT's massively parallel butterfly structure with far lower energy and latency than an equivalent digital ASIC. Optalysys closed a GBP 23 million Series A extension in January 2026, and has reported roughly a 90% latency reduction on targeted FHE operations, with partnerships spanning Duality/OpenFHE (integrating photonic NTT acceleration into the OpenFHE stack) and Google's HEIR compiler project (targeting photonic backends as an HEIR compilation target). Verify Photonic FHE acceleration remains earlier-stage than the DPRIVE ASIC efforts in terms of deployed scale, but represents a genuinely distinct point in the hardware design space — trading digital circuit power/latency for optical precision and calibration challenges.

28.2.2 ASICs — Niobium Microsystems and Cornami

Niobium Microsystems has raised $5.5M (May 2024) and a further $23M+ round (December 2025), and in February 2026 contracted SEMIFIVE (a Samsung Foundry design-solution partner) to develop its FHE accelerator ASIC on Samsung's 8nm 8LPU process, in a design-services deal reported at around KRW 10 billion. Read the tense carefully: this is a contract to advance the ASIC toward production, targeting what would be the first commercially manufacturable FHE ASIC. No commercially fabricated FHE ASIC exists yet — claiming one has been "fabricated" is precisely the kind of maturity inflation this chapter warns against, and the distinction between taped-out silicon, a design contract, and a shipping product is exactly what separates a procurement decision from a press release. Verify Niobium's hardware is also the accelerator side of the CryptoLab partnership for encrypted LLM inference discussed in Chapter 27.

Cornami closed a Series D round in July 2025 and reported roughly 52 employees, positioning its FHE-accelerated hardware specifically around encrypted AI inference workloads rather than general-purpose FHE acceleration. Verify

28.2.3 GPU and Vector-Instruction Acceleration

Below the ASIC tier, two acceleration paths are already integrated into mainstream software libraries rather than being separate hardware products: Intel HEXL, an open-source library of AVX-512-vectorized primitives (NTT, modular arithmetic) that plugs into OpenFHE, SEAL, and HElib and reports on the order of 2–5x speedups on compatible Intel CPUs simply by exploiting wide SIMD instructions already present in server-class x86 hardware; Certain and GPU-accelerated CKKS implementations such as FIDESlib (2025), which target CKKS's NTT and key-switching operations on GPU hardware, following a research line (alongside academic GPU-CKKS implementations from multiple groups) that treats commodity GPUs as a near-term acceleration path available well before custom FHE ASICs reach broad deployment. Likely A January 2025 collaboration among Cornell, Google, MIT, and Georgia Tech researchers explored a further path: running FHE workloads on existing AI-focused ASICs, specifically Google's TPUs, on the premise that FHE's dense modular-arithmetic matrix operations share enough structure with the matrix-multiply-heavy workloads TPUs were built for that meaningful acceleration is achievable without any FHE-specific silicon at all. Verify

28.3 Compilers

Hardware acceleration is only as useful as the software stack that targets it, and two compiler efforts are shaping how FHE circuits get from a high-level description to optimized, hardware-appropriate code: Google HEIR (Homomorphic Encryption Intermediate Representation), an MLIR-based compiler framework explicitly aimed at becoming a scheme-agnostic, industry-standard FHE compilation target — supporting multiple schemes (CKKS, BFV/BGV, and TFHE-style backends) behind a common IR, with a Python front end intended to let ML practitioners write ordinary-looking model code that HEIR lowers into scheme-appropriate, optimized FHE circuits, including targeting emerging hardware backends such as Optalysys's photonic NTT; Likely and Zama's Concrete compiler, whose v2.10 release added Rust language support and multiple-precision arithmetic handling, continuing Concrete's focus as the compilation layer beneath Concrete ML and TFHE-based circuit evaluation. Likely The broader trend both represent — compiling from familiar high-level languages (Python, Rust) down to FHE circuits, rather than requiring practitioners to hand-write scheme-specific homomorphic operation sequences — is likely to matter as much for FHE's practical adoption curve as any single hardware speedup, since compiler-level automatic optimization (operation scheduling, automatic bootstrapping placement, packing selection) directly attacks the engineering cost that has historically made FHE deployments expensive to build even once the cryptographic and hardware pieces are in place.

28.4 Standardization

HomomorphicEncryption.org, the community standardization body first convened in 2017, maintains the FHE Security Standard (currently v1.1) that defines recommended parameter sets and security-level methodology referenced throughout this book's parameter-selection discussions (Chapter 6 and Chapter 17 in particular) — essentially every production library's default parameter tables trace back to this standard's guidance. The body meets periodically to revise guidance as cryptanalysis of lattice problems advances; recent and upcoming meetings include the 7th meeting in Salt Lake City (October 2024), the 8th in Istanbul (March 2025), and the 9th scheduled for Seoul (March 2026). Verify

Two academic/industry conference venues round out the community's coordination points: WAHC (the Workshop on Encrypted Computing and Applied Homomorphic Cryptography), now in its 14th edition, co-located with ACM CCS on November 19 in The Hague, remains the longest-running dedicated venue for applied FHE research; and FHE.org's annual conference, now co-located with IACR events, held its 4th edition in Sofia (March 2025) and 5th in Taipei (March 2026), alongside a dedicated Hardware Day event in 2025 specifically tracking the DPRIVE-adjacent hardware acceleration landscape surveyed in §28.1–28.2. Verify

28.5 Commercial and Blockchain Deployment Landscape

A representative (not exhaustive) sample of the commercial deployment landscape as of this writing:

  • Duality Technologies partnered with Oracle to bring government/defense-focused FHE workloads to Oracle Cloud Infrastructure (October 2025), extending an existing Azure Marketplace presence dating to 2024. Verify
  • CryptoLab signed a 3-year genomics-focused contract with Macrogen, and its HEaaN stack has been paired with IBM HElayers and Samsung Smart SSD hardware in reported integrations. Verify
  • Enveil's ZeroReveal product line has raised $46.4M cumulatively, secured a Defense Innovation Unit (DIU) contract (May 2025), and received Gartner analyst recognition — positioning encrypted search/computation for government and financial-sector use cases. Verify
  • Zama's fhEVM — a fully homomorphic Ethereum Virtual Machine enabling confidential smart contracts — reached Ethereum mainnet in December 2025, with an associated $ZAMA token launch in January 2026. Verify
  • Fhenix, another FHE-blockchain project, acquired Sunscreen (announced 2 July 2026) and shipped a "Decomposable BFV" scheme variant (February 2026) aimed at making confidential smart-contract computation more efficient. Verify
  • Apple's Swift Homomorphic Encryption (apple/swift-homomorphic-encryption — that is its actual name; "swift-HE" will not find it) underpins private information retrieval features shipped on iOS 18 and later, a rare example of FHE-adjacent cryptography (PIR, closely related to but distinct from general-purpose FHE) deployed at true consumer scale. Likely
  • Mind Network, notable for a reported integration with ByteDance, represents a further blockchain-FHE entrant alongside Zama's fhEVM and Fhenix, applying FHE to confidential on-chain computation and cross-chain data privacy. Verify

Reading this landscape correctly requires distinguishing three tiers of maturity: production deployments with real users and transaction volume (fhEVM mainnet, Apple's PIR features), enterprise pilots and multi-year contracts with named customers (Duality-Oracle, CryptoLab-Macrogen, Enveil-DIU), and funding/roadmap announcements that signal investor confidence and direction but not yet deployed capability (most of the hardware-company figures in §28.1–28.2). All three tiers are legitimate signals of a maturing field, but they answer different questions, and conflating them — treating a funding round as evidence of a shipped product, or a research ASIC's reported speedup as an end-to-end application benchmark — is the most common calibration error in FHE industry commentary.

28.6 Open Problems

Six problems dominate the field's current research agenda, in roughly descending order of how directly they gate broader FHE adoption:

  1. Bootstrapping cost reduction. Despite a decade of progress (Part IV of this book traces CKKS bootstrapping specifically), bootstrapping remains the single most expensive operation in any FHE pipeline deep enough to need it, and every hardware and algorithmic advance surveyed in this chapter is, in large part, an attack on this one cost center.
  2. Encrypted training of deep networks. This book's applications chapters (16, 24, 27) focus overwhelmingly on encrypted inference; encrypted training — backpropagation through many layers, with gradient updates that must themselves remain encrypted across potentially thousands of iterations — multiplies the depth and iteration-count challenges of inference by orders of magnitude and remains largely open beyond small models and few-iteration fine-tuning (compare Concrete ML's hybrid fine-tuning approach, Chapter 27, which sidesteps full encrypted training rather than solving it).
  3. Batch scheme switching. As detailed in Chapter 26, the sequential, per-slot nature of current CKKS↔FHEW conversion is a hard ceiling on hybrid-scheme throughput; a genuinely SIMD-parallel scheme-switching primitive would materially change the cost calculus for encrypted transformer and CNN inference alike.
  4. FHE-friendly architectures. Rather than approximating existing plaintext-optimized architectures (ReLU, softmax, layer norm — all designed with no regard for FHE cost), a promising but underexplored direction is designing model architectures from the ground up around operations FHE evaluates cheaply and exactly (polynomial activations, division-free normalization), accepting that such architectures must still match plaintext accuracy to be adopted — a joint ML-architecture and cryptography design problem rather than a purely cryptographic one.
  5. FHE + MPC + ZKP composition. FHE, secure multi-party computation, and zero-knowledge proofs each cover different points on the trust/efficiency spectrum (FHE: no interaction, expensive computation; MPC: interaction required, often cheaper per-operation; ZKP: proves a computation was done correctly without revealing inputs, orthogonal to hiding the inputs during computation), and composing them — using MPC to accelerate the parts of a circuit FHE handles poorly, or ZKPs to let a verifier check an FHE evaluation was performed correctly without redoing it — is an active research seam with no settled best practice, particularly for verifying correct FHE evaluation in adversarial (not just honest-but-curious) settings.
  6. FHE compiler auto-optimization. Choosing packing layouts, bootstrapping placement, and rotation key sets currently requires substantial manual expertise (much of Parts III and IV of this book); closing the gap between hand-tuned expert circuits and what a compiler like HEIR or Concrete can produce automatically is both an engineering and a research problem, and is a precondition for FHE adoption by teams without dedicated cryptography expertise.
Artifact 28.A — Research Roadmap: Top 3 Open Problems
ProblemProposed ApproachFeasibility (2–3yr horizon)
1. Batch scheme switching Extend FHEW/TFHE blind rotation to process short vectors per bootstrap rather than single scalars, amortizing the bootstrapping-key application across multiple slots simultaneously; alternatively, restructure CKKS-to-FHEW slot extraction to batch multiple values into a single LWE-to-RLWE conversion pass rather than one-at-a-time extraction. Moderate. Builds directly on published blind-rotation research; the main risk is that batching may only amortize the bootstrapping-key lookup, not the per-value noise/precision tradeoff, limiting the achievable speedup. A 2-5x reduction in per-slot switching cost within this horizon is plausible; full SIMD parity with CKKS packing is not.
2. Bootstrapping cost reduction via hardware/software co-design Combine DPRIVE-lineage ASIC acceleration (dedicated NTT/modular-arithmetic datapaths) with algorithmic bootstrapping improvements (better modulus-chain scheduling, fewer bootstraps via smarter depth budgeting) rather than treating hardware and algorithm improvements as independent efforts. High. Already demonstrated in isolation (HERACLES's reported 1,074x-5,547x kernel speedups; CKKS bootstrapping's steady multi-year algorithmic improvement). The remaining work is integration — ensuring compiler-level (HEIR/Concrete) bootstrap placement decisions are hardware-aware — which is an engineering-heavy but low-cryptographic-risk problem.
3. Encrypted training beyond small-model fine-tuning Rather than pursuing fully encrypted backpropagation through deep networks directly, pursue hybrid training (Concrete ML-style: plaintext base model, encrypted low-rank adapter updates) as a stepping stone, while separately researching FHE-friendly optimizer variants (gradient approximations that tolerate CKKS's approximate arithmetic without diverging) for the eventual fully-encrypted case. Low-to-moderate for full training; high for hybrid/adapter-scale training. Hybrid approaches are already shipping (Chapter 27); genuinely full encrypted training of deep networks at production scale is unlikely to mature within this horizon given the compounding depth and iteration-count costs, and should be treated as a longer-term research target.
Key Takeaways
  • DARPA DPRIVE has driven demonstrated three-to-four-orders-of-magnitude FHE kernel speedups (Intel HERACLES, Duality TREBUCHET, SRI CraterLake lineage), closing much but not all of the "within 10x of unencrypted" gap for the specific operations accelerated.
  • Hardware approaches span digital ASICs (Niobium on Samsung 8nm, Cornami), photonic computing (Optalysys), and software-level vectorization already in production (Intel HEXL) and GPU acceleration (FIDESlib) — each occupying a different point in the maturity/deployment-scale space.
  • Compiler efforts (Google HEIR, Zama Concrete v2.10) are attacking the engineering-cost side of FHE adoption, which matters as much as raw hardware speedup for broad practitioner uptake.
  • Standardization (HomomorphicEncryption.org's Security Standard v1.1) and community venues (FHE.org, WAHC) continue to govern parameter selection and community practice, with regular meetings tracking cryptanalytic advances.
  • The commercial and blockchain landscape (Duality-Oracle, CryptoLab-Macrogen, Zama fhEVM, Fhenix, Enveil) spans production deployments, enterprise pilots, and roadmap announcements — distinguishing these tiers is essential to accurately assessing the field's maturity.
  • The leading open problems — bootstrapping cost, encrypted training, batch scheme switching, FHE-friendly architectures, FHE/MPC/ZKP composition, and compiler auto-optimization — collectively define the field's research agenda for the coming years.
Appendix A

Notation and Mathematical Prerequisites

This appendix collects the notation used throughout the book and summarizes the background material from probability, linear algebra, and complexity theory that the constructions in Parts I–V assume. It is a reference, not a tutorial — readers wanting a first introduction to any topic below should consult a dedicated text; readers who have seen this material before but want the specific conventions this book uses should read on.

A.1 Symbol Table

SymbolMeaning
$n$ (or $N$)Ring/lattice dimension; for RLWE-based schemes, the ring $R = \mathbb{Z}[X]/(X^N+1)$ has degree $N$, a power of two.
$q$Ciphertext modulus (coefficient modulus). Often a product of primes $q = \prod_i q_i$ under RNS/CRT representation.
$t$ (or $p$)Plaintext modulus, in exact schemes (BFV, BGV). Not used in CKKS, which encodes reals directly.
$R_q$The quotient ring $\mathbb{Z}_q[X]/(X^N+1)$ — the working ring for ciphertext coefficients.
$\chi$, $\chi_{err}$Error (noise) distribution, typically a discrete Gaussian with standard deviation $\sigma$, or a bounded uniform distribution in some implementations.
$s$, $sk$Secret key.
$pk$Public key.
$evk$, $rlk$Evaluation key / relinearization key, used to reduce ciphertext dimension after multiplication.
$ct$, $ct_0, ct_1$Ciphertext (and its component polynomials under the $(c_0, c_1)$ RLWE representation).
$\Delta$CKKS scaling factor, mapping real-valued plaintext into the integer domain used for ring arithmetic.
$L$, $\ell$Maximum ciphertext level (initial multiplicative depth budget) and current level after some number of rescalings/mod-switches.
$\lambda$Security parameter, conventionally measured in bits (e.g. $\lambda = 128$).
negl$(\lambda)$A negligible function of $\lambda$ — see Definition A.2 below.
$\lceil \cdot \rfloor$Rounding to nearest integer.
$\| \cdot \|_\infty$, $\|\cdot\|_2$Infinity norm and Euclidean norm, applied coefficient-wise to ring elements viewed as integer vectors.
$\langle a, b \rangle$Inner product.
$\text{PPT}$Probabilistic polynomial-time (algorithm/adversary).
$\mathcal{A}$An adversary (typically PPT) in a security game.
$\Pr[\cdot]$Probability of an event, typically over the randomness of key generation, encryption, and/or the adversary.

A.2 Probability Background

FHE security proofs and noise-growth analyses lean on a small, consistent set of probability tools: the discrete Gaussian distribution $D_{\mathbb{Z}, \sigma}$, which assigns probability proportional to $e^{-x^2/2\sigma^2}$ to each integer $x$ and is the standard error distribution for LWE/RLWE samples, chosen because Gaussian tails concentrate sharply (a discrete Gaussian with parameter $\sigma$ exceeds $t\sigma$ in absolute value with probability that falls off as $e^{-t^2/2}$, which is what makes noise-budget arguments — "the noise stays below the correctness threshold except with negligible probability" — go through); union bounds, used routinely to bound the probability that any of several noise terms (from a chain of homomorphic operations) exceeds its individual bound by summing the per-term failure probabilities; and the basic vocabulary of indistinguishability — two distributions $\mathcal{D}_0, \mathcal{D}_1$ are computationally indistinguishable if no PPT adversary can distinguish samples from one from samples from the other with more than negligible advantage, which is the semantic-security definition underlying every IND-CPA security proof in Part I.

A.3 Linear Algebra Over Finite Rings and Fields

Ring-LWE constructions require comfort with linear algebra over $\mathbb{Z}_q$ and its polynomial-ring extension $R_q$, rather than over $\mathbb{R}$ or $\mathbb{C}$: matrix-vector products, inner products, and norms are all computed with reduction modulo $q$ at every step, which means familiar real-linear-algebra intuitions (e.g., "small perturbations stay small") must be re-derived carefully, since reduction modulo $q$ can turn a small negative number into a value near $q$ if not handled with a centered (balanced) representative convention — this book consistently uses the centered representative $[-q/2, q/2)$ for modulus-$q$ values specifically to keep "small" meaning small in the ordinary sense throughout noise-growth arguments. The Chinese Remainder Theorem (CRT), both at the level of the plaintext space (batching/SIMD packing, Chapter 2 and Chapter 10) and at the level of the ciphertext modulus (RNS representation, splitting $q = \prod q_i$ into machine-word-sized primes for efficient implementation, Chapter 4), is the single most load-bearing piece of algebra in the entire book — readers should be comfortable with both uses before Part III.

A.4 Complexity Theory Essentials

Definition A.1 — PPT (Probabilistic Polynomial Time)

An algorithm is PPT if it runs in time polynomial in the length of its input (and any explicit security parameter $\lambda$) and has access to a source of random bits. Every honest party (key generator, encryptor, decryptor) and every adversary considered in this book's security definitions is implicitly restricted to PPT unless stated otherwise — FHE's security guarantees are computational, not information-theoretic, and hold only against attackers with bounded computational resources.

Definition A.2 — Negligible Function

A function $\text{negl}: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$ is negligible if for every polynomial $p(\lambda)$, there exists $\lambda_0$ such that for all $\lambda > \lambda_0$, $\text{negl}(\lambda) < 1/p(\lambda)$. Informally: it vanishes faster than the reciprocal of any polynomial. Security definitions throughout this book require an adversary's advantage in breaking a scheme to be bounded by a negligible function of the security parameter $\lambda$ — "negligible" is doing real mathematical work here, not just meaning "small."

Definition A.3 — Advantage and Security Games

An adversary's advantage in a security game (e.g., IND-CPA, semantic security) is typically defined as $\text{Adv}(\mathcal{A}) = |\Pr[\mathcal{A} \text{ wins}] - 1/2|$ for a game with a random bit the adversary must guess. A scheme is secure (with respect to that game) if $\text{Adv}(\mathcal{A})$ is negligible in $\lambda$ for every PPT $\mathcal{A}$. Reductions in Part I (e.g., "IND-CPA security of BFV reduces to the hardness of decision-RLWE") show that any adversary with non-negligible advantage against the scheme can be converted into an efficient algorithm with non-negligible advantage against the underlying hard problem — establishing security relative to that problem's assumed hardness, not in an absolute sense.

Readers new to these definitions should treat them as load-bearing throughout the security arguments in Chapters 3–6 and should resist the temptation to read "negligible" or "PPT" as loose qualifiers — every asymptotic claim in this book's security proofs depends on the precise definitions above.

Appendix B

Installation and Environment Setup

This appendix gives a practical, library-by-library setup reference for every FHE library used in this book's artifacts. Version numbers are pinned to what this book's code was written and tested against; treat any later version as likely compatible but unverified against these exact instructions, and always check each project's own release notes before upgrading a pinned dependency in a real project. Verify

B.1 OpenFHE (v1.5.1)

OpenFHE is a C++ library built with CMake, and building from source is the supported path. The dependency chain is modest (a C++17-capable compiler, CMake ≥ 3.5.1, and optionally OpenMP for multithreading), and the build is the route the project documents.

Note that the OpenFHE project publishes no official Docker image. Guides that suggest docker pull openfhe/openfhe-development are pointing at an image that does not exist — the openfhe Docker Hub namespace returns 404. If you want a container, write your own Dockerfile around the from-source build below, or use the community openfhe-configurator tooling from the project's GitHub organisation.

# From source (Linux/macOS)
git clone https://github.com/openfheorg/openfhe-development.git
cd openfhe-development && git checkout v1.5.1
mkdir build && cd build
cmake ..
make -j$(nproc)
sudo make install

# Hello-world verification: compile and run a trivial BFV example
# from openfhe-development/src/pke/examples/simple-integers.cpp
cd ../build/bin/examples/pke
./simple-integers
# Expect printed plaintext/ciphertext round-trip output with no errors.

Common build issues: stale CMake caches after switching branches/tags (delete build/ and reconfigure rather than reusing a cache across version changes); missing OpenMP on macOS (install via brew install libomp and ensure CMake finds it); and linker errors from mixing a system-installed OpenFHE with a locally built one — uninstall or isolate one before building the other, since OpenFHE does not namespace its installed libraries by version by default.

B.2 Microsoft SEAL (v4.4)

git clone https://github.com/microsoft/SEAL.git
cd SEAL && git checkout v4.4.0
# NOTE: SEAL_BUILD_EXAMPLES defaults to OFF, so the examples binary is NOT
# built unless you ask for it. Omit this flag and build/bin/sealexamples
# will simply not exist.
cmake -S . -B build -DSEAL_BUILD_EXAMPLES=ON \
                    -DSEAL_USE_INTEL_HEXL=ON   # HEXL acceleration is optional
cmake --build build
sudo cmake --install build

# Hello-world: run the native examples binary
cd build/bin && ./sealexamples
# Select the BFV or CKKS "basics" example from the printed menu.

SEAL's most common friction point is the optional Intel HEXL integration (Chapter 28) — enabling SEAL_USE_INTEL_HEXL requires HEXL to be built and discoverable first, and mismatched HEXL/SEAL version pairs fail at link time with unhelpful errors; when in doubt, build without HEXL first to confirm the base library works, then add acceleration.

B.3 TenSEAL (v0.3.16)

pip install tenseal==0.3.16

# Hello-world verification
python3 -c "
import tenseal as ts
ctx = ts.context(ts.SCHEME_TYPE.CKKS, poly_modulus_degree=8192,
                  coeff_mod_bit_sizes=[60, 40, 40, 60])
ctx.global_scale = 2**40
ctx.generate_galois_keys()
v = ts.ckks_vector(ctx, [1.0, 2.0, 3.0])
result = (v + v).decrypt()
print(result)   # expect approximately [2.0, 4.0, 6.0]
"

TenSEAL ships prebuilt wheels for common platforms (Linux, macOS, Windows on standard Python versions); if pip install falls back to a source build, ensure a working C++ toolchain and CMake are present, since TenSEAL wraps SEAL internally and inherits its build requirements.

B.4 Concrete ML (v1.8)

pip install concrete-ml==1.8

# Hello-world: train a small model and compile it for FHE
python3 -c "
from concrete.ml.sklearn import LogisticRegression
import numpy as np
X = np.random.rand(50, 4)
y = (X[:, 0] > 0.5).astype(int)
model = LogisticRegression(n_bits=8)
model.fit(X, y)
# compile() returns a concrete.fhe.Circuit and also arms the model itself.
# Note it does NOT return a wrapped model: calling .predict() on the return
# value raises AttributeError, because Circuit has no predict method.
# Keep calling predict() on the MODEL, selecting the execution mode there.
model.compile(X)
pred = model.predict(X[:1], fhe='execute')
print(pred)
"
