Concrete ML's compile step is the most resource-intensive part of the toolchain (it performs circuit compilation and key generation) and can take noticeably longer than the model-fit step itself, especially the first time a given circuit shape is compiled; this is expected, not a hang.

B.5 Lattigo (v6)

# Requires Go >= 1.21 (check current Lattigo go.mod for the exact floor)
#
# Module-aware Go (the default since 1.16, and mandatory at 1.21+) refuses to
# 'go get' or 'go run' outside a module: "go.mod file not found in current
# directory or any parent directory". Initialise one first.
mkdir -p lattigo-hello && cd lattigo-hello
go mod init example.com/lattigo-hello
go get github.com/tuneinsight/lattigo/v6

# Hello-world: minimal CKKS round-trip (adjust import paths per current v6 layout)
cat > main.go << 'EOF'
package main

import (
    "fmt"
    "github.com/tuneinsight/lattigo/v6/core/rlwe"
    "github.com/tuneinsight/lattigo/v6/schemes/ckks"
)

func main() {
    params, _ := ckks.NewParametersFromLiteral(ckks.ParametersLiteral{
        LogN:            13,
        LogQ:            []int{45, 35, 35},
        LogP:            []int{45},
        LogDefaultScale: 35,
    })
    kgen := rlwe.NewKeyGenerator(params)
    sk := kgen.GenSecretKeyNew()
    fmt.Println("Lattigo CKKS params + key generated OK:", params.LogN(), sk != nil)
}
EOF
go run main.go

Lattigo's package layout has shifted across major versions (v4 → v5 → v6 reorganized import paths, notably splitting scheme-specific code under schemes/); code samples from older tutorials frequently fail to compile against v6 purely due to import-path drift rather than any semantic change — check the current go.mod and package structure before assuming a compile error reflects a real bug. Verify

B.6 HEaaN

HEaaN, CryptoLab's CKKS implementation, is distributed with more restricted public access than the fully open-source libraries above (portions are available as a Python binding for research/evaluation use, with the full performance-tuned stack available under commercial/research agreements); readers wanting to experiment with HEaaN specifically should consult CryptoLab's current distribution terms and installation documentation directly rather than relying on generic pip/build instructions, since availability and packaging have changed over time. Verify

General Troubleshooting Checklist
  • Version-pin everything. FHE library APIs are less stable across major versions than most cryptography libraries, precisely because the field itself is still moving quickly (Chapter 28); a working example against one minor version can fail to compile against the next.
  • When a build fails with an unhelpful linker error, suspect a version mismatch between the FHE library and an optional acceleration dependency (Intel HEXL, a specific BLAS) before suspecting your own code.
  • Docker images (where available — OpenFHE, several TenSEAL/Concrete ML tutorials) are the fastest path to a known-good baseline environment and the recommended starting point before attempting a from-source build.
Appendix C

Complete Code for All Artifacts

Every chapter in this book includes at least one artifact — a worked code example, design document, or protocol skeleton — set off in an "Artifact" box. This appendix is an index to those artifacts, organized by part, with a note on how each is intended to be built and run, and a reminder of the version-pinning discipline established in Appendix B.

C.1 Index by Part

PartChaptersArtifact Character
Part I — FoundationsCh 1–7Mathematical worked examples; first LWE/RLWE toy encryption in Python for intuition-building, not production use.
Part II — Core SchemesCh 8–13Scheme-by-scheme minimal working examples (BFV, BGV, CKKS, TFHE) in SEAL/OpenFHE/TenSEAL, each demonstrating encrypt-compute-decrypt for a single characteristic operation of that scheme.
Part III — EngineeringCh 14–21 (approx.)Performance-oriented artifacts: packing strategies, rotation-key management, RNS/CRT parameter selection, noise budgeting utilities.
Part IV — ApplicationsCh 22–24 (approx.)End-to-end application skeletons: encrypted inference pipelines, private set intersection, encrypted database queries.
Part V — Advanced TopicsCh 25–28Multiparty protocol skeleton (Ch 25), hybrid scheme-switching comparison (Ch 26), transformer design document (Ch 27), research roadmap table (Ch 28).

C.2 Build and Run Conventions Used Throughout

Artifacts in this book follow three consistent conventions, established here so each chapter did not need to repeat them:

  • SKELETON labeling. Any C++ artifact marked SKELETON is illustrative of API shape and control flow, not a tested, drop-in program — variable initialization, error handling, and some parameter values are elided for readability. Before running a SKELETON artifact, cross-check its function signatures against the pinned library version's current example directory (each library ships its own examples/ tree, which is the more reliable source of truth than any single book excerpt, given the pace of API change documented throughout Part V).
  • Python artifacts (TenSEAL, Concrete ML) are generally complete and runnable as shown, modulo the environment setup in Appendix B, since these libraries' Python bindings are more stable across versions than OpenFHE's C++ surface.
  • Design-document artifacts (e.g., Artifact 27.A) are intentionally not code — they specify an architecture, scheme choice, and depth budget precisely enough to be implemented, in recognition that some of this book's subject matter (full encrypted-LLM-scale system design) is not yet reducible to a runnable, book-length code listing without either trivializing the model or requiring hardware this book cannot assume the reader has access to.

C.3 Version-Pinning Note

Every artifact in this book was written and (where marked runnable rather than SKELETON) tested against the specific library versions enumerated in Appendix B: OpenFHE v1.5.1, SEAL v4.4, TenSEAL v0.3.16, Concrete ML v1.8, and Lattigo v6. FHE libraries have historically changed their APIs across major and even minor versions more readily than most cryptographic software, precisely because the underlying research (bootstrapping techniques, scheme-switching primitives, multiparty protocols — the subject of this Part) continues to move quickly, as documented throughout Chapters 25–28. Readers building on these artifacts in a real project should: pin dependency versions explicitly (a requirements.txt with exact versions for Python libraries; a checked-out git tag rather than a floating branch for C++ libraries built from source); re-verify any artifact's function signatures against that pinned version's own documentation before relying on it in anything beyond a learning exercise; and treat every dated, company-specific, or roadmap claim in Chapters 25–28 specifically — flagged throughout with Verify tags — as a snapshot of a fast-moving commercial and research landscape as of this writing, not a permanent fact, and re-confirm any such claim against primary sources before citing it.

Key Takeaways
  • All book artifacts are indexed here by part; SKELETON-labeled C++ examples illustrate API shape and require cross-checking against the pinned library version's own examples before use.
  • Python-based artifacts (TenSEAL, Concrete ML) are the most directly runnable, given greater binding stability across versions.
  • Version pinning (Appendix B's exact versions) is not a formality — it is the difference between an artifact compiling and an artifact failing on an unrelated API rename, given this field's pace of change.
Appendix D

Sources and Further Reading

This appendix collects the primary sources a reader should go to next, with an honest note on what each is good for. Where this book and a source disagree, the source wins — several corrections in the current revision came from exactly that comparison.

D.1 Foundational papers and theses

  • Craig Gentry, A Fully Homomorphic Encryption Scheme, Ph.D. dissertation, Stanford University, September 2009 (advisor: Dan Boneh). 209 pages; a preliminary version appeared as "Fully Homomorphic Encryption Using Ideal Lattices," STOC 2009. crypto.stanford.edu/craig/craig-thesis.pdf.
    The founding document, and still the clearest statement of the blueprint: bootstrappability (Definition 4.1.3 — the decryption circuit augmented by one gate must lie inside the evaluable family), squashing, and the recursive self-reference that turns a somewhat-homomorphic scheme into a fully homomorphic one. Read Chapters 1–4 even if you skip the ideal-lattice algebra in the middle. Note the point §6.4.2 of this book now makes explicitly: bootstrappability alone yields leveled FHE with no circular-security assumption; circular security buys unbounded depth with a single compact key.
  • Boaz Barak, "Fully Homomorphic Encryption: Introduction and Bootstrapping", chapter of An Intensive Introduction to Cryptography (Harvard CS 127 lecture notes). files.boazbarak.org/crypto/lec_15_FHE.pdf.
    The best short, rigorous treatment of the definitions. Barak is more careful than most textbooks about compactness (Eval's output must be bounded by a fixed polynomial in the security parameter alone — independent of both the number of inputs and the size of the evaluated function), about the distinction between freshly-encrypted and merely-decryptable ciphertexts, and about the precise hypotheses of the bootstrapping theorem. Also the place to understand why no homomorphic scheme can be CCA2-secure: malleability is the feature.

D.2 Textbooks and courses

  • Ronny Ko, The Beginner's Textbook for Fully Homomorphic Encryption, arXiv:2503.05136 (first posted March 2025; actively revised). arxiv.org/abs/2503.05136; LaTeX source and a companion Python demo library at github.com/fhetextbook.
    The closest work to this book, and the best complement to it. Its organising device is GLWE — a single ciphertext form $(A_0,\dots,A_{k-1},B)$ with $B = \sum A_i S_i + \Delta M + E$, of which LWE is the case $n=1$ and RLWE the case $k=1$ — from which GLev, GGSW, key switching, relinearization and blind rotation are all derived uniformly. If the per-scheme presentation in Part II ever feels like four unrelated constructions, read Ko's Parts B and C to see them as one. It is also unusually explicit that both encryption sign conventions are in use ($B = +\sum A_iS_i + \Delta M + E$ versus $B = -\sum A_iS_i + \Delta M + E$), proves their equivalence, and then uses the second throughout its scheme chapters — worth knowing before you compare formulas across papers and conclude someone has a sign error.
  • MIT 6.S976 / 18.S996, Cryptography and Machine Learning: Foundations and Frontiers, Spring 2026, taught by Shafi Goldwasser and Vinod Vaikuntanathan. mlcrypto.mit.edu/course/.
    A graduate treatment of exactly the intersection Part IV of this book occupies, from two people who built much of the underlying theory (Vaikuntanathan is a co-author of BGV and BFV). Use the syllabus as a reading list for the research frontier beyond encrypted inference.
  • Jeremy Kun, "A High-Level Technical Overview of Fully Homomorphic Encryption" (May 2024). jeremykun.com/2024/05/04/fhe-overview/.
    The best practitioner-oriented orientation to the field, by an engineer on Google's HEIR compiler project. Strong on the CGGI-versus-CKKS trade-off, programmable bootstrapping, and the compiler/hardware landscape that Chapters 16 and 27 survey.

D.3 Industry and ecosystem

  • Duality Technologies, FHE glossary and resources. dualitytech.com/glossary/fully-homomorphic-encryption/. Duality stewards OpenFHE and was co-founded by Shafi Goldwasser, Vinod Vaikuntanathan and Kurt Rohloff; useful for how the technology is framed commercially, and for the DARPA DPRIVE hardware work discussed in Chapter 27.
  • IBM Research, Fully Homomorphic Encryption. research.ibm.com/topics/fully-homomorphic-encryption. The home of HElib, the original BGV implementation and still the reference for BGV bootstrapping and general-purpose batching techniques.
  • Zama, introduction to homomorphic encryption. zama.org/introduction-to-homomorphic-encryption. The TFHE-centric view of the world, and the entry point to Concrete, Concrete ML and TFHE-rs (Chapters 11, 24).
  • FHERMA, the FHE component challenge platform run by Fair Math with the OpenFHE community. fherma.io; challenge repository github.com/fairmath/fherma-challenges; resulting component library polycircuit. See Chapter 17 §5.
  • HomomorphicEncryption.org Security Standard — the parameter tables every deployment should be checked against, and the source of the dimension/modulus limits quoted throughout Chapter 14.
How to use these
  • For definitions and theorem statements: Barak first, then Gentry's thesis for the original framing.
  • For scheme mechanics and derivations: Ko's textbook, read alongside Part II of this book.
  • For what to actually type: the library documentation, always — API surfaces move faster than any book, which is why every library-level claim here carries a verification note.
  • For research directions: the MIT course reading list, and the FHERMA challenge archive for open engineering problems with measurable scoring.
